                if together >= FABRIC_AFTER && self.blamed.insert(link) {
                    println!(
                        "{:4}s  {together} machines on {link} flapped together: the link is at fault, no machine is drained",
                        s.at
                    );
                }
            }
        }
    }
}

impl Plane {
    /// Cordons a Healthy machine. A machine already under repair stays where
    /// it is.
    fn condemn(&mut self, now: u32, index: usize, why: &str) {
        if self.records[index].state == State::Healthy {
            self.records[index].step = 0;
            self.transition(now, index, State::Cordoned, why);
        }
    }
}

impl Plane {
    /// Counts the machines that hold a slot of the disruption budget: every
    /// machine that is draining, under repair, in burn-in, or quarantined.
    fn out_of_service(&self) -> usize {
        self.records
            .iter()
            .filter(|r| r.state >= State::Draining)
            .count()
    }
}

impl Plane {
    /// Advances every machine by one second. A Cordoned machine starts to
    /// drain when the budget has a free slot. The other states each last a
    /// fixed time and then move on.
    fn step(&mut self, now: u32) {
        for index in 0..self.records.len() {
            let (state, step, name) = {
                let r = &self.records[index];
                (r.state, r.step, r.node.name)
            };
            let waited = now - self.records[index].since;

            match state {
                State::Cordoned if self.out_of_service() < BUDGET => {
                    self.transition(now, index, State::Draining, "jobs told to checkpoint");
                }
                State::Cordoned if waited == 1 => {
                    println!(
                        "{now:4}s  {name:<10} blocked: {BUDGET} machines are already out of service"
                    );
                }
                State::Draining if waited >= DRAIN_TIME => {
                    self.transition(now, index, State::Remediating, LADDER[step]);
                }
                State::Remediating if waited >= REPAIR_TIME => {
                    let why = format!("burn-in after {}", LADDER[step]);
                    self.transition(now, index, State::Validating, &why);
                }
                State::Validating if waited >= BURN_IN_TIME => self.finish_burn_in(now, index),
                _ => {}
            }
        }
    }

    /// Acts on the result of a burn-in test. A pass returns the machine to
    /// service. A failure sends it back for the next action on the ladder, and
    /// a machine with no actions left is quarantined.
    fn finish_burn_in(&mut self, now: u32, index: usize) {
        let name = self.records[index].node.name;
        let failures = self.burn_in.entry(name).or_default();

        if *failures == 0 {
            self.records[index].fault_jobs.clear();
            self.transition(now, index, State::Healthy, "burn-in passed");

            return;
        }

        *failures -= 1;

        if self.records[index].step + 1 == LADDER.len() {
            let why = "burn-in failed after every action; ticket opened";
            self.transition(now, index, State::Quarantined, why);

            return;
        }

        self.records[index].step += 1;
        let why = format!("burn-in failed; {}", LADDER[self.records[index].step]);
        self.transition(now, index, State::Remediating, &why);
    }
}

/// The run: the signals in the order they arrive.
const SCRIPT: [Signal; 11] = [
    // One job faults on three different Thors.
    Signal {
        at: 5,
        node: "thor-1",
        job: "finetune-7b",
        kind: Kind::AppFault,
    },
    Signal {
        at: 9,
        node: "thor-2",
        job: "finetune-7b",
        kind: Kind::AppFault,
    },
    Signal {
        at: 14,
        node: "thor-3",
        job: "finetune-7b",
        kind: Kind::AppFault,
    },
    // A memory error on a Spark, which one GPU reset repairs.
    Signal {
        at: 20,
        node: "spark-1",
        job: "",
        kind: Kind::MemoryEcc,
    },
    // A Thor whose GPU stops answering, and which no action repairs.
    Signal {
        at: 40,
        node: "thor-4",
        job: "",
        kind: Kind::OffBus,
    },
    // The 25 GbE switch restarts: four Thors lose their link together.
    Signal {
        at: 60,
        node: "thor-1",
        job: "",
        kind: Kind::LinkFlap,
    },
    Signal {
        at: 61,
        node: "thor-2",
        job: "",
        kind: Kind::LinkFlap,
    },
    Signal {
        at: 61,
        node: "thor-3",
        job: "",
        kind: Kind::LinkFlap,
    },
    Signal {
        at: 62,
        node: "thor-5",
        job: "",
        kind: Kind::LinkFlap,
    },
    // Two machines fail in the same second while thor-4 is quarantined.
    Signal {
        at: 80,
        node: "spark-2",
        job: "",
        kind: Kind::OffBus,
    },
    Signal {
        at: 80,
        node: "orin-nx-1",
        job: "",
        kind: Kind::MemoryEcc,
    },
];

fn main() {
    // thor-4 fails every burn-in; every other machine passes its first.
    let mut plane = Plane::new(BTreeMap::from([("thor-4", LADDER.len())]));
    let mut signals = SCRIPT.iter().peekable();

    for now in 0..=DURATION {
        while let Some(signal) = signals.next_if(|s| s.at == now) {
            plane.observe(signal);
        }

        plane.step(now);
    }

    println!("final states:");

    for r in &plane.records {
        println!("  {:<10} {:<16} {}", r.node.name, r.node.model, r.state);
    }
}

Listing 4.23 The complete program in Go. code/go/fencing/main.go

// Command fencing shows why a checkpoint store has to check who is writing.
//
// A training job runs on thor-1. The Thor loses its network link and keeps
// training. The control plane sees silence, declares thor-1 dead, and starts
// the job again on thor-2 from the last checkpoint. For a while two copies of
// the job run, and both write checkpoints to the same store.
//
// The program plays that sequence twice: against a store that accepts every
// write, and against a store that remembers the highest epoch it has seen
// and refuses writes that carry an older one.
//
// Run it from code/go with:
//
//	go run ./fencing
package main

import (
	"errors"
	"fmt"
)

// Checkpoint is what a worker saves: the training step it reached and the
// epoch of the assignment under which it was running.
type Checkpoint struct {
	// Step is the training step the checkpoint holds.
	Step int
	// Epoch is the assignment number the writer was given.
	Epoch int
	// Writer is the host that wrote the checkpoint.
	Writer string
}

// ErrFenced is returned to a writer whose epoch is older than one the store
// has already accepted.
var ErrFenced = errors.New("fenced: a newer assignment has written")

// Store holds the latest checkpoint of one job.
type Store struct {
	// Fencing turns the epoch check on.
	Fencing bool
	// latest is the stored checkpoint.
	latest Checkpoint
}

// Write stores a checkpoint. With fencing on, a checkpoint whose epoch is
// lower than the stored one is refused.
func (s *Store) Write(c Checkpoint) error {
	if s.Fencing && c.Epoch < s.latest.Epoch {
		return ErrFenced
	}

	s.latest = c

	return nil
}


// ControlPlane hands out assignments of one job. Each assignment gets the
// next epoch, so a later assignment always carries a higher number.
type ControlPlane struct {
	epoch int
}

// Assign gives the job to a host and returns the epoch of the assignment.
func (c *ControlPlane) Assign(host string) int {
	c.epoch++
	fmt.Printf("  control plane: job assigned to %s with epoch %d\n", host, c.epoch)

	return c.epoch
}


// save writes one checkpoint and prints what the store answered.
func save(s *Store, host string, epoch, step int) {
	err := s.Write(Checkpoint{Step: step, Epoch: epoch, Writer: host})

	if err != nil {
		fmt.Printf("  %s writes step %d with epoch %d: refused, %v\n", host, step, epoch, err)

		return
	}

	fmt.Printf("  %s writes step %d with epoch %d: stored\n", host, step, epoch)
}

// run plays the sequence against one store.
func run(s *Store) {
	var plane ControlPlane
	first := plane.Assign("thor-1")
	save(s, "thor-1", first, 1000)
	fmt.Println("  thor-1 loses its link and keeps training; its Lease expires")
	second := plane.Assign("thor-2")
	fmt.Printf("  thor-2 resumes from step %d\n", s.latest.Step)
	save(s, "thor-2", second, 2000)
	fmt.Println("  thor-1's link returns; it still holds epoch 1")
	save(s, "thor-1", first, 1500)
	fmt.Printf("  a restart now resumes from step %d, written by %s\n", s.latest.Step, s.latest.Writer)
}

func main() {
	fmt.Println("a store that accepts every write:")
	run(&Store{})
	fmt.Println("a store that checks the epoch:")
	run(&Store{Fencing: true})
}

Listing 4.24 The complete program in Rust. code/rust/fencing/src/main.rs

//! Shows why a checkpoint store has to check who is writing.
//!
//! A training job runs on thor-1. The Thor loses its network link and keeps
//! training. The control plane sees silence, declares thor-1 dead, and starts
//! the job again on thor-2 from the last checkpoint. For a while two copies of
//! the job run, and both write checkpoints to the same store.
//!
//! The program plays that sequence twice: against a store that accepts every
//! write, and against a store that remembers the highest epoch it has seen
//! and refuses writes that carry an older one.
//!
//! Run it from code/rust with:
//!
//! ```text
//! cargo run -p fencing
//! ```

use std::fmt;

/// What a worker saves: the training step it reached and the epoch of the
/// assignment under which it was running.
#[derive(Clone, Copy, Default)]
struct Checkpoint {
    /// The training step the checkpoint holds.
    step: u32,
    /// The assignment number the writer was given.
    epoch: u32,
    /// The host that wrote the checkpoint.
    writer: &'static str,
}

/// Returned to a writer whose epoch is older than one the store has already
/// accepted.
struct Fenced;

impl fmt::Display for Fenced {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut fmt::Formatter<'_>) -> fmt::Result {
        f.write_str("fenced: a newer assignment has written")
    }
}

/// Holds the latest checkpoint of one job.
#[derive(Default)]
struct Store {
    /// Turns the epoch check on.
    fencing: bool,
    /// The stored checkpoint.
    latest: Checkpoint,
}

impl Store {
    /// Stores a checkpoint. With fencing on, a checkpoint whose epoch is lower
    /// than the stored one is refused.
    fn write(&mut self, c: Checkpoint) -> Result<(), Fenced> {
        if self.fencing && c.epoch < self.latest.epoch {
            return Err(Fenced);
        }

        self.latest = c;

        Ok(())
    }
}

/// Hands out assignments of one job. Each assignment gets the next epoch, so a
/// later assignment always carries a higher number.
#[derive(Default)]
struct ControlPlane {
    epoch: u32,
}

impl ControlPlane {
    /// Gives the job to a host and returns the epoch of the assignment.
    fn assign(&mut self, host: &str) -> u32 {
        self.epoch += 1;
        println!(
            "  control plane: job assigned to {host} with epoch {}",
            self.epoch
        );

        self.epoch
    }
}

/// Writes one checkpoint and prints what the store answered.
fn save(store: &mut Store, writer: &'static str, epoch: u32, step: u32) {
    match store.write(Checkpoint {
        step,
        epoch,
        writer,
    }) {
        Ok(()) => println!("  {writer} writes step {step} with epoch {epoch}: stored"),
        Err(e) => println!("  {writer} writes step {step} with epoch {epoch}: refused, {e}"),
    }
}

/// Plays the sequence against one store.
fn run(mut store: Store) {
    let mut plane = ControlPlane::default();
    let first = plane.assign("thor-1");
    save(&mut store, "thor-1", first, 1000);
    println!("  thor-1 loses its link and keeps training; its Lease expires");
    let second = plane.assign("thor-2");
    println!("  thor-2 resumes from step {}", store.latest.step);
    save(&mut store, "thor-2", second, 2000);
    println!("  thor-1's link returns; it still holds epoch 1");
    save(&mut store, "thor-1", first, 1500);
    println!(
        "  a restart now resumes from step {}, written by {}",
        store.latest.step, store.latest.writer
    );
}

fn main() {
    println!("a store that accepts every write:");
    run(Store::default());
    println!("a store that checks the epoch:");
    run(Store {
        fencing: true,
        ..Store::default()
    });
}

Summary

  • A failure report can come from a faulty job, a faulty machine, or a faulty link. Counting reports per job, per machine, and per link separates the three.
  • The device plugin stops new work on a broken GPU within seconds. A health service and a remediation controller decide, more slowly, what happens to the machine.
  • A machine under repair is cordoned, drained with time for its jobs to checkpoint, repaired one rung at a time, and validated with a burn-in before it returns.
  • A disruption budget limits the number of machines the repair system may take out of service at once.
  • An epoch that rises with each assignment, checked by the checkpoint store, keeps a replaced copy of a job from overwriting newer progress.

References

  • NVIDIA, Xid errors: https://docs.nvidia.com/deploy/xid-errors/
  • NVIDIA, DGX Spark hardware overview: https://docs.nvidia.com/dgx/dgx-spark/hardware.html
  • NVIDIA, Jetson modules technical specifications: https://developer.nvidia.com/embedded/jetson-modules
  • Kubernetes, Safely drain a node: https://kubernetes.io/docs/tasks/administer-cluster/safely-drain-node/
  • Kubernetes, API-initiated eviction: https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/scheduling-eviction/api-eviction/
  • Kubernetes, Device plugins: https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/extend-kubernetes/compute-storage-net/device-plugins/
  • M. Kleppmann, “How to do distributed locking”, 2016: https://martin.kleppmann.com/2016/02/08/how-to-do-distributed-locking.html

5. Kubernetes in a new cloud account

So far our cluster has existed before each chapter began. In this chapter, we will look at where a cluster comes from. We start with an account at a provider of machines, which holds nothing, and end with a set of machines that the API server knows as nodes and the scheduler can place GPU pods on.

Two separate things have to happen. Something has to ask the provider for machines and keep asking until the right number exist, and each machine, once it boots, has to prove to the cluster that it is allowed to join. We will take the second first, because we can watch it on the cluster we already have. Then we will write provisioner, in Go and in Rust, a model of the controller that asks for machines, and use it to study the one failure that makes this job harder than it looks: a request that succeeds while its reply is lost.

This chapter covers

  • what an empty account offers: a create call, a quota, and nothing else
  • how a machine joins a cluster: a bootstrap token, a signed certificate, a Node object
  • the labels and capacity a node reports, read from our own cluster
  • a controller that fills pools of machines, and what a lost reply does to it
  • client tokens, which make a create call safe to send twice
  • how Cluster API expresses the same design as Kubernetes objects

What the account gives us

A provider of machines, whether a public cloud or the team that racks hardware in a company’s own data center, offers the same small interface. We can ask for a machine of a given model, list the machines we have, and give one back. The account also has a quota for each model: the largest number of machines of that model it may hold at once.

Our fleet from chapter 4 fits this picture exactly, and its quotas are physical. There are five Jetson AGX Thors on the shelf and two DGX Sparks on the desk. A request for a sixth Thor fails for the same reason a request for a sixth GPU instance fails in a cloud account whose quota is five: the provider has no more to give us.

A new machine is a computer with an operating system and a network address. It knows nothing about our cluster, and our cluster knows nothing about it.

How a machine becomes a node

A machine becomes a node when the kubelet on it registers with the API server. From chapter 1 we know that every request to the API server is an HTTP request, and the API server requires each one to identify its sender. A kubelet identifies itself with a client certificate: a file that states a name, here the node’s name, and carries a signature from the cluster’s certificate authority (CA), a key pair the cluster created for itself when it was first set up. The API server trusts any certificate that its CA signed.

A new machine has no such certificate, and it cannot be handed the CA’s private key, since any holder of that key can impersonate every node in the cluster. The machine is given something much weaker instead, and trades it for a certificate. Figure 5.1 shows the exchange.

The weaker thing is a bootstrap token: a short string that the API server accepts for a limited time and for one purpose. Our kind cluster was created with kubeadm, the standard tool for setting up a cluster, and kubeadm can print the command a new machine would run to join. We have cut the two secret values from the output:

$ docker exec gpu-lab-control-plane kubeadm token create --ttl 10m --print-join-command
kubeadm join gpu-lab-control-plane:6443 --token <token> --discovery-token-ca-cert-hash sha256:<hash>

The command carries three facts, and each one answers a question the new machine has.

  • gpu-lab-control-plane:6443 is where the API server listens.
  • The token is what the machine will show to prove it was invited. We asked for it to expire after ten minutes.
  • The hash is a fingerprint of the cluster’s CA public key. The machine uses it in the other direction: before it sends the token anywhere, it checks that the server it reached presents a certificate signed by the CA with this fingerprint. A machine without the hash could be tricked into sending its token to an impostor.

With the token accepted, the kubelet generates a key pair of its own and submits a CertificateSigningRequest, a Kubernetes object that says “please sign a certificate that names me system:node:thor-3”. The token is permitted to do only this. A controller in the control plane checks that the request comes from a bootstrap token and asks for a node identity, approves it, and signs it with the CA. The kubelet downloads the certificate and from then on uses it for every request. The private key was generated on the machine and has stayed there.

With its certificate, the kubelet creates the two objects we already know: a Node object, and the Lease from chapter 3 that it will renew every ten seconds.

What a node reports about itself

The Node object is where the kubelet tells the scheduler what the machine has. Here is the capacity our Orin NX reports, with the huge-page lines removed:

$ kubectl get node gpu-lab-control-plane -o jsonpath='{.status.capacity}'
{"cpu":"8","ephemeral-storage":"1966327652352","memory":"16031232Ki","nvidia.com/gpu":"1","pods":"110"}

Eight CPU cores, 16,031,232 KiB of memory, which is the board’s 15.3 GiB, and one nvidia.com/gpu, reported by the device plugin from Appendix A. The scheduler subtracts what running pods have requested from these numbers to find out what is free.

The Node object also carries labels: pairs of a key and a value that describe the machine.

$ kubectl get node gpu-lab-control-plane -o jsonpath='{.metadata.labels}'
{"beta.kubernetes.io/arch":"arm64","beta.kubernetes.io/os":"linux","kubernetes.io/arch":"arm64",
"kubernetes.io/hostname":"gpu-lab-control-plane","kubernetes.io/os":"linux",
"node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane":""}

The kubelet sets the architecture, the operating system, and the host name by itself. Nothing here says which GPU the machine has. A count of nvidia.com/gpu: 1 is the same on an Orin NX with 16 GB and on a Thor with 128 GB, and a job that needs 60 GB of GPU memory fits on only one of them. On clusters that the GPU Operator supports, a component called GPU Feature Discovery adds labels such as nvidia.com/gpu.product and nvidia.com/gpu.memory to each node. On our fleet we would set a label by hand as each machine joins:

$ kubectl label node thor-3 gpucloud.dev/model=jetson-agx-thor

A pod then restricts itself to machines with that label through the nodeSelector field of its spec, and the scheduler considers only nodes whose labels match. Chapter 8 uses labels of this kind to place jobs.

Asking for machines

Now for the first half: getting the machines to exist. We describe what we want as pools. A pool is a model and a count: five Thors, two Sparks. The controller’s job is to make the account hold exactly that.

This is reconciliation again, with the provider’s interface in place of the API server. The controller compares the wanted count with the machines it has and creates the difference. The difficulty lies in a property of the create call that the calls in chapter 1 did not have. Creating a machine changes the world outside the cluster, costs money while the machine runs, and counts against a quota.

Consider what the controller knows after a create call. There are three outcomes:

  1. A reply arrives with the new machine’s identifier. The machine exists.
  2. A reply arrives with an error such as “quota exceeded”. No machine was created.
  3. No reply arrives before the controller’s timeout.

The third outcome is the awkward one, because it covers two different situations that look identical from the controller’s side. The request may have been lost on the way, in which case nothing was created. Or the provider may have created the machine and the reply was lost on the way back. Figure 5.2 follows the second situation.

If the controller assumes nothing was created and sends another create, it may end up with two machines where it wanted one. It owns the second and has no record of the first, which runs, costs money, and uses up quota until someone finds it. If the controller assumes the machine was created, it has no identifier to manage it by.

A token that names the request

The remedy is to let the controller name each request. The controller attaches a client token to the call: a string it chose, which identifies this particular machine-to-be. The provider stores the token with the machine it creates. When a second call arrives with a token it has already seen, the provider creates nothing and returns the existing machine.

A call with this property is called idempotent: sending it twice has the same effect as sending it once. With an idempotent create, the controller needs no way to tell the two timeout situations apart. It sends the same call again, and the answer is the same machine either way.

The token has to be the same on the retry, so it cannot be random. Our controller derives it from the pool’s name and the slot it is filling: the third Thor’s token is thor-3, on every attempt. Real providers work the same way. The call that creates a virtual machine in Amazon EC2 takes a ClientToken parameter for exactly this purpose.

Low-level design: provisioner

TypeHoldsRule it keeps
Machineidentifier, model, the token it was created withfixed once created
Providerthe quota per model, every machine, a call countera model’s machines stay within its quota; one machine per token
Poola name, a model, the wanted countfixed for the run
Controllerthe provider, and the machines it owns per poolit owns a machine only after a successful reply
OperationDoes
Provider.Create(model, token)returns the machine with this token if one exists; otherwise checks the quota and creates one
Controller.Reconcile(pool)fills the pool’s empty slots in order and stops at the first failed call

Building provisioner

The provider

The provider is the account. Create does three things in order. It looks for a machine that already carries the caller’s token and, if it finds one, returns it. It checks the quota. Then it creates the machine, and only after that does the run get the chance to lose the reply. That ordering is what makes the lost reply dangerous: the machine is in the list before the caller learns anything.

Listing 5.1 The provider and its create call, in Go. code/go/provisioner/main.go

// Machine is one machine the provider has created.
type Machine struct {
	// ID is the provider's identifier for the machine.
	ID string
	// Model is the kind of machine.
	Model string
	// Token is the client token of the call that created it, or "".
	Token string
}

// Errors a create call can return.
var (
	// ErrTimeout means no reply arrived. The machine may or may not exist.
	ErrTimeout = errors.New("timed out")
	// ErrQuota means the account already holds as many machines of this model
	// as it is allowed.
	ErrQuota = errors.New("quota exceeded")
)

// Provider is the account that machines are created in.
type Provider struct {
	// quota is the number of machines of each model the account may hold.
	quota map[string]int
	// machines lists every machine that exists, in creation order.
	machines []Machine
	// calls counts create calls, so the run can lose the reply to one of them.
	calls int
	// loseReply is the number of the call whose reply is lost.
	loseReply int
}

// count returns how many machines of a model exist.
func (p *Provider) count(model string) int {
	n := 0

	for _, m := range p.machines {
		if m.Model == model {
			n++
		}
	}

	return n
}

// Create makes one machine. A call that carries a token already used by an
// existing machine returns that machine and creates nothing.
func (p *Provider) Create(model, token string) (Machine, error) {
	p.calls++

	if token != "" {
		for _, m := range p.machines {
			if m.Token == token {
				return m, nil
			}
		}
	}

	if p.count(model) >= p.quota[model] {
		return Machine{}, ErrQuota
	}

	m := Machine{ID: fmt.Sprintf("m-%02d", len(p.machines)+1), Model: model, Token: token}
	p.machines = append(p.machines, m)

	if p.calls == p.loseReply {
		return Machine{}, ErrTimeout
	}

	return m, nil
}

Listing 5.2 The provider and its create call, in Rust. code/rust/provisioner/src/main.rs

/// One machine the provider has created.
#[derive(Clone)]
struct Machine {
    /// The provider's identifier for the machine.
    id: String,
    /// The kind of machine.
    model: &'static str,
    /// The client token of the call that created it, if it carried one.
    token: Option<String>,
}

/// The ways a create call can fail.
enum CreateError {
    /// No reply arrived. The machine may or may not exist.
    Timeout,
    /// The account already holds as many machines of this model as it is
    /// allowed.
    Quota,
}

impl fmt::Display for CreateError {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut fmt::Formatter<'_>) -> fmt::Result {
        f.write_str(match self {
            CreateError::Timeout => "timed out",
            CreateError::Quota => "quota exceeded",
        })
    }
}

/// The account that machines are created in.
struct Provider {
    /// The number of machines of each model the account may hold.
    quota: BTreeMap<&'static str, usize>,
    /// Every machine that exists, in creation order.
    machines: Vec<Machine>,
    /// Counts create calls, so the run can lose the reply to one of them.
    calls: usize,
    /// The number of the call whose reply is lost.
    lose_reply: usize,
}

impl Provider {
    /// Returns how many machines of a model exist.
    fn count(&self, model: &str) -> usize {
        self.machines.iter().filter(|m| m.model == model).count()
    }

    /// Makes one machine. A call that carries a token already used by an
    /// existing machine returns that machine and creates nothing.
    fn create(&mut self, model: &'static str, token: Option<&str>) -> Result<Machine, CreateError> {
        self.calls += 1;
        if let Some(token) = token
            && let Some(existing) = self
                .machines
                .iter()
                .find(|m| m.token.as_deref() == Some(token))
        {
            return Ok(existing.clone());
        }

        if self.count(model) >= self.quota[model] {
            return Err(CreateError::Quota);
        }

        let machine = Machine {
            id: format!("m-{:02}", self.machines.len() + 1),
            model,
            token: token.map(str::to_owned),
        };
        self.machines.push(machine.clone());

        if self.calls == self.lose_reply {
            return Err(CreateError::Timeout);
        }

        Ok(machine)
    }
}

The Rust version represents a missing token as None, and the lookup is an if let ... && let ... chain, the same construct we used in section 1.1: the first let unwraps the caller’s token, and the second searches for a machine that carries it.

The controller

Reconcile works on one pool. It names the next empty slot, builds the token from that name when tokens are switched on, and calls the provider. A failed call ends the pass for this pool, and the controller records nothing for it. The next pass starts from the same slot.

Listing 5.3 The pool and the controller's reconcile, in Go. code/go/provisioner/main.go

// Pool is a group of identical machines and the size its owner wants.
type Pool struct {
	// Name prefixes the name of each slot: thor-1, thor-2, and so on.
	Name string
	// Model is the kind of machine in the pool.
	Model string
	// Want is the number of machines the pool should have.
	Want int
}

// Controller brings each pool to its wanted size.
type Controller struct {
	provider *Provider
	// tokens says whether create calls carry a client token.
	tokens bool
	// owned maps a pool's name to the machines the controller knows it has.
	owned map[string][]Machine
}

// Reconcile fills the empty slots of one pool, in order, and stops at the
// first call that fails. It returns true when the pool is full.
func (c *Controller) Reconcile(pool Pool) bool {
	for len(c.owned[pool.Name]) < pool.Want {
		slot := fmt.Sprintf("%s-%d", pool.Name, len(c.owned[pool.Name])+1)
		token := ""

		if c.tokens {
			token = slot
		}

		m, err := c.provider.Create(pool.Model, token)

		if err != nil {
			fmt.Printf("    %-8s create failed: %v\n", slot, err)

			return false
		}

		fmt.Printf("    %-8s is machine %s\n", slot, m.ID)
		c.owned[pool.Name] = append(c.owned[pool.Name], m)
	}

	return true
}

Listing 5.4 The pool and the controller's reconcile, in Rust. code/rust/provisioner/src/main.rs

/// A group of identical machines and the size its owner wants.
struct Pool {
    /// Prefixes the name of each slot: thor-1, thor-2, and so on.
    name: &'static str,
    /// The kind of machine in the pool.
    model: &'static str,
    /// The number of machines the pool should have.
    want: usize,
}

/// Brings each pool to its wanted size.
struct Controller {
    provider: Provider,
    /// Whether create calls carry a client token.
    tokens: bool,
    /// Maps a pool's name to the machines the controller knows it has.
    owned: BTreeMap<&'static str, Vec<Machine>>,
}

impl Controller {
    /// Fills the empty slots of one pool, in order, and stops at the first
    /// call that fails. It returns true when the pool is full.
    fn reconcile(&mut self, pool: &Pool) -> bool {
        let owned = self.owned.entry(pool.name).or_default();

        while owned.len() < pool.want {
            let slot = format!("{}-{}", pool.name, owned.len() + 1);
            let token = self.tokens.then_some(slot.as_str());

            match self.provider.create(pool.model, token) {
                Ok(machine) => {
                    println!("    {slot:<8} is machine {}", machine.id);
                    owned.push(machine);
                }
                Err(e) => {
                    println!("    {slot:<8} create failed: {e}");

                    return false;
                }
            }
        }

        true
    }
}

The controller counts only what it has been told. owned grows by one machine for each successful reply, and Reconcile reads the pool’s size from it. This is where a lost reply does its damage in the first run, as the output will show.

The run

The run asks for five Thors and two Sparks, with quotas of exactly five and two, and loses the reply to the third create call. It allows three passes and then prints what the controller owns beside what the provider holds.

Listing 5.5 The pools, the three passes, and the final comparison, in Go. code/go/provisioner/main.go

// pools is the fleet we ask for: the Thors on the shelf and the two Sparks.
var pools = []Pool{
	{"thor", "Jetson AGX Thor", 5},
	{"spark", "DGX Spark", 2},
}

// run reconciles every pool for three passes and then compares what the
// controller owns with what the provider holds.
func run(tokens bool) {
	provider := &Provider{quota: map[string]int{"Jetson AGX Thor": 5, "DGX Spark": 2}, loseReply: 3}
	c := &Controller{provider: provider, tokens: tokens, owned: map[string][]Machine{}}

	for pass := 1; pass <= 3; pass++ {
		fmt.Printf("  pass %d\n", pass)
		full := true

		for _, pool := range pools {
			full = c.Reconcile(pool) && full
		}

		if full {
			fmt.Println("    every pool is full")
			break
		}
	}

	known := map[string]bool{}

	for _, pool := range pools {
		for _, m := range c.owned[pool.Name] {
			known[m.ID] = true
		}

		fmt.Printf("  %-5s wanted %d, controller owns %d, provider holds %d\n",
			pool.Name, pool.Want, len(c.owned[pool.Name]), provider.count(pool.Model))
	}

	for _, m := range provider.machines {
		if !known[m.ID] {
			fmt.Printf("  machine %s (%s) is running and belongs to no pool\n", m.ID, m.Model)
		}
	}
}

func main() {
	fmt.Println("create calls without a client token:")
	run(false)
	fmt.Println("create calls with the slot name as the client token:")
	run(true)
}

Listing 5.6 The pools, the three passes, and the final comparison, in Rust. code/rust/provisioner/src/main.rs

/// The fleet we ask for: the Thors on the shelf and the two Sparks.
const POOLS: [Pool; 2] = [
    Pool {
        name: "thor",
        model: "Jetson AGX Thor",
        want: 5,
    },
    Pool {
        name: "spark",
        model: "DGX Spark",
        want: 2,
    },
];

/// Reconciles every pool for three passes and then compares what the
/// controller owns with what the provider holds.
fn run(tokens: bool) {
    let provider = Provider {
        quota: BTreeMap::from([("Jetson AGX Thor", 5), ("DGX Spark", 2)]),
        machines: Vec::new(),
        calls: 0,
        lose_reply: 3,
    };
    let mut c = Controller {
        provider,
        tokens,
        owned: BTreeMap::new(),
    };

    for pass in 1..=3 {
        println!("  pass {pass}");
        let mut full = true;

        for pool in &POOLS {
            full = c.reconcile(pool) && full;
        }

        if full {
            println!("    every pool is full");
            break;
        }
    }

    let mut known = BTreeSet::new();

    for pool in &POOLS {
        let owned = &c.owned[pool.name];
        known.extend(owned.iter().map(|m| m.id.as_str()));
        println!(
            "  {:<5} wanted {}, controller owns {}, provider holds {}",
            pool.name,
            pool.want,
            owned.len(),
            c.provider.count(pool.model)
        );
    }

    for m in c
        .provider
        .machines
        .iter()
        .filter(|m| !known.contains(m.id.as_str()))
    {
        println!(
            "  machine {} ({}) is running and belongs to no pool",
            m.id, m.model
        );
    }
}

fn main() {
    println!("create calls without a client token:");
    run(false);
    println!("create calls with the slot name as the client token:");
    run(true);
}
$ go run ./provisioner
create calls without a client token:
  pass 1
    thor-1   is machine m-01
    thor-2   is machine m-02
    thor-3   create failed: timed out
    spark-1  is machine m-04
    spark-2  is machine m-05
  pass 2
    thor-3   is machine m-06
    thor-4   is machine m-07
    thor-5   create failed: quota exceeded
  pass 3
    thor-5   create failed: quota exceeded
  thor  wanted 5, controller owns 4, provider holds 5
  spark wanted 2, controller owns 2, provider holds 2
  machine m-03 (Jetson AGX Thor) is running and belongs to no pool
create calls with the slot name as the client token:
  pass 1
    thor-1   is machine m-01
    thor-2   is machine m-02
    thor-3   create failed: timed out
    spark-1  is machine m-04
    spark-2  is machine m-05
  pass 2
    thor-3   is machine m-03
    thor-4   is machine m-06
    thor-5   is machine m-07
    every pool is full
  thor  wanted 5, controller owns 5, provider holds 5
  spark wanted 2, controller owns 2, provider holds 2

Both runs begin the same way. The third call creates machine m-03 and its reply is lost, so the controller reports a timeout for thor-3 and moves on to the Sparks.

In the first run, pass 2 sends a fresh create for thor-3. The provider has no way to connect it with the earlier call and creates m-06. The controller fills thor-4 with m-07, and then the create for thor-5 fails: the account now holds five Thors, which is the whole quota. Pass 3 fails in the same way, and so would every later pass. The controller owns four Thors and has no way to get a fifth, while m-03 runs with no owner. The pool is one machine short and the account is full.

In the second run, pass 2 sends the create for thor-3 again with the token thor-3. The provider finds m-03 under that token and returns it. The controller then creates thor-4 and thor-5, and every pool is full after two passes, with nothing left over.

Animation 5.1 replays both runs for the pool of Thors.

Finding machines that have no owner. Tokens prevent duplicates from retries. A controller can also lose track of a machine by crashing after the create call and before it records the result. For that reason production provisioners also tag each machine with the name of its pool at creation and periodically list the account by tag. A machine that carries the tag and appears in no record is adopted or deleted. The model’s last loop does this comparison once, at the end of the run.

The same design as Kubernetes objects

Cluster API is the Kubernetes project that manages clusters with Kubernetes objects. It runs in one cluster, called the management cluster, and creates others. Its objects map onto our model directly:

Our modelCluster API
Pool: a model and a wanted countMachineDeployment, with a replica count and a machine template
one slot of a poolMachine
Machine.IDspec.providerID on the Machine
Provideran infrastructure provider, one per cloud or kind of hardware
the join step from the start of this chaptera bootstrap provider, which generates the join configuration for each machine

A Machine object is created first and the provider’s machine second, so the object’s own name can serve as the client token, and a controller that restarts finds every slot it was working on by listing Machine objects. The record of ownership lives in the API server, where the resource versions of chapter 1 protect it, and the provider’s account is reconciled towards it.

The complete program

Listing 5.7 The complete program in Go. code/go/provisioner/main.go

// Command provisioner models a controller that asks a provider for machines
// until a pool has the size its owner asked for.
//
// The provider's create call can fail in an awkward way: the machine is
// created and the reply is lost. The program runs the same controller twice.
// The first run retries a lost reply with a plain create call. The second
// sends a client token with every call, so a retry returns the machine that
// the first attempt created.
//
// Run it from code/go with:
//
//	go run ./provisioner
package main

import (
	"errors"
	"fmt"
)

// Machine is one machine the provider has created.
type Machine struct {
	// ID is the provider's identifier for the machine.
	ID string
	// Model is the kind of machine.
	Model string
	// Token is the client token of the call that created it, or "".
	Token string
}

// Errors a create call can return.
var (
	// ErrTimeout means no reply arrived. The machine may or may not exist.
	ErrTimeout = errors.New("timed out")
	// ErrQuota means the account already holds as many machines of this model
	// as it is allowed.
	ErrQuota = errors.New("quota exceeded")
)

// Provider is the account that machines are created in.
type Provider struct {
	// quota is the number of machines of each model the account may hold.
	quota map[string]int
	// machines lists every machine that exists, in creation order.
	machines []Machine
	// calls counts create calls, so the run can lose the reply to one of them.
	calls int
	// loseReply is the number of the call whose reply is lost.
	loseReply int
}

// count returns how many machines of a model exist.
func (p *Provider) count(model string) int {
	n := 0

	for _, m := range p.machines {
		if m.Model == model {
			n++
		}
	}

	return n
}

// Create makes one machine. A call that carries a token already used by an
// existing machine returns that machine and creates nothing.
func (p *Provider) Create(model, token string) (Machine, error) {
	p.calls++

	if token != "" {
		for _, m := range p.machines {
			if m.Token == token {
				return m, nil
			}
		}
	}

	if p.count(model) >= p.quota[model] {
		return Machine{}, ErrQuota
	}

	m := Machine{ID: fmt.Sprintf("m-%02d", len(p.machines)+1), Model: model, Token: token}
	p.machines = append(p.machines, m)

	if p.calls == p.loseReply {
		return Machine{}, ErrTimeout
	}

	return m, nil
}


// Pool is a group of identical machines and the size its owner wants.
type Pool struct {
	// Name prefixes the name of each slot: thor-1, thor-2, and so on.
	Name string
	// Model is the kind of machine in the pool.
	Model string
	// Want is the number of machines the pool should have.
	Want int
}

// Controller brings each pool to its wanted size.
type Controller struct {
	provider *Provider
	// tokens says whether create calls carry a client token.
	tokens bool
	// owned maps a pool's name to the machines the controller knows it has.
	owned map[string][]Machine
}

// Reconcile fills the empty slots of one pool, in order, and stops at the
// first call that fails. It returns true when the pool is full.
func (c *Controller) Reconcile(pool Pool) bool {
	for len(c.owned[pool.Name]) < pool.Want {
		slot := fmt.Sprintf("%s-%d", pool.Name, len(c.owned[pool.Name])+1)
		token := ""

		if c.tokens {
			token = slot
		}

		m, err := c.provider.Create(pool.Model, token)

		if err != nil {
			fmt.Printf("    %-8s create failed: %v\n", slot, err)

			return false
		}

		fmt.Printf("    %-8s is machine %s\n", slot, m.ID)
		c.owned[pool.Name] = append(c.owned[pool.Name], m)
	}

	return true
}


// pools is the fleet we ask for: the Thors on the shelf and the two Sparks.
var pools = []Pool{
	{"thor", "Jetson AGX Thor", 5},
	{"spark", "DGX Spark", 2},
}

// run reconciles every pool for three passes and then compares what the
// controller owns with what the provider holds.
func run(tokens bool) {
	provider := &Provider{quota: map[string]int{"Jetson AGX Thor": 5, "DGX Spark": 2}, loseReply: 3}
	c := &Controller{provider: provider, tokens: tokens, owned: map[string][]Machine{}}

	for pass := 1; pass <= 3; pass++ {
		fmt.Printf("  pass %d\n", pass)
		full := true

		for _, pool := range pools {
			full = c.Reconcile(pool) && full
		}

		if full {
			fmt.Println("    every pool is full")
			break
		}
	}

	known := map[string]bool{}

	for _, pool := range pools {
		for _, m := range c.owned[pool.Name] {
			known[m.ID] = true
		}

		fmt.Printf("  %-5s wanted %d, controller owns %d, provider holds %d\n",
			pool.Name, pool.Want, len(c.owned[pool.Name]), provider.count(pool.Model))
	}

	for _, m := range provider.machines {
		if !known[m.ID] {
			fmt.Printf("  machine %s (%s) is running and belongs to no pool\n", m.ID, m.Model)
		}
	}
}

func main() {
	fmt.Println("create calls without a client token:")
	run(false)
	fmt.Println("create calls with the slot name as the client token:")
	run(true)
}

Listing 5.8 The complete program in Rust. code/rust/provisioner/src/main.rs

//! Models a controller that asks a provider for machines until a pool has the
//! size its owner asked for.
//!
//! The provider's create call can fail in an awkward way: the machine is
//! created and the reply is lost. The program runs the same controller twice.
//! The first run retries a lost reply with a plain create call. The second
//! sends a client token with every call, so a retry returns the machine that
//! the first attempt created.
//!
//! Run it from code/rust with:
//!
//! ```text
//! cargo run -p provisioner
//! ```

use std::collections::{BTreeMap, BTreeSet};
use std::fmt;

/// One machine the provider has created.
#[derive(Clone)]
struct Machine {
    /// The provider's identifier for the machine.
    id: String,
    /// The kind of machine.
    model: &'static str,
    /// The client token of the call that created it, if it carried one.
    token: Option<String>,
}

/// The ways a create call can fail.
enum CreateError {
    /// No reply arrived. The machine may or may not exist.
    Timeout,
    /// The account already holds as many machines of this model as it is
    /// allowed.
    Quota,
}

impl fmt::Display for CreateError {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut fmt::Formatter<'_>) -> fmt::Result {
        f.write_str(match self {
            CreateError::Timeout => "timed out",
            CreateError::Quota => "quota exceeded",
        })
    }
}

/// The account that machines are created in.
struct Provider {
    /// The number of machines of each model the account may hold.
    quota: BTreeMap<&'static str, usize>,
    /// Every machine that exists, in creation order.
    machines: Vec<Machine>,
    /// Counts create calls, so the run can lose the reply to one of them.
    calls: usize,
    /// The number of the call whose reply is lost.
    lose_reply: usize,
}

impl Provider {
    /// Returns how many machines of a model exist.
    fn count(&self, model: &str) -> usize {
        self.machines.iter().filter(|m| m.model == model).count()
    }

    /// Makes one machine. A call that carries a token already used by an
    /// existing machine returns that machine and creates nothing.
    fn create(&mut self, model: &'static str, token: Option<&str>) -> Result<Machine, CreateError> {
        self.calls += 1;
        if let Some(token) = token
            && let Some(existing) = self
                .machines
                .iter()
                .find(|m| m.token.as_deref() == Some(token))
        {
            return Ok(existing.clone());
        }

        if self.count(model) >= self.quota[model] {
            return Err(CreateError::Quota);
        }

        let machine = Machine {
            id: format!("m-{:02}", self.machines.len() + 1),
            model,
            token: token.map(str::to_owned),
        };
        self.machines.push(machine.clone());

        if self.calls == self.lose_reply {
            return Err(CreateError::Timeout);
        }

        Ok(machine)
    }
}

/// A group of identical machines and the size its owner wants.
struct Pool {
    /// Prefixes the name of each slot: thor-1, thor-2, and so on.
    name: &'static str,
    /// The kind of machine in the pool.
    model: &'static str,
    /// The number of machines the pool should have.
    want: usize,
}

/// Brings each pool to its wanted size.
struct Controller {
    provider: Provider,
    /// Whether create calls carry a client token.
    tokens: bool,
    /// Maps a pool's name to the machines the controller knows it has.
    owned: BTreeMap<&'static str, Vec<Machine>>,
}

impl Controller {
    /// Fills the empty slots of one pool, in order, and stops at the first
    /// call that fails. It returns true when the pool is full.
    fn reconcile(&mut self, pool: &Pool) -> bool {
        let owned = self.owned.entry(pool.name).or_default();

        while owned.len() < pool.want {
            let slot = format!("{}-{}", pool.name, owned.len() + 1);
            let token = self.tokens.then_some(slot.as_str());

            match self.provider.create(pool.model, token) {
                Ok(machine) => {
                    println!("    {slot:<8} is machine {}", machine.id);
                    owned.push(machine);
                }
                Err(e) => {
                    println!("    {slot:<8} create failed: {e}");

                    return false;
                }
            }
        }

        true
    }
}

/// The fleet we ask for: the Thors on the shelf and the two Sparks.
const POOLS: [Pool; 2] = [
    Pool {
        name: "thor",
        model: "Jetson AGX Thor",
        want: 5,
    },
    Pool {
        name: "spark",
        model: "DGX Spark",
        want: 2,
    },
];

/// Reconciles every pool for three passes and then compares what the
/// controller owns with what the provider holds.
fn run(tokens: bool) {
    let provider = Provider {
        quota: BTreeMap::from([("Jetson AGX Thor", 5), ("DGX Spark", 2)]),
        machines: Vec::new(),
        calls: 0,
        lose_reply: 3,
    };
    let mut c = Controller {
        provider,
        tokens,
        owned: BTreeMap::new(),
    };

    for pass in 1..=3 {
        println!("  pass {pass}");
        let mut full = true;

        for pool in &POOLS {
            full = c.reconcile(pool) && full;
        }

        if full {
            println!("    every pool is full");
            break;
        }
    }

    let mut known = BTreeSet::new();

    for pool in &POOLS {
        let owned = &c.owned[pool.name];
        known.extend(owned.iter().map(|m| m.id.as_str()));
        println!(
            "  {:<5} wanted {}, controller owns {}, provider holds {}",
            pool.name,
            pool.want,
            owned.len(),
            c.provider.count(pool.model)
        );
    }

    for m in c
        .provider
        .machines
        .iter()
        .filter(|m| !known.contains(m.id.as_str()))
    {
        println!(
            "  machine {} ({}) is running and belongs to no pool",
            m.id, m.model
        );
    }
}

fn main() {
    println!("create calls without a client token:");
    run(false);
    println!("create calls with the slot name as the client token:");
    run(true);
}

Summary

  • An account offers a create call, a list call, and a quota for each model of machine.
  • A new machine joins with a bootstrap token and the hash of the cluster’s CA key. It trades the token for a certificate that names one node, then creates its Node object and its Lease.
  • A node reports capacity and labels. The GPU count alone does not distinguish a 16 GB Orin NX from a 128 GB Thor, so GPU machines also carry labels that name the model.
  • A create call with no reply may or may not have created a machine. Retrying a plain create can leave a machine with no owner and a pool that can no longer be filled.
  • A client token derived from the slot’s name makes the create call idempotent, and the retry returns the machine the first attempt made.

References

  • Kubernetes, TLS bootstrapping: https://kubernetes.io/docs/reference/access-authn-authz/kubelet-tls-bootstrapping/
  • Kubernetes, Authenticating with bootstrap tokens: https://kubernetes.io/docs/reference/access-authn-authz/bootstrap-tokens/
  • Kubernetes, kubeadm join: https://kubernetes.io/docs/reference/setup-tools/kubeadm/kubeadm-join/
  • Amazon EC2, Ensuring idempotency in API requests: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/ec2/latest/devguide/ec2-api-idempotency.html
  • The Cluster API Book: https://cluster-api.sigs.k8s.io/
  • NVIDIA, GPU Feature Discovery: https://github.com/NVIDIA/k8s-device-plugin/blob/main/docs/gpu-feature-discovery/README.md

6. Virtual machines on Kubernetes

Every workload so far has run in a container. A container shares the host’s kernel, and some users of a GPU cluster need a kernel of their own: a customer who must run a particular operating system, a tenant who is not trusted to share a kernel with others, or a team with an appliance that ships as a disk image. For them the unit of work is a virtual machine (VM), a complete computer simulated in software, with its own kernel, on a real one.

In this chapter, we will see how a VM runs on a Kubernetes cluster and what that means for the two things this book cares about: giving the VM a GPU, and moving the VM off a machine that has to be repaired. The second leads to live migration, moving a running VM to another host without stopping it. We will reason about when that can work, and then write migration, in Go and in Rust, a model of the algorithm that we can run with the memory sizes and link speeds of our own fleet.

This chapter covers

  • what a virtual machine is on Linux: a process, with help from the kernel
  • KubeVirt, which runs each VM inside a pod
  • two ways to give a VM a GPU, and what each costs
  • live migration by pre-copy: rounds, dirty memory, and the final pause
  • when a migration converges, worked out for the links between our machines
  • why a VM that holds a GPU directly has to be stopped to be moved

A virtual machine is a process

On Linux, a VM is an ordinary process on the host. The program is usually QEMU. It allocates a large block of its own memory and presents it to the guest as the guest’s physical memory, and it creates one thread for each of the guest’s CPUs.

Those threads run the guest’s code directly on the host’s CPU cores, at close to full speed, through a kernel feature called KVM (Kernel-based Virtual Machine). KVM uses the CPU’s virtualization support to run guest code in a restricted mode, and returns control to QEMU whenever the guest does something that needs the host’s help, such as talking to a disk or a network card. A program asks for this by opening the device file /dev/kvm.

The Orin NX that runs our cluster has it:

$ ls -l /dev/kvm
crw-rw----+ 1 root kvm 10, 232 Nov 21  2023 /dev/kvm
$ zcat /proc/config.gz | grep -E '^CONFIG_(KVM|VFIO)='
CONFIG_KVM=y
CONFIG_VFIO=y

Since a VM is a process, it can run in a container, and so in a pod. That observation is the whole basis for running VMs on Kubernetes.

KubeVirt: a VM inside a pod

KubeVirt is the project that adds virtual machines to Kubernetes. It follows the pattern of section 1.1: it teaches the API server new kinds of object with CustomResourceDefinitions and runs controllers that reconcile them. Figure 6.1 shows the parts.

A user creates a VirtualMachine object, whose spec describes the VM: its CPUs, its memory, its disks, and its devices. A controller, virt-controller, creates a pod for it, named virt-launcher followed by the VM’s name. The pod’s resource requests match the VM’s size, so the scheduler places it exactly as it places any pod, and everything we have built so far applies: the pod is counted against the node’s capacity, it shows up in gpuusage from chapter 2 if it holds a GPU, and it is evicted when chapter 4’s controller drains the node.

On each node runs virt-handler, an agent that plays the kubelet’s part for VMs. When a launcher pod starts on its node, it has the launcher start a QEMU process inside the pod’s container. The guest’s memory is that process’s memory, so it counts against the pod’s memory limit.

The running VM is represented by a second object, the VirtualMachineInstance, whose status reports the node it is on and the phase it is in. The VirtualMachine object outlives it, in the way a GpuJob outlived its pod: stopping a VM deletes the instance and keeps the VirtualMachine.

Giving a VM a GPU

A container reaches a GPU through the host’s driver, as Appendix A showed. A VM has its own kernel and so needs its own driver, which has to talk to a GPU device. There are two ways to give it one.

Passthrough hands a whole physical GPU to one VM. The host detaches the GPU from its own driver and attaches it to a stub driver, VFIO, whose job is to let a process map the device’s registers and receive its interrupts. QEMU presents the device to the guest, and the guest’s NVIDIA driver then operates the real GPU directly. The guest gets the full performance of the card. The host can no longer use it, and can no longer see what it is doing.

A vGPU, which we met in chapter 2, divides one physical GPU among several VMs. The host keeps its own driver, which creates several virtual devices and schedules their work on the real GPU. Each VM is given one virtual device.

PassthroughvGPU
What the VM holdsone whole physical GPUa share of a GPU
Driver on the hosta stub; the host does not use the GPUthe vendor’s host driver, which schedules the VMs
VMs per GPUoneseveral
Host can read the device’s statenoyes

In KubeVirt, the VM’s spec names the device it wants in a gpus list, by the same kind of resource name a pod uses, and a device plugin on the node advertises the GPUs that have been set aside for VMs. The scheduler counts them as it counts nvidia.com/gpu.

The last row of the table decides what the rest of this chapter can do for a VM with a GPU, as we will see once we have built the model.

On our machines. Passthrough with VFIO works on a device that sits on a PCI bus, and our two boards differ here. The Orin NX’s GPU is part of the system-on-chip and has no bus address. Its driver is nvgpu, and nvidia-smi shows nothing where the address would be:

$ nvidia-smi | sed -n 9,10p        # on the Orin NX
|   0  Orin (nvgpu)                  N/A  | N/A              N/A |                  N/A |
| N/A   N/A  N/A               N/A /  N/A | Not Supported        |     N/A          N/A |

There is no device to detach, so a GPU workload on the Orin runs in a container. The Thor’s GPU also shares the board’s memory with the CPU, yet it appears on the PCI bus and runs the same nvidia driver as a discrete card:

$ lspci -nnk -s 01:00.0            # on the Thor
0000:01:00.0 3D controller [0302]: NVIDIA Corporation Device [10de:2b00] (rev a1)
	Kernel driver in use: nvidia
	Kernel modules: nouveau, nvgpu, nvidia_drm, nvidia

The Thor also has /dev/kvm. We have not tried to pass its GPU to a virtual machine, and with one GPU on the board, doing so would leave the host with none. The memory sizes and link speeds of the fleet are what we use in the rest of the chapter.

Moving a running VM

Chapter 4’s controller drains a machine before repairing it. For a container, draining means stopping the process and starting a new one elsewhere. A VM often runs software that expects to stay up for months, and its users did not write it to be restarted. For a VM we would like something better: to move it to another host while it runs.

A VM’s state is its memory, the registers of its CPUs, and the state of its devices. The registers and most device state amount to a few kilobytes. The memory is the problem. A VM sized for a Thor may hold 100 GB, and at the 3.125 GB per second that one 25 GbE lane carries, copying it takes 32 seconds. If the VM were paused for that long, its network connections would time out and its users would see an outage.

So the copy happens while the VM runs, and that creates a second problem: the VM keeps writing to its memory during the copy, and the pages it writes after they were sent are stale on the other side. Memory that has been written since it was last sent is called dirty.

Pre-copy, round by round

The standard algorithm, pre-copy, turns this into a loop.

  1. Mark all of the VM’s memory as dirty.
  2. Send every dirty page to the target host, and clear the mark on each page as it is sent. The VM keeps running, and the hypervisor marks each page it writes.
  3. When the round ends, some pages are dirty again. If they are few enough to send within the pause we are willing to accept, go to step 4. Otherwise go back to step 2.
  4. Pause the VM, send the remaining dirty pages and the CPU and device state, and resume the VM on the target.

Each round sends less than the one before, provided the VM dirties memory more slowly than the link carries it. Let us work one case by hand. A 100 GB VM dirties 4 GB each second, and the link carries 12.5 GB each second, which is four bonded 25 GbE lanes.

  • Round 1 sends 100 GB. That takes 100 / 12.5 = 8 seconds, during which the VM dirties 4 × 8 = 32 GB.
  • Round 2 sends 32 GB in 2.56 seconds, during which the VM dirties 10.24 GB.
  • Round 3 sends 10.24 GB in 0.82 seconds, during which the VM dirties 3.28 GB.
  • 3.28 GB takes 0.26 seconds to send. If we allow a pause of 0.3 seconds, the VM is paused now and the last 3.28 GB are sent.

Each round is smaller than the last by the same factor: the dirty rate divided by the link rate, here 4 / 12.5 = 0.32. Figure 6.2 shows the rounds as bars.

That factor is the whole story of convergence:

  • When the VM dirties memory more slowly than the link carries it, the factor is below one, the rounds shrink, and the migration finishes.
  • When the VM dirties memory as fast as the link carries it, or faster, the factor is one or more. Every round ends with as much dirty memory as it started with, and the migration does not reach step 4.

A hypervisor has two remedies for the second case. It can slow the guest down, by giving its CPU threads less time to run, which lowers the rate at which they dirty memory. QEMU calls this auto-converge. Or it can switch to post-copy: pause the VM at once, move only the CPU and device state, resume it on the target, and fetch each page from the source at the moment the guest first touches it. Post-copy always finishes, at the price that a network failure during the migration leaves the VM’s memory split between two hosts with no complete copy on either.

Low-level design: migration

TypeHolds
VMa name, the memory size in GB, the rate at which memory is dirtied in GB per second
Linka name, and what it carries in GB per second
OperationDoes
Migrate(vm, link, throttle)runs rounds until the remainder fits in the pause, and prints each round
RuleValue
the longest pause0.3 seconds, so the last round may send at most 0.3 × the link rate
a round makes no progresswhen the memory dirtied during it is at least the memory it sent
throttlingeach round with no progress slows the guest by a further 20 percent, up to 80 percent

Building migration

The VM and the link

The model needs three numbers: the VM’s memory, the rate at which the VM dirties it, and the rate of the link. Link speeds are quoted in gigabits per second and memory in gigabytes, so each link is entered in gigabytes per second: 25 GbE is 25 / 8 = 3.125.

Listing 6.1 The VM, the link, and the limits, in Go. code/go/migration/main.go

// VM describes the virtual machine being moved.
type VM struct {
	// Name labels the VM in the output.
	Name string
	// MemoryGB is the size of the VM's memory.
	MemoryGB float64
	// DirtyGBps is how much memory the VM's programs change each second.
	DirtyGBps float64
}

// Link is the network path between the two hosts.
type Link struct {
	// Name labels the link in the output.
	Name string
	// GBps is what the link carries, in gigabytes per second. A link of
	// 8 gigabits per second carries 1 gigabyte per second.
	GBps float64
}

// Limits of the algorithm.
const (
	// maxPauseSeconds is the longest the VM may be paused for the last round.
	maxPauseSeconds = 0.3
	// maxRounds ends a migration that keeps making progress too slowly.
	maxRounds = 30
	// throttleStep is how much more the guest is slowed each time a round
	// fails to shrink the remaining memory, as a fraction of full speed.
	throttleStep = 0.2
	// maxThrottle is the most the guest may be slowed.
	maxThrottle = 0.8
)

Listing 6.2 The VM, the link, and the limits, in Rust. code/rust/migration/src/main.rs

/// The virtual machine being moved.
struct Vm {
    /// Labels the VM in the output.
    name: &'static str,
    /// The size of the VM's memory.
    memory_gb: f64,
    /// How much memory the VM's programs change each second.
    dirty_gbps: f64,
}

/// The network path between the two hosts.
struct Link {
    /// Labels the link in the output.
    name: &'static str,
    /// What the link carries, in gigabytes per second. A link of 8 gigabits
    /// per second carries 1 gigabyte per second.
    gbps: f64,
}

/// The longest the VM may be paused for the last round, in seconds.
const MAX_PAUSE_SECONDS: f64 = 0.3;
/// Ends a migration that keeps making progress too slowly.
const MAX_ROUNDS: u32 = 30;
/// How much more the guest is slowed each time a round fails to shrink the
/// remaining memory, as a fraction of full speed.
const THROTTLE_STEP: f64 = 0.2;
/// The most the guest may be slowed.
const MAX_THROTTLE: f64 = 0.8;

One migration

Migrate keeps one quantity between rounds: remaining, the memory still to be sent. It starts as the whole memory. Each pass of the loop first asks whether remaining can be sent within the pause, and if so pauses the VM and finishes. Otherwise it computes how long the round takes and how much memory the guest dirties in that time. The dirtied amount is capped at the VM’s size, since a page that is written twice is still one dirty page.

Listing 6.3 The pre-copy loop, in Go. code/go/migration/main.go

// Migrate runs pre-copy rounds until the remaining memory can be sent within
// the allowed pause, and prints each round. With throttle set, it slows the
// guest whenever a round leaves as much memory to send as it started with.
func Migrate(vm VM, link Link, throttle bool) {
	fmt.Printf("%s over %s", vm.Name, link.Name)

	if throttle {
		fmt.Print(", with throttling")
	}

	fmt.Println(":")

	remaining := vm.MemoryGB // memory still to be sent
	slowed := 0.0            // fraction by which the guest is slowed
	elapsed := 0.0

	for round := 1; round <= maxRounds; round++ {
		if remaining <= link.GBps*maxPauseSeconds {
			pause := remaining / link.GBps
			fmt.Printf("  round %2d: VM paused, %.2f GB sent in %.2f s\n", round, remaining, pause)
			fmt.Printf("  done in %.1f s; the VM was paused for %.0f ms\n", elapsed+pause, pause*1000)

			return
		}

		seconds := remaining / link.GBps
		dirtied := min(vm.DirtyGBps*(1-slowed)*seconds, vm.MemoryGB)
		fmt.Printf("  round %2d: %6.2f GB sent in %6.1f s, %6.2f GB changed meanwhile\n",
			round, remaining, seconds, dirtied)
		elapsed += seconds

		if dirtied >= remaining {
			if !throttle || slowed >= maxThrottle {
				fmt.Println("  does not converge: memory changes as fast as the link carries it")

				return
			}

			slowed += throttleStep
			fmt.Printf("            no progress: guest slowed to %.0f%% of full speed\n", (1-slowed)*100)
		}

		remaining = dirtied
	}

	fmt.Printf("  gave up after %d rounds\n", maxRounds)
}

Listing 6.4 The pre-copy loop, in Rust. code/rust/migration/src/main.rs

/// Runs pre-copy rounds until the remaining memory can be sent within the
/// allowed pause, and prints each round. With `throttle` set, it slows the
/// guest whenever a round leaves as much memory to send as it started with.
fn migrate(vm: &Vm, link: &Link, throttle: bool) {
    let suffix = if throttle { ", with throttling" } else { "" };
    println!("{} over {}{suffix}:", vm.name, link.name);

    let mut remaining = vm.memory_gb; // memory still to be sent
    let mut slowed = 0.0; // fraction by which the guest is slowed
    let mut elapsed = 0.0;

    for round in 1..=MAX_ROUNDS {
        if remaining <= link.gbps * MAX_PAUSE_SECONDS {
            let pause = remaining / link.gbps;
            println!("  round {round:2}: VM paused, {remaining:.2} GB sent in {pause:.2} s");
            println!(
                "  done in {:.1} s; the VM was paused for {:.0} ms",
                elapsed + pause,
                pause * 1000.0
            );

            return;
        }

        let seconds = remaining / link.gbps;
        let dirtied = (vm.dirty_gbps * (1.0 - slowed) * seconds).min(vm.memory_gb);
        println!(
            "  round {round:2}: {remaining:6.2} GB sent in {seconds:6.1} s, {dirtied:6.2} GB changed meanwhile"
        );
        elapsed += seconds;

        if dirtied >= remaining {
            if !throttle || slowed >= MAX_THROTTLE {
                println!("  does not converge: memory changes as fast as the link carries it");

                return;
            }

            slowed += THROTTLE_STEP;
            println!(
                "            no progress: guest slowed to {:.0}% of full speed",
                (1.0 - slowed) * 100.0
            );
        }

        remaining = dirtied;
    }

    println!("  gave up after {MAX_ROUNDS} rounds");
}

A round that dirties at least as much as it sent has made no progress. Without throttling the function reports that the migration does not converge. With throttling it slows the guest by another 20 percent and tries again.

The run

The run uses two VMs and the four links our fleet has: the Orin NX’s 1 GbE, one of a Thor’s 25 GbE lanes, all four lanes bonded together, and the 200 GbE cable between the two Sparks.

Listing 6.5 The VMs, the links, and the six migrations, in Go. code/go/migration/main.go

func main() {
	// An inference VM sized for the Orin NX, and a training VM sized for a
	// Thor or a Spark.
	inference := VM{"16 GB inference VM (0.2 GB/s changed)", 16, 0.2}
	training := VM{"100 GB training VM (4 GB/s changed)", 100, 4}

	// The links in the fleet, in gigabytes per second.
	orin := Link{"1 GbE", 0.125}
	lane := Link{"one 25 GbE lane", 3.125}
	bonded := Link{"four bonded 25 GbE lanes", 12.5}
	spark := Link{"the Sparks' 200 GbE cable", 25}

	Migrate(inference, orin, false)
	Migrate(inference, lane, false)
	Migrate(training, lane, false)
	Migrate(training, lane, true)
	Migrate(training, bonded, false)
	Migrate(training, spark, false)
}

Listing 6.6 The VMs, the links, and the six migrations, in Rust. code/rust/migration/src/main.rs

fn main() {
    // An inference VM sized for the Orin NX, and a training VM sized for a
    // Thor or a Spark.
    let inference = Vm {
        name: "16 GB inference VM (0.2 GB/s changed)",
        memory_gb: 16.0,
        dirty_gbps: 0.2,
    };
    let training = Vm {
        name: "100 GB training VM (4 GB/s changed)",
        memory_gb: 100.0,
        dirty_gbps: 4.0,
    };

    // The links in the fleet, in gigabytes per second.
    let orin = Link {
        name: "1 GbE",
        gbps: 0.125,
    };
    let lane = Link {
        name: "one 25 GbE lane",
        gbps: 3.125,
    };
    let bonded = Link {
        name: "four bonded 25 GbE lanes",
        gbps: 12.5,
    };
    let spark = Link {
        name: "the Sparks' 200 GbE cable",
        gbps: 25.0,
    };

    migrate(&inference, &orin, false);
    migrate(&inference, &lane, false);
    migrate(&training, &lane, false);
    migrate(&training, &lane, true);
    migrate(&training, &bonded, false);
    migrate(&training, &spark, false);
}

  round  1: 100.00 GB sent in    8.0 s,  32.00 GB changed meanwhile
  round  2:  32.00 GB sent in    2.6 s,  10.24 GB changed meanwhile

100 GB training VM (4 GB/s changed) over the Sparks' 200 GbE cable:
  round  1: 100.00 GB sent in    4.0 s,  16.00 GB changed meanwhile
  round  2:  16.00 GB sent in    0.6 s,   2.56 GB changed meanwhile
  round  3: VM paused, 2.56 GB sent in 0.10 s
  done in 4.7 s; the VM was paused for 102 ms

The inference VM dirties 0.2 GB per second. The Orin NX’s 1 GbE link carries 0.125 GB per second, which is less, so after 128 seconds of copying all 16 GB are dirty again. Over one 25 GbE lane the same VM moves in 5.5 seconds with a pause of 21 milliseconds. The VM is the same in both runs. The link decides the outcome.

The training VM dirties 4 GB per second, which is more than one 25 GbE lane carries, so that migration does not converge either. With throttling, the first two rounds make no progress and the guest is slowed twice, to 60 percent of full speed. At that speed it dirties 2.4 GB per second, the factor becomes 2.4 / 3.125 = 0.768, and the rounds shrink, slowly: the migration takes 21 rounds and 201 seconds, and the training job runs at reduced speed for most of that time.

Over the four bonded lanes the factor is 0.32 and the migration takes 11.6 seconds. Over the Sparks’ cable it is 0.16 and the migration takes 4.7 seconds, with a pause of 102 milliseconds against 262.

Animation 6.1 plays three of these migrations for the training VM, with the memory still to send beside the memory that changes during the round.

This is the reason clusters that migrate large VMs give migration a fast network of its own. The memory size sets how long the first round takes. The ratio of dirty rate to link rate sets whether there is a last round at all.

Migration in KubeVirt

KubeVirt runs this algorithm through objects. A VirtualMachineInstanceMigration names a running instance. A controller creates a second launcher pod on another node, the two QEMU processes connect to each other, and the source sends its memory in rounds. When the migration completes, the instance’s status names the new node and the old pod is removed.

A VM’s spec can set evictionStrategy: LiveMigrate. An eviction of the launcher pod, the request chapter 4’s drain sends for every pod on a node, then starts a migration and is refused until the VM has moved. This is how the two chapters meet: the remediation controller drains with the Eviction API and needs no knowledge of VMs, and each VM leaves in the way its owner chose.

The VM that cannot move

Step 4 of pre-copy sends “the CPU and device state”. For a virtual disk or a virtual network card, the hypervisor implements the device, so it has that state in its own memory and can send it.

A GPU given to the VM by passthrough is different. The device’s state, including everything in the GPU’s memory, is inside the physical card on the source host, and the host gave up its driver for that card when it attached the stub. The hypervisor has nothing to read and nothing to send, and the target host’s GPU is a different physical device. KubeVirt therefore marks a VM with a passed-through device as not migratable. Draining its node means shutting the VM down and starting it again elsewhere, and the checkpointing discipline of chapter 4 applies to the jobs inside it.

With a vGPU, the host’s driver holds the device’s state and can save and restore it, so migration is possible when the host driver and the hypervisor both support it. That support depends on the driver and hypervisor versions, and has to be checked for each combination.

The complete program

Listing 6.7 The complete program in Go. code/go/migration/main.go

// Command migration models the live migration of a virtual machine: moving a
// running VM's memory from one host to another while the VM keeps running.
//
// The model is the pre-copy algorithm. Each round sends the memory that
// changed during the round before it. The VM is paused only for the last
// round, when what remains is small enough to send within the allowed pause.
// The program runs the algorithm for VMs of two sizes over the links in our
// fleet and prints each round.
//
// Run it from code/go with:
//
//	go run ./migration
package main

import "fmt"

// VM describes the virtual machine being moved.
type VM struct {
	// Name labels the VM in the output.
	Name string
	// MemoryGB is the size of the VM's memory.
	MemoryGB float64
	// DirtyGBps is how much memory the VM's programs change each second.
	DirtyGBps float64
}

// Link is the network path between the two hosts.
type Link struct {
	// Name labels the link in the output.
	Name string
	// GBps is what the link carries, in gigabytes per second. A link of
	// 8 gigabits per second carries 1 gigabyte per second.
	GBps float64
}

// Limits of the algorithm.
const (
	// maxPauseSeconds is the longest the VM may be paused for the last round.
	maxPauseSeconds = 0.3
	// maxRounds ends a migration that keeps making progress too slowly.
	maxRounds = 30
	// throttleStep is how much more the guest is slowed each time a round
	// fails to shrink the remaining memory, as a fraction of full speed.
	throttleStep = 0.2
	// maxThrottle is the most the guest may be slowed.
	maxThrottle = 0.8
)


// Migrate runs pre-copy rounds until the remaining memory can be sent within
// the allowed pause, and prints each round. With throttle set, it slows the
// guest whenever a round leaves as much memory to send as it started with.
func Migrate(vm VM, link Link, throttle bool) {
	fmt.Printf("%s over %s", vm.Name, link.Name)

	if throttle {
		fmt.Print(", with throttling")
	}

	fmt.Println(":")

	remaining := vm.MemoryGB // memory still to be sent
	slowed := 0.0            // fraction by which the guest is slowed
	elapsed := 0.0

	for round := 1; round <= maxRounds; round++ {
		if remaining <= link.GBps*maxPauseSeconds {
			pause := remaining / link.GBps
			fmt.Printf("  round %2d: VM paused, %.2f GB sent in %.2f s\n", round, remaining, pause)
			fmt.Printf("  done in %.1f s; the VM was paused for %.0f ms\n", elapsed+pause, pause*1000)

			return
		}

		seconds := remaining / link.GBps
		dirtied := min(vm.DirtyGBps*(1-slowed)*seconds, vm.MemoryGB)
		fmt.Printf("  round %2d: %6.2f GB sent in %6.1f s, %6.2f GB changed meanwhile\n",
			round, remaining, seconds, dirtied)
		elapsed += seconds

		if dirtied >= remaining {
			if !throttle || slowed >= maxThrottle {
				fmt.Println("  does not converge: memory changes as fast as the link carries it")

				return
			}

			slowed += throttleStep
			fmt.Printf("            no progress: guest slowed to %.0f%% of full speed\n", (1-slowed)*100)
		}

		remaining = dirtied
	}

	fmt.Printf("  gave up after %d rounds\n", maxRounds)
}


func main() {
	// An inference VM sized for the Orin NX, and a training VM sized for a
	// Thor or a Spark.
	inference := VM{"16 GB inference VM (0.2 GB/s changed)", 16, 0.2}
	training := VM{"100 GB training VM (4 GB/s changed)", 100, 4}

	// The links in the fleet, in gigabytes per second.
	orin := Link{"1 GbE", 0.125}
	lane := Link{"one 25 GbE lane", 3.125}
	bonded := Link{"four bonded 25 GbE lanes", 12.5}
	spark := Link{"the Sparks' 200 GbE cable", 25}

	Migrate(inference, orin, false)
	Migrate(inference, lane, false)
	Migrate(training, lane, false)
	Migrate(training, lane, true)
	Migrate(training, bonded, false)
	Migrate(training, spark, false)
}

Listing 6.8 The complete program in Rust. code/rust/migration/src/main.rs

//! Models the live migration of a virtual machine: moving a running VM's
//! memory from one host to another while the VM keeps running.
//!
//! The model is the pre-copy algorithm. Each round sends the memory that
//! changed during the round before it. The VM is paused only for the last
//! round, when what remains is small enough to send within the allowed pause.
//! The program runs the algorithm for VMs of two sizes over the links in our
//! fleet and prints each round.
//!
//! Run it from code/rust with:
//!
//! ```text
//! cargo run -p migration
//! ```

/// The virtual machine being moved.
struct Vm {
    /// Labels the VM in the output.
    name: &'static str,
    /// The size of the VM's memory.
    memory_gb: f64,
    /// How much memory the VM's programs change each second.
    dirty_gbps: f64,
}

/// The network path between the two hosts.
struct Link {
    /// Labels the link in the output.
    name: &'static str,
    /// What the link carries, in gigabytes per second. A link of 8 gigabits
    /// per second carries 1 gigabyte per second.
    gbps: f64,
}

/// The longest the VM may be paused for the last round, in seconds.
const MAX_PAUSE_SECONDS: f64 = 0.3;
/// Ends a migration that keeps making progress too slowly.
const MAX_ROUNDS: u32 = 30;
/// How much more the guest is slowed each time a round fails to shrink the
