/// remaining memory, as a fraction of full speed.
const THROTTLE_STEP: f64 = 0.2;
/// The most the guest may be slowed.
const MAX_THROTTLE: f64 = 0.8;

/// Runs pre-copy rounds until the remaining memory can be sent within the
/// allowed pause, and prints each round. With `throttle` set, it slows the
/// guest whenever a round leaves as much memory to send as it started with.
fn migrate(vm: &Vm, link: &Link, throttle: bool) {
    let suffix = if throttle { ", with throttling" } else { "" };
    println!("{} over {}{suffix}:", vm.name, link.name);

    let mut remaining = vm.memory_gb; // memory still to be sent
    let mut slowed = 0.0; // fraction by which the guest is slowed
    let mut elapsed = 0.0;

    for round in 1..=MAX_ROUNDS {
        if remaining <= link.gbps * MAX_PAUSE_SECONDS {
            let pause = remaining / link.gbps;
            println!("  round {round:2}: VM paused, {remaining:.2} GB sent in {pause:.2} s");
            println!(
                "  done in {:.1} s; the VM was paused for {:.0} ms",
                elapsed + pause,
                pause * 1000.0
            );

            return;
        }

        let seconds = remaining / link.gbps;
        let dirtied = (vm.dirty_gbps * (1.0 - slowed) * seconds).min(vm.memory_gb);
        println!(
            "  round {round:2}: {remaining:6.2} GB sent in {seconds:6.1} s, {dirtied:6.2} GB changed meanwhile"
        );
        elapsed += seconds;

        if dirtied >= remaining {
            if !throttle || slowed >= MAX_THROTTLE {
                println!("  does not converge: memory changes as fast as the link carries it");

                return;
            }

            slowed += THROTTLE_STEP;
            println!(
                "            no progress: guest slowed to {:.0}% of full speed",
                (1.0 - slowed) * 100.0
            );
        }

        remaining = dirtied;
    }

    println!("  gave up after {MAX_ROUNDS} rounds");
}

fn main() {
    // An inference VM sized for the Orin NX, and a training VM sized for a
    // Thor or a Spark.
    let inference = Vm {
        name: "16 GB inference VM (0.2 GB/s changed)",
        memory_gb: 16.0,
        dirty_gbps: 0.2,
    };
    let training = Vm {
        name: "100 GB training VM (4 GB/s changed)",
        memory_gb: 100.0,
        dirty_gbps: 4.0,
    };

    // The links in the fleet, in gigabytes per second.
    let orin = Link {
        name: "1 GbE",
        gbps: 0.125,
    };
    let lane = Link {
        name: "one 25 GbE lane",
        gbps: 3.125,
    };
    let bonded = Link {
        name: "four bonded 25 GbE lanes",
        gbps: 12.5,
    };
    let spark = Link {
        name: "the Sparks' 200 GbE cable",
        gbps: 25.0,
    };

    migrate(&inference, &orin, false);
    migrate(&inference, &lane, false);
    migrate(&training, &lane, false);
    migrate(&training, &lane, true);
    migrate(&training, &bonded, false);
    migrate(&training, &spark, false);
}

Summary

  • A virtual machine on Linux is a QEMU process that runs guest code through KVM. KubeVirt runs that process inside a pod, so the scheduler, quotas, and drains treat a VM as a pod.
  • Passthrough gives a VM one whole GPU and removes it from the host. A vGPU gives a VM a share of a GPU that the host’s driver still controls.
  • Pre-copy migration sends memory in rounds while the VM runs, and pauses the VM only for the last round.
  • Each round is smaller than the one before by the ratio of the dirty rate to the link rate. A ratio of one or more means the migration does not finish without slowing the guest or switching to post-copy.
  • A VM with a passed-through GPU cannot be migrated live, because the device’s state is out of the hypervisor’s reach.

References

  • KubeVirt user guide, Architecture: https://kubevirt.io/user-guide/architecture/
  • KubeVirt user guide, Live migration: https://kubevirt.io/user-guide/compute/live_migration/
  • KubeVirt user guide, Host devices assignment: https://kubevirt.io/user-guide/compute/host-devices/
  • QEMU, Migration framework: https://www.qemu.org/docs/master/devel/migration/main.html
  • The Linux kernel, VFIO: https://docs.kernel.org/driver-api/vfio.html
  • C. Clark and others, “Live Migration of Virtual Machines”, 2nd USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI), 2005.

7. Collective communication and training failures

The jobs in the earlier chapters each ran on one machine. A model that is too large or too slow to train on one machine is trained on several at once, and that changes what a failure means. In this chapter, we will look at what the machines of a training job say to each other during every step, why the failure of one machine stops all of them, and how often a job should save its state so that a failure costs little.

We will first reason about the communication with pencil and paper, on the five Thors from chapter 4. Then we will write allreduce, in Go and in Rust: five workers running at the same time, connected in a ring, exchanging the pieces of a gradient. We will run it healthy, run it again with one worker failing part of the way through, and finish with the arithmetic of checkpoints.

This chapter covers

  • one step of training on several machines, and the sum every machine needs
  • all-reduce, and why a ring carries it better than a coordinator
  • the cost of a step in gigabytes, for our Thors and our Sparks
  • five concurrent workers in Go (goroutines and channels) and in Rust (threads and channels)
  • how the failure of one worker spreads around the ring, one neighbour per exchange
  • Young’s formula for the time between checkpoints

One step of training on five machines

Training adjusts a model’s parameters, the numbers that make up the model, a little at a time. One step takes a batch of training examples, measures how wrong the model’s output is for them, and computes a gradient: a list with one number for each parameter, saying in which direction and how strongly that parameter should change. The step ends by changing every parameter by a small multiple of its gradient entry.

With five machines, each holds a full copy of the model and takes a different batch. Each computes its own gradient. Then all five have to apply the same change, or their copies of the model drift apart. The change they apply is the average of the five gradients, so before any machine can finish the step, every machine needs the sum of all five gradients.

That requirement has a name. An operation in which every member of a group contributes a value and every member receives the combined result is called all-reduce. It belongs to a family called collective communication, a term from the MPI standard for operations that a group of processes performs together, as opposed to one process sending a message to one other. On NVIDIA GPUs the library that implements these operations is NCCL, the NVIDIA Collective Communications Library, and training frameworks call it once per step.

The gradient is as large as the model. A model with 7 billion parameters stored as 16-bit numbers has a gradient of 14 GB. The step cannot finish until 14 GB from each machine have been combined and the result has reached every machine, so the network between the machines is part of the inner loop of training.

Two ways to add five gradients

The obvious design gives the job to one machine. Every worker sends its gradient to a coordinator, the coordinator adds them, and sends the sum back to every worker.

Count what the coordinator’s network link carries in one step: five gradients in and five out. Every other link carries one in each direction. The coordinator’s link is the bottleneck, and it gets worse in proportion to the number of workers. With 14 GB gradients and five workers, 70 GB pass through one link in each direction, every step.

The second design has no coordinator. Arrange the workers in a ring, so each has a left and a right neighbour, and cut every gradient into as many chunks as there are workers. Figure 7.1 compares the two shapes.

The ring works in two phases. We follow one chunk, chunk 0, through both. Our five workers start with the values 1, 2, 3, 4, and 5 in chunk 0, so its sum is 15.

In the first phase, each worker passes a partial sum to the right, and the receiver adds its own value:

  • thor-1 sends its chunk 0, the value 1, to thor-2.
  • thor-2 adds its own 2 and sends 3 to thor-3.
  • thor-3 adds 3 and sends 6 to thor-4.
  • thor-4 adds 4 and sends 10 to thor-5.
  • thor-5 adds 5 and now holds 15, the finished sum of chunk 0.

That took four exchanges, one fewer than the number of workers. Meanwhile the other four chunks made the same journey from different starting points: chunk 1 started at thor-2 and finished at thor-1, and so on. Every worker sent one chunk in every exchange, each worker ends the phase holding the finished sum of exactly one chunk, and no two hold the same one.

In the second phase the finished chunks travel around the ring so that everyone gets a copy. thor-5 sends the 15 to thor-1, which stores it and passes it on, and after four more exchanges every worker holds it. The other finished chunks circulate at the same time.

Now count what a link carries. With N workers there are 2(N − 1) exchanges, and each sends one chunk, which is one Nth of a gradient, across every link. A worker therefore sends 2(N − 1)/N gradients’ worth of data per step. For five workers that is 1.6, and however many workers join, it stays below 2. Every link carries the same amount, and none is special.

Here is what that costs on our machines for the 14 GB gradient:

WorkersLink between themSent by each worker per stepTime for the exchange
five Thorsone 25 GbE lane, 3.125 GB/s1.6 × 14 = 22.4 GB7.2 s
five Thorsfour bonded lanes, 12.5 GB/s22.4 GB1.8 s
two Sparksthe 200 GbE cable, 25 GB/s1.0 × 14 = 14 GB0.56 s

A step whose computation takes a second is dominated by seven seconds of communication on a single 25 GbE lane. This is the reason the fast links on these machines exist, and the reason chapter 8 places a job’s workers by the links between them.

One more property follows from the ring’s shape. Every chunk crosses every link. If one link is slower than the rest, every chunk waits for it, and the whole step runs at the speed of the slowest link.

When one worker fails

The same shape decides what happens when a worker fails in the middle of a step. A worker’s every exchange depends on its left-hand neighbour: it cannot add to a chunk it has not received. So when one worker stops, its right-hand neighbour is stuck at its next exchange. That neighbour had already sent its chunk for the exchange, so the worker after it completes one more exchange and is stuck at the following one. The stall travels around the ring, one worker per exchange, until it has reached everyone.

When the stall is complete, no worker holds the sum. Each holds some finished chunks, some partial sums, and some of its own original values, in a mixture that depends on where it was stopped. The step cannot be finished by the workers that remain, because the failed worker’s values for some chunks were not sent.

The model in every worker’s memory is intact, since the step had not yet changed it. But a training job is built as a fixed group: its workers were started together with a fixed ring, and the frameworks do not shrink the ring in mid-run. The practical response to one failed worker is to stop all of them, replace the failed one, and start the whole group again from the last saved state.

This has a consequence for the control plane that the single-machine jobs did not have. The controller that owns a training job treats its pods as one unit: when one pod fails, it deletes all of them and creates them all again. And it makes the two numbers from chapter 3 matter in a new way. The time to detect the failed machine is time during which the surviving machines hold their GPUs and do nothing.

Low-level design: allreduce

TypeHoldsRule it keeps
messagea chunk’s index and its valueone message per exchange per worker
Workerits ring position, its gradient, a channel from the left and one to the rightit only sends right and only receives left
resultexchanges completed, and why the worker stopped earlyone per worker
OperationDoes
Worker.Run(n)performs 2(n − 1) exchanges: send one chunk right, receive one from the left, add it in the first phase and store it in the second
ring(n, failing, failAfter)builds the channels, starts every worker at once, and waits for all of them
interval(jobMTBF)the time between checkpoints that loses the least work

Building allreduce

Each worker in the program stands for one Thor. One number stands for each chunk, which keeps the output readable. The algorithm does the same thing to a chunk of three gigabytes.

A worker and its two channels

A worker has a position in the ring, its gradient, and two channels: it receives on one and sends on the other. Both languages use a channel, a queue that one concurrently running piece of code puts values into and another takes them out of. Nothing else is shared between workers, which mirrors the real situation: machines in a training job share no memory, only a network.

Listing 7.1 The message, the worker, and how long it waits, in Go. code/go/allreduce/main.go

// message carries one chunk of a gradient from a worker to its right-hand
// neighbour.
type message struct {
	// chunk is the index of the chunk within the gradient.
	chunk int
	// value is the chunk's contents: one number stands for the whole chunk.
	value int
}

// Worker is one machine's part in the step.
type Worker struct {
	// id is the worker's position in the ring, from 0.
	id int
	// gradient holds one value per chunk. It starts as this worker's own
	// gradient and ends as the sum over all workers.
	gradient []int
	// in receives from the left-hand neighbour, and out sends to the right.
	in  <-chan message
	out chan<- message
	// failAfter is the number of messages the worker exchanges before it
	// fails, or -1 for a worker that stays healthy.
	failAfter int
}

// errFailed is the result of the worker that the run makes fail.
var errFailed = errors.New("failed")

// patience is how long a worker waits for its neighbour before giving up.
const patience = 100 * time.Millisecond

Listing 7.2 The message, the worker, and how long it waits, in Rust. code/rust/allreduce/src/main.rs

/// Carries one chunk of a gradient from a worker to its right-hand neighbour.
struct Message {
    /// The index of the chunk within the gradient.
    chunk: usize,
    /// The chunk's contents: one number stands for the whole chunk.
    value: i64,
}

/// One machine's part in the step.
struct Worker {
    /// The worker's position in the ring, from 0.
    id: usize,
    /// One value per chunk. It starts as this worker's own gradient and ends
    /// as the sum over all workers.
    gradient: Vec<i64>,
    /// Receives from the left-hand neighbour.
    inbox: Receiver<Message>,
    /// Sends to the right-hand neighbour.
    out: Sender<Message>,
    /// The number of messages the worker exchanges before it fails, if the
    /// run makes it fail.
    fail_after: Option<usize>,
}

/// How long a worker waits for its neighbour before giving up.
const PATIENCE: Duration = Duration::from_millis(100);

The exchanges

Run is the algorithm. Each pass of its loop is one exchange: send a chunk to the right, then wait for a chunk from the left. The chunk to send is found by counting backwards from the worker’s own position, one place per exchange. Working through the hand example shows why that is right: after the first exchange, the chunk a worker sends next is always the one it received last.

Listing 7.3 A worker's 2(n − 1) exchanges, in Go. code/go/allreduce/main.go

// Run performs the worker's 2(n-1) exchanges. In each one it sends a chunk to
// the right and receives a chunk from the left. During the first n-1
// exchanges it adds what it receives to its own chunk. During the last n-1
// the chunks it receives are finished sums, and it stores them. Run returns
// the number of exchanges completed and the reason it stopped early, if any.
func (w *Worker) Run(n int) (int, error) {
	for step := range 2 * (n - 1) {
		if step == w.failAfter {
			return step, errFailed
		}

		// The chunk to send moves one place back around the gradient each
		// step, so after the first step it is the chunk just received.
		chunk := mod(w.id-step, n)
		w.out <- message{chunk, w.gradient[chunk]}
		reducing := step < n-1

		select {
		case m := <-w.in:
			if reducing {
				w.gradient[m.chunk] += m.value
			} else {
				w.gradient[m.chunk] = m.value
			}
		case <-time.After(patience):
			return step, fmt.Errorf("no chunk from %s within %v", name(mod(w.id-1, n)), patience)
		}
	}

	return 2 * (n - 1), nil
}

Listing 7.4 A worker's 2(n − 1) exchanges, in Rust. code/rust/allreduce/src/main.rs

impl Worker {
    /// Performs the worker's 2(n-1) exchanges. In each one it sends a chunk to
    /// the right and receives a chunk from the left. During the first n-1
    /// exchanges it adds what it receives to its own chunk. During the last
    /// n-1 the chunks it receives are finished sums, and it stores them.
    /// Returns the worker with the number of exchanges completed and the
    /// reason it stopped early, if any.
    fn run(mut self, n: usize) -> (Self, usize, Option<String>) {
        for step in 0..2 * (n - 1) {
            if self.fail_after == Some(step) {
                return (self, step, Some("failed".to_owned()));
            }

            // The chunk to send moves one place back around the gradient each
            // step, so after the first step it is the chunk just received.
            // Adding 2n keeps the subtraction from going below zero.
            let chunk = (self.id + 2 * n - step) % n;
            // The right-hand neighbour may have stopped; its channel still accepts.
            let _ = self.out.send(Message {
                chunk,
                value: self.gradient[chunk],
            });
            let reducing = step < n - 1;

            match self.inbox.recv_timeout(PATIENCE) {
                Ok(m) if reducing => self.gradient[m.chunk] += m.value,
                Ok(m) => self.gradient[m.chunk] = m.value,
                Err(_) => {
                    let why = format!(
                        "no chunk from {} within {PATIENCE:?}",
                        name((self.id + n - 1) % n)
                    );

                    return (self, step, Some(why));
                }
            }
        }

        (self, 2 * (n - 1), None)
    }
}

The wait for the left-hand neighbour has a time limit, which the program calls patience. In Go the wait is a select with two cases, a message on the channel or the timer from time.After, and whichever is ready first runs. In Rust it is the channel’s own recv_timeout, which returns an error if nothing arrives in time. Without the limit a worker whose neighbour had failed would wait forever, and the program, like a training job with no timeouts, would hang with no report of what went wrong.

The Rust version of run takes self by value and returns it. The worker is moved into its thread when the thread starts and moved back out with the result when it ends, so the main thread can read each worker’s final gradient without any lock.

Building the ring

ring creates one channel per link and gives each worker the receiving end of its own link and the sending end of its right-hand neighbour’s. It then starts every worker at once and waits for all of them.

Listing 7.5 Connecting the workers and running them together, in Go. code/go/allreduce/main.go

// result is what one worker reports when it stops.
type result struct {
	steps int
	err   error
}

// ring connects n workers in a circle, runs them all at once, and returns the
// workers and their results. The worker at position failing stops after
// failAfter exchanges; pass -1 for a run with no failure.
func ring(n, failing, failAfter int) ([]*Worker, []result) {
	// Each link holds every message a worker can send in one step, so a send
	// goes through even when the receiver has stopped.
	links := make([]chan message, n)

	for i := range links {
		links[i] = make(chan message, 2*(n-1))
	}

	workers := make([]*Worker, n)

	for i := range workers {
		gradient := make([]int, n)

		for chunk := range gradient {
			gradient[chunk] = i + 1 + 10*chunk
		}

		workers[i] = &Worker{id: i, gradient: gradient, in: links[i], out: links[mod(i+1, n)], failAfter: -1}
	}

	if failing >= 0 {
		workers[failing].failAfter = failAfter
	}

	results := make([]result, n)
	var wg sync.WaitGroup

	for i, w := range workers {
		wg.Go(func() {
			steps, err := w.Run(n)
			results[i] = result{steps, err}
		})
	}

	wg.Wait()

	return workers, results
}

Listing 7.6 Connecting the workers and running them together, in Rust. code/rust/allreduce/src/main.rs

/// Connects n workers in a circle, runs them all at once, and returns each
/// worker with its result. The worker named in `failing` stops after that
/// many exchanges.
fn ring(n: usize, failing: Option<(usize, usize)>) -> Vec<(Worker, usize, Option<String>)> {
    // One channel per link. `senders[i]` leads to worker i.
    let (senders, receivers): (Vec<_>, Vec<_>) = (0..n).map(|_| mpsc::channel()).unzip();
    let workers: Vec<Worker> = receivers
        .into_iter()
        .enumerate()
        .map(|(id, inbox)| Worker {
            id,
            gradient: own_gradient(id, n),
            inbox,
            out: senders[(id + 1) % n].clone(),
            fail_after: failing
                .filter(|(who, _)| *who == id)
                .map(|(_, after)| after),
        })
        .collect();

    let handles: Vec<_> = workers
        .into_iter()
        .map(|w| thread::spawn(move || w.run(n)))
        .collect();

    handles
        .into_iter()
        .map(|h| h.join().expect("a worker panicked"))
        .collect()
}

In Go each worker runs in a goroutine started with wg.Go, a method of sync.WaitGroup that starts a function and counts it, and wg.Wait blocks until all have returned. Each goroutine writes its result into its own element of results, so no two goroutines write the same memory. In Rust each worker runs in a thread from thread::spawn, and join waits for the thread and hands back what run returned.

The Go channels have a buffer large enough for every message a worker can send in a step, and the Rust channels have no limit. A send therefore completes even when the receiving worker has stopped, as it does on a real network, where data sent to a dead machine leaves the sender and is acknowledged by nobody. The sender finds out only when it waits for something in return.

The time between checkpoints

The last piece is arithmetic. Saving a checkpoint, the model’s parameters and the position in the training data, takes time during which no training happens. Saving rarely wastes little time on checkpoints and loses much work at each failure. Saving often does the opposite. Somewhere between lies the interval that wastes the least.

Let C be the time one checkpoint takes, and M the job’s mean time between failures (MTBF), the average time from one failure to the next. With a checkpoint every T seconds:

  • the fraction of time spent writing checkpoints is C / T;
  • a failure arrives, on average, halfway between two checkpoints, so it destroys T / 2 of work, and with one failure every M seconds the fraction of time lost that way is T / (2M).

The total, C / T + T / (2M), is smallest when the two parts are equal, which happens at T = √(2CM). This is Young’s formula, published in 1974.

The M in the formula is the job’s, and a job fails when any one of its machines fails. A job on N machines therefore fails N times as often as one machine: its MTBF is one machine’s divided by N.

Listing 7.7 Young's formula, in Go. code/go/allreduce/main.go

// Costs and failure rates for the checkpoint arithmetic, in seconds.
const (
	// checkpointCost is the time one checkpoint takes to write.
	checkpointCost = 50.0
	// machineMTBF is the mean time between failures of one machine: 30 days.
	machineMTBF = 30 * 24 * 3600.0
)

// interval returns the time between checkpoints that loses the least work,
// by Young's formula: the square root of twice the checkpoint cost times the
// job's mean time between failures.
func interval(jobMTBF float64) float64 {
	return math.Sqrt(2 * checkpointCost * jobMTBF)
}

Listing 7.8 Young's formula, in Rust. code/rust/allreduce/src/main.rs

/// The time one checkpoint takes to write, in seconds.
const CHECKPOINT_COST: f64 = 50.0;
/// The mean time between failures of one machine, in seconds: 30 days.
const MACHINE_MTBF: f64 = 30.0 * 24.0 * 3600.0;

/// Returns the time between checkpoints that loses the least work, by Young's
/// formula: the square root of twice the checkpoint cost times the job's mean
/// time between failures.
fn interval(job_mtbf: f64) -> f64 {
    (2.0 * CHECKPOINT_COST * job_mtbf).sqrt()
}

The run

Listing 7.9 The healthy step, the failed step, and the checkpoint table, in Go. code/go/allreduce/main.go

func main() {
	const n = 5

	fmt.Printf("ring all-reduce over %d workers, %d chunks each:\n", n, n)
	workers, _ := ring(n, -1, 0)

	for i := range n {
		own := make([]int, n)

		for chunk := range own {
			own[chunk] = i + 1 + 10*chunk
		}

		fmt.Printf("  %s starts with %-17s and ends with %s\n", name(i), show(own), show(workers[i].gradient))
	}

	sent := 2 * (n - 1)
	fmt.Printf("  each worker sent %d chunks, %.1f times its gradient; %d messages in all\n",
		sent, float64(sent)/n, sent*n)

	fmt.Printf("the same step, with %s failing after 2 of its %d exchanges:\n", name(2), sent)
	_, results := ring(n, 2, 2)

	for i, r := range results {
		fmt.Printf("  %s stopped after %d of %d: %v\n", name(i), r.steps, sent, r.err)
	}

	fmt.Println("checkpoint interval, for a 50 s checkpoint and one failure per machine every 30 days:")
	fmt.Println("  machines  job fails every  checkpoint every  time lost")

	for _, machines := range []int{1, 5, 64, 512} {
		jobMTBF := machineMTBF / float64(machines)
		every := interval(jobMTBF)
		lost := checkpointCost/every + every/(2*jobMTBF)
		fmt.Printf("  %8d  %10.1f hours  %9.0f minutes  %8.1f%%\n", machines, jobMTBF/3600, every/60, lost*100)
	}
}

Listing 7.10 The healthy step, the failed step, and the checkpoint table, in Rust. code/rust/allreduce/src/main.rs

fn main() {
    const N: usize = 5;

    println!("ring all-reduce over {N} workers, {N} chunks each:");

    for (worker, _, _) in ring(N, None) {
        println!(
            "  {} starts with {:<17} and ends with {}",
            name(worker.id),
            show(&own_gradient(worker.id, N)),
            show(&worker.gradient)
        );
    }

    let sent = 2 * (N - 1);
    println!(
        "  each worker sent {sent} chunks, {:.1} times its gradient; {} messages in all",
        sent as f64 / N as f64,
        sent * N
    );

    println!(
        "the same step, with {} failing after 2 of its {sent} exchanges:",
        name(2)
    );

    for (worker, steps, why) in ring(N, Some((2, 2))) {
        let why = why.unwrap_or_else(|| "finished".to_owned());
        println!(
            "  {} stopped after {steps} of {sent}: {why}",
            name(worker.id)
        );
    }

    println!(
        "checkpoint interval, for a 50 s checkpoint and one failure per machine every 30 days:"
    );
    println!("  machines  job fails every  checkpoint every  time lost");

    for machines in [1, 5, 64, 512] {
        let job_mtbf = MACHINE_MTBF / f64::from(machines);
        let every = interval(job_mtbf);
        let lost = CHECKPOINT_COST / every + every / (2.0 * job_mtbf);
        println!(
            "  {machines:8}  {:10.1} hours  {:9.0} minutes  {:8.1}%",
            job_mtbf / 3600.0,
            every / 60.0,
            lost * 100.0
        );
    }
}
$ go run ./allreduce
ring all-reduce over 5 workers, 5 chunks each:
  thor-1 starts with 1 11 21 31 41     and ends with 15 65 115 165 215
  thor-2 starts with 2 12 22 32 42     and ends with 15 65 115 165 215
  thor-3 starts with 3 13 23 33 43     and ends with 15 65 115 165 215
  thor-4 starts with 4 14 24 34 44     and ends with 15 65 115 165 215
  thor-5 starts with 5 15 25 35 45     and ends with 15 65 115 165 215
  each worker sent 8 chunks, 1.6 times its gradient; 40 messages in all
the same step, with thor-3 failing after 2 of its 8 exchanges:
  thor-1 stopped after 4 of 8: no chunk from thor-5 within 100ms
  thor-2 stopped after 5 of 8: no chunk from thor-1 within 100ms
  thor-3 stopped after 2 of 8: failed
  thor-4 stopped after 2 of 8: no chunk from thor-3 within 100ms
  thor-5 stopped after 3 of 8: no chunk from thor-4 within 100ms
checkpoint interval, for a 50 s checkpoint and one failure per machine every 30 days:
  machines  job fails every  checkpoint every  time lost
         1       720.0 hours        268 minutes       0.6%
         5       144.0 hours        120 minutes       1.4%
        64        11.2 hours         34 minutes       5.0%
       512         1.4 hours         12 minutes      14.1%

In the first part every worker ends with the same five sums, starting with the 15 we computed by hand. Each sent 8 chunks, 1.6 gradients’ worth, as the count predicted.

In the second part thor-3 fails after two exchanges. thor-4, on its right, also completed two and stopped at the third, waiting for thor-3. thor-5 completed three, thor-1 four, and thor-2 five. Reading the workers in ring order from the failure, the counts are 2, 3, 4, 5: the stall moved one worker per exchange, as we reasoned. Each worker names the neighbour it was waiting for, and only one of the four names the machine that failed. A person who reads the report from thor-2 alone would suspect thor-1, which is healthy. Finding the worker that every chain of blame leads back to is the same counting exercise as chapter 4’s triage.

Animation 7.1 replays both runs on the ring, following chunk 0 in the first and the count of completed exchanges in the second.

The third part assumes that one machine fails every 30 days and that a checkpoint takes 50 seconds, which is 100 GB written at 2 GB per second. These are assumptions for the arithmetic, and the formula takes whatever numbers a real fleet measures.

A job on one machine should checkpoint every 268 minutes and loses 0.6 percent of its time. Our five Thors together fail every 144 hours, and the best interval is exactly two hours, at a cost of 1.4 percent. At 512 machines the job fails every 1.4 hours on average. It should checkpoint every 12 minutes, and 14.1 percent of its time goes to checkpoints and repeated work. The machines are no less reliable than before. There are more of them in one job, and this is why large training jobs invest in making checkpoints fast.

The complete program

Listing 7.11 The complete program in Go. code/go/allreduce/main.go

// Command allreduce runs one step of distributed training's communication:
// five workers each hold a gradient, and every worker has to end the step
// holding the sum of all five.
//
// The workers are goroutines connected in a ring by channels, and they run
// the ring all-reduce algorithm. The program runs the step three ways: with
// every worker healthy, with one worker failing part of the way through, and
// then as arithmetic, to find how often a job of a given size should save a
// checkpoint.
//
// Run it from code/go with:
//
//	go run ./allreduce
package main

import (
	"errors"
	"fmt"
	"math"
	"strings"
	"sync"
	"time"
)

// message carries one chunk of a gradient from a worker to its right-hand
// neighbour.
type message struct {
	// chunk is the index of the chunk within the gradient.
	chunk int
	// value is the chunk's contents: one number stands for the whole chunk.
	value int
}

// Worker is one machine's part in the step.
type Worker struct {
	// id is the worker's position in the ring, from 0.
	id int
	// gradient holds one value per chunk. It starts as this worker's own
	// gradient and ends as the sum over all workers.
	gradient []int
	// in receives from the left-hand neighbour, and out sends to the right.
	in  <-chan message
	out chan<- message
	// failAfter is the number of messages the worker exchanges before it
	// fails, or -1 for a worker that stays healthy.
	failAfter int
}

// errFailed is the result of the worker that the run makes fail.
var errFailed = errors.New("failed")

// patience is how long a worker waits for its neighbour before giving up.
const patience = 100 * time.Millisecond


// name returns the host name of the worker at a ring position.
func name(id int) string { return fmt.Sprintf("thor-%d", id+1) }

// mod returns a modulo n as a value from 0 to n-1, for negative a as well.
func mod(a, n int) int { return ((a % n) + n) % n }

// Run performs the worker's 2(n-1) exchanges. In each one it sends a chunk to
// the right and receives a chunk from the left. During the first n-1
// exchanges it adds what it receives to its own chunk. During the last n-1
// the chunks it receives are finished sums, and it stores them. Run returns
// the number of exchanges completed and the reason it stopped early, if any.
func (w *Worker) Run(n int) (int, error) {
	for step := range 2 * (n - 1) {
		if step == w.failAfter {
			return step, errFailed
		}

		// The chunk to send moves one place back around the gradient each
		// step, so after the first step it is the chunk just received.
		chunk := mod(w.id-step, n)
		w.out <- message{chunk, w.gradient[chunk]}
		reducing := step < n-1

		select {
		case m := <-w.in:
			if reducing {
				w.gradient[m.chunk] += m.value
			} else {
				w.gradient[m.chunk] = m.value
			}
		case <-time.After(patience):
			return step, fmt.Errorf("no chunk from %s within %v", name(mod(w.id-1, n)), patience)
		}
	}

	return 2 * (n - 1), nil
}


// result is what one worker reports when it stops.
type result struct {
	steps int
	err   error
}

// ring connects n workers in a circle, runs them all at once, and returns the
// workers and their results. The worker at position failing stops after
// failAfter exchanges; pass -1 for a run with no failure.
func ring(n, failing, failAfter int) ([]*Worker, []result) {
	// Each link holds every message a worker can send in one step, so a send
	// goes through even when the receiver has stopped.
	links := make([]chan message, n)

	for i := range links {
		links[i] = make(chan message, 2*(n-1))
	}

	workers := make([]*Worker, n)

	for i := range workers {
		gradient := make([]int, n)

		for chunk := range gradient {
			gradient[chunk] = i + 1 + 10*chunk
		}

		workers[i] = &Worker{id: i, gradient: gradient, in: links[i], out: links[mod(i+1, n)], failAfter: -1}
	}

	if failing >= 0 {
		workers[failing].failAfter = failAfter
	}

	results := make([]result, n)
	var wg sync.WaitGroup

	for i, w := range workers {
		wg.Go(func() {
			steps, err := w.Run(n)
			results[i] = result{steps, err}
		})
	}

	wg.Wait()

	return workers, results
}


// show formats a gradient as its values separated by spaces.
func show(gradient []int) string {
	return strings.Trim(fmt.Sprint(gradient), "[]")
}

// Costs and failure rates for the checkpoint arithmetic, in seconds.
const (
	// checkpointCost is the time one checkpoint takes to write.
	checkpointCost = 50.0
	// machineMTBF is the mean time between failures of one machine: 30 days.
	machineMTBF = 30 * 24 * 3600.0
)

// interval returns the time between checkpoints that loses the least work,
// by Young's formula: the square root of twice the checkpoint cost times the
// job's mean time between failures.
func interval(jobMTBF float64) float64 {
	return math.Sqrt(2 * checkpointCost * jobMTBF)
}


func main() {
	const n = 5

	fmt.Printf("ring all-reduce over %d workers, %d chunks each:\n", n, n)
	workers, _ := ring(n, -1, 0)

	for i := range n {
		own := make([]int, n)

		for chunk := range own {
			own[chunk] = i + 1 + 10*chunk
		}

		fmt.Printf("  %s starts with %-17s and ends with %s\n", name(i), show(own), show(workers[i].gradient))
	}

	sent := 2 * (n - 1)
	fmt.Printf("  each worker sent %d chunks, %.1f times its gradient; %d messages in all\n",
		sent, float64(sent)/n, sent*n)

	fmt.Printf("the same step, with %s failing after 2 of its %d exchanges:\n", name(2), sent)
	_, results := ring(n, 2, 2)

	for i, r := range results {
		fmt.Printf("  %s stopped after %d of %d: %v\n", name(i), r.steps, sent, r.err)
	}

	fmt.Println("checkpoint interval, for a 50 s checkpoint and one failure per machine every 30 days:")
	fmt.Println("  machines  job fails every  checkpoint every  time lost")

	for _, machines := range []int{1, 5, 64, 512} {
		jobMTBF := machineMTBF / float64(machines)
		every := interval(jobMTBF)
		lost := checkpointCost/every + every/(2*jobMTBF)
		fmt.Printf("  %8d  %10.1f hours  %9.0f minutes  %8.1f%%\n", machines, jobMTBF/3600, every/60, lost*100)
	}
}

Listing 7.12 The complete program in Rust. code/rust/allreduce/src/main.rs

//! Runs one step of distributed training's communication: five workers each
//! hold a gradient, and every worker has to end the step holding the sum of
//! all five.
//!
//! The workers are threads connected in a ring by channels, and they run the
//! ring all-reduce algorithm. The program runs the step three ways: with every
//! worker healthy, with one worker failing part of the way through, and then
//! as arithmetic, to find how often a job of a given size should save a
//! checkpoint.
//!
//! Run it from code/rust with:
//!
//! ```text
//! cargo run -p allreduce
//! ```

use std::sync::mpsc::{self, Receiver, Sender};
use std::thread;
use std::time::Duration;

/// Carries one chunk of a gradient from a worker to its right-hand neighbour.
struct Message {
    /// The index of the chunk within the gradient.
    chunk: usize,
    /// The chunk's contents: one number stands for the whole chunk.
    value: i64,
}

/// One machine's part in the step.
struct Worker {
    /// The worker's position in the ring, from 0.
    id: usize,
    /// One value per chunk. It starts as this worker's own gradient and ends
    /// as the sum over all workers.
    gradient: Vec<i64>,
    /// Receives from the left-hand neighbour.
    inbox: Receiver<Message>,
    /// Sends to the right-hand neighbour.
    out: Sender<Message>,
    /// The number of messages the worker exchanges before it fails, if the
    /// run makes it fail.
    fail_after: Option<usize>,
}

/// How long a worker waits for its neighbour before giving up.
const PATIENCE: Duration = Duration::from_millis(100);

/// Returns the host name of the worker at a ring position.
fn name(id: usize) -> String {
    format!("thor-{}", id + 1)
}

impl Worker {
    /// Performs the worker's 2(n-1) exchanges. In each one it sends a chunk to
    /// the right and receives a chunk from the left. During the first n-1
    /// exchanges it adds what it receives to its own chunk. During the last
    /// n-1 the chunks it receives are finished sums, and it stores them.
    /// Returns the worker with the number of exchanges completed and the
    /// reason it stopped early, if any.
    fn run(mut self, n: usize) -> (Self, usize, Option<String>) {
        for step in 0..2 * (n - 1) {
            if self.fail_after == Some(step) {
                return (self, step, Some("failed".to_owned()));
            }

            // The chunk to send moves one place back around the gradient each
            // step, so after the first step it is the chunk just received.
            // Adding 2n keeps the subtraction from going below zero.
            let chunk = (self.id + 2 * n - step) % n;
            // The right-hand neighbour may have stopped; its channel still accepts.
            let _ = self.out.send(Message {
                chunk,
                value: self.gradient[chunk],
            });
            let reducing = step < n - 1;

            match self.inbox.recv_timeout(PATIENCE) {
                Ok(m) if reducing => self.gradient[m.chunk] += m.value,
                Ok(m) => self.gradient[m.chunk] = m.value,
                Err(_) => {
                    let why = format!(
                        "no chunk from {} within {PATIENCE:?}",
                        name((self.id + n - 1) % n)
                    );

                    return (self, step, Some(why));
                }
            }
        }

        (self, 2 * (n - 1), None)
    }
}

/// Connects n workers in a circle, runs them all at once, and returns each
/// worker with its result. The worker named in `failing` stops after that
/// many exchanges.
fn ring(n: usize, failing: Option<(usize, usize)>) -> Vec<(Worker, usize, Option<String>)> {
    // One channel per link. `senders[i]` leads to worker i.
    let (senders, receivers): (Vec<_>, Vec<_>) = (0..n).map(|_| mpsc::channel()).unzip();
    let workers: Vec<Worker> = receivers
        .into_iter()
        .enumerate()
        .map(|(id, inbox)| Worker {
            id,
            gradient: own_gradient(id, n),
            inbox,
            out: senders[(id + 1) % n].clone(),
            fail_after: failing
                .filter(|(who, _)| *who == id)
                .map(|(_, after)| after),
        })
        .collect();

    let handles: Vec<_> = workers
        .into_iter()
        .map(|w| thread::spawn(move || w.run(n)))
        .collect();

    handles
        .into_iter()
        .map(|h| h.join().expect("a worker panicked"))
        .collect()
}

/// Returns the gradient a worker starts with: a different value in each chunk.
fn own_gradient(id: usize, n: usize) -> Vec<i64> {
    (0..n).map(|chunk| (id + 1 + 10 * chunk) as i64).collect()
}

/// Formats a gradient as its values separated by spaces.
fn show(gradient: &[i64]) -> String {
    gradient
        .iter()
        .map(i64::to_string)
        .collect::<Vec<_>>()
        .join(" ")
}

/// The time one checkpoint takes to write, in seconds.
const CHECKPOINT_COST: f64 = 50.0;
/// The mean time between failures of one machine, in seconds: 30 days.
const MACHINE_MTBF: f64 = 30.0 * 24.0 * 3600.0;

/// Returns the time between checkpoints that loses the least work, by Young's
/// formula: the square root of twice the checkpoint cost times the job's mean
/// time between failures.
fn interval(job_mtbf: f64) -> f64 {
    (2.0 * CHECKPOINT_COST * job_mtbf).sqrt()
}

fn main() {
    const N: usize = 5;

    println!("ring all-reduce over {N} workers, {N} chunks each:");

    for (worker, _, _) in ring(N, None) {
        println!(
            "  {} starts with {:<17} and ends with {}",
            name(worker.id),
            show(&own_gradient(worker.id, N)),
            show(&worker.gradient)
        );
    }

    let sent = 2 * (N - 1);
    println!(
        "  each worker sent {sent} chunks, {:.1} times its gradient; {} messages in all",
        sent as f64 / N as f64,
        sent * N
    );

    println!(
        "the same step, with {} failing after 2 of its {sent} exchanges:",
        name(2)
    );

    for (worker, steps, why) in ring(N, Some((2, 2))) {
        let why = why.unwrap_or_else(|| "finished".to_owned());
        println!(
            "  {} stopped after {steps} of {sent}: {why}",
            name(worker.id)
        );
    }

    println!(
        "checkpoint interval, for a 50 s checkpoint and one failure per machine every 30 days:"
    );
    println!("  machines  job fails every  checkpoint every  time lost");

    for machines in [1, 5, 64, 512] {
        let job_mtbf = MACHINE_MTBF / f64::from(machines);
        let every = interval(job_mtbf);
        let lost = CHECKPOINT_COST / every + every / (2.0 * job_mtbf);
        println!(
            "  {machines:8}  {:10.1} hours  {:9.0} minutes  {:8.1}%",
            job_mtbf / 3600.0,
            every / 60.0,
            lost * 100.0
        );
    }
}

Summary

  • Each step of training on several machines ends with an all-reduce: every worker contributes a gradient and every worker needs the sum.
  • A ring all-reduce cuts each gradient into N chunks and takes 2(N − 1) exchanges. Each worker sends a little under two gradients’ worth of data, and every link carries the same load.
  • Every chunk crosses every link, so the step runs at the speed of the slowest link.
  • A failed worker stalls its right-hand neighbour, and the stall moves around the ring one worker per exchange. The job restarts as a group from its last checkpoint.
  • The time between checkpoints that wastes the least is √(2CM), where C is the cost of a checkpoint and M is the job’s mean time between failures, which shrinks as the job grows.

References

  • NVIDIA, NCCL documentation: https://docs.nvidia.com/deeplearning/nccl/user-guide/docs/index.html
  • A. Gibiansky, “Bringing HPC Techniques to Deep Learning”, Baidu Research, 2017, which describes the ring all-reduce used here.
  • P. Patarasuk and X. Yuan, “Bandwidth optimal all-reduce algorithms for clusters of workstations”, Journal of Parallel and Distributed Computing, 2009.
  • J. W. Young, “A first order approximation to the optimum checkpoint interval”, Communications of the ACM, 17(9), 1974.
  • MPI Forum, MPI: A Message-Passing Interface Standard, chapter on collective communication: https://www.mpi-forum.org/docs/

8. Placing jobs on GPUs

Chapter 7 gave us two facts about a training job that spans several machines. It makes progress only when all of its workers are running, and its speed is set by the slowest network link among them. In this chapter, we will see what those facts ask of the scheduler, the component that chooses a machine for each pod.

We will first look at how the Kubernetes scheduler places one pod, and at what goes wrong when a job of several pods is placed that way. Then we will write gangsched, in Go and in Rust: a model that hands out the eight GPUs of our fleet one pod at a time, then one whole job at a time, and then chooses a job’s machines by the links between them. Finally we will map the model onto the pieces of a real cluster: Kueue, pod groups, and topology labels.

This chapter covers

  • how the scheduler places a single pod: filter, score, bind
  • two jobs that each hold part of what they need, and neither can run
  • gang scheduling: a job starts with all of its workers or stays in the queue
  • topology: choosing machines that share a fast link
  • packing small jobs into small groups, so large groups stay whole
  • Kueue, pod groups, and node labels, which do this on a real cluster

How one pod is placed

The scheduler is a controller in the sense of chapter 1. It watches for pods that have no machine yet, which it recognises by an empty spec.nodeName, and it handles them one at a time, in three stages.

  1. Filter. It discards every node that cannot run the pod: nodes without enough free CPU, memory, or nvidia.com/gpu, nodes that are cordoned, as in chapter 4, and nodes whose labels do not satisfy the pod’s nodeSelector, as in chapter 5.
  2. Score. It gives each remaining node a number by a set of rules, such as preferring nodes that already have the pod’s image.
  3. Bind. It writes the name of the best node into the pod’s spec.nodeName. The kubelet on that node sees the pod through its own watch and starts the containers.

Each pod is a separate decision. The scheduler has no notion that five pods belong together, and that is the source of the first problem.

Two jobs, each with part of what it needs

Suppose two training jobs arrive at the same moment, each made of five worker pods that ask for one GPU, and our fleet has eight GPUs. Ten pods wait, and the scheduler takes them in turn. The first eight are placed and the last two wait.

If the pods of the two jobs are interleaved in the queue, each job ends up with four of its five workers. Neither can run a single step, because an all-reduce needs every worker. Each holds four GPUs and waits for a fifth, and the only way a fifth can become free is for the other job to give one up. Nothing in the system will make either do so. All eight GPUs are allocated, the dashboards show a cluster in full use, and no training is happening. Figure 8.1 shows the state.

The remedy is to make the job the unit of the decision. A job is admitted when all of its workers can be placed, and all of them are placed together. Until then it holds nothing. This is called gang scheduling, the gang being the group of pods that start together or stay queued together.

With gang scheduling the same two jobs behave differently. The first is admitted and takes five GPUs. The second needs five, finds three, and waits without taking any. One job runs at full speed, the three free GPUs remain available to smaller jobs, and when the first job finishes the second starts.

Which machines

Admitting a job settles when it starts. Where it runs matters as much, because of the links.

Our eight machines are connected in three groups, which we will call domains. The five Thors share the 25 GbE switch on the shelf. The two Sparks share the 200 GbE cable on the desk. The Orin NX sits on the bench with 1 GbE. Between domains, the only path is the house network that all machines use for SSH and for reaching the API server, at 1 GbE. Figure 8.2 draws this as a tree.

Chapter 7 showed that a ring all-reduce runs at the speed of its slowest link and moves 2(N − 1)/N gradients per worker. For the 14 GB gradient of that chapter, two workers that share the Sparks’ cable need 14 / 25 = 0.56 seconds for each all-reduce. The same two workers placed in different domains exchange their gradients over the house network and need 14 / 0.125 = 112 seconds. The job is the same and the GPUs are equally fast. The placement makes it two hundred times slower.

So the placement rule has two parts.

  • Keep a job inside one domain when one can hold it. Then the job’s slowest link is the domain’s own.
  • Among the domains that can hold it, choose the one with the fewest free machines. A two-worker job fits on the desk and on the shelf. Putting it on the shelf leaves three Thors, and a later job that needs four of them no longer fits anywhere. Putting it on the desk uses the desk completely and leaves the shelf whole.

The second part is a defence against fragmentation: free GPUs scattered across domains in groups too small for the jobs that are waiting. The rule is a heuristic. It makes good choices for the jobs it has seen and cannot know what will arrive next.

Low-level design: gangsched

TypeHoldsRule it keeps
Nodename, domain, and the job that holds its GPUone GPU, held by at most one job
Linka label and a rate in GB per secondone per domain, plus the house network
Joba name and a number of workersevery worker needs one GPU
OperationDoes
podAtATime(fleet, jobs)gives each job one GPU in turn until none are left
admit(fleet, job, place)asks place for machines for every worker, and starts the job only if it gets them
firstFree(open, workers)a placer: the first free machines in name order
byTopology(open, workers)a placer: the tightest single domain that fits, or the first free machines if none does
exchangeSeconds(nodes)the time of one all-reduce over the slowest link among the nodes

Building gangsched

The fleet as the scheduler sees it

Each machine has one GPU, so a node is a name, a domain, and the job that holds its GPU. The machines are listed in name order, which is the order in which a listing of Node objects from the API server arrives.

Listing 8.1 Nodes, links, and jobs, in Go. code/go/gangsched/main.go

// Node is one machine with one GPU.
type Node struct {
	// Name is the machine's host name.
	Name string
	// Domain names the group of machines that share a fast link.
	Domain string
	// Job is the job that holds the machine's GPU, or "" when it is free.
	Job string
}

// Link is the network path a group of machines shares.
type Link struct {
	// Label is how the link is printed.
	Label string
	// GBps is what the link carries, in gigabytes per second.
	GBps float64
}

// links maps each domain to its link.
var links = map[string]Link{
	"shelf": {"the 25 GbE switch", 3.125},
	"desk":  {"the 200 GbE cable", 25},
	"bench": {"1 GbE", 0.125},
}

// houseNetwork is the link between machines in different domains.
var houseNetwork = Link{"the 1 GbE house network", 0.125}

// newFleet returns the machines in name order, all free.
func newFleet() []*Node {
	return []*Node{
		{Name: "orin-nx-1", Domain: "bench"},
		{Name: "spark-1", Domain: "desk"}, {Name: "spark-2", Domain: "desk"},
		{Name: "thor-1", Domain: "shelf"}, {Name: "thor-2", Domain: "shelf"}, {Name: "thor-3", Domain: "shelf"},
		{Name: "thor-4", Domain: "shelf"}, {Name: "thor-5", Domain: "shelf"},
	}
}

// Job is a group of workers that have to run at the same time, one GPU each.
type Job struct {
	Name    string
	Workers int
}

Listing 8.2 Nodes, links, and jobs, in Rust. code/rust/gangsched/src/main.rs

/// One machine with one GPU.
struct Node {
    /// The machine's host name.
    name: &'static str,
    /// The group of machines that share a fast link.
    domain: Domain,
    /// The job that holds the machine's GPU, if any.
    job: Option<&'static str>,
}

/// A group of machines that share a fast link.
#[derive(Clone, Copy, PartialEq)]
enum Domain {
    /// The Orin NX, alone on 1 GbE.
    Bench,
    /// The two Sparks, on a 200 GbE cable.
    Desk,
    /// The five Thors, on a 25 GbE switch.
    Shelf,
}

/// The network path a group of machines shares.
struct Link {
    /// How the link is printed.
    label: &'static str,
    /// What the link carries, in gigabytes per second.
    gbps: f64,
}

impl Domain {
    /// Returns the link the machines of this domain share.
    fn link(self) -> Link {
        match self {
            Domain::Shelf => Link {
                label: "the 25 GbE switch",
                gbps: 3.125,
            },
            Domain::Desk => Link {
                label: "the 200 GbE cable",
                gbps: 25.0,
            },
            Domain::Bench => Link {
                label: "1 GbE",
                gbps: 0.125,
            },
        }
    }
}

/// The link between machines in different domains.
const HOUSE_NETWORK: Link = Link {
    label: "the 1 GbE house network",
    gbps: 0.125,
};

/// Returns the machines in name order, all free.
fn new_fleet() -> Vec<Node> {
    [
        ("orin-nx-1", Domain::Bench),
        ("spark-1", Domain::Desk),
        ("spark-2", Domain::Desk),
        ("thor-1", Domain::Shelf),
        ("thor-2", Domain::Shelf),
        ("thor-3", Domain::Shelf),
        ("thor-4", Domain::Shelf),
        ("thor-5", Domain::Shelf),
    ]
    .into_iter()
    .map(|(name, domain)| Node {
        name,
        domain,
        job: None,
    })
    .collect()
}

/// A group of workers that have to run at the same time, one GPU each.
#[derive(Clone, Copy)]
struct Job {
    name: &'static str,
    workers: usize,
}

The Go version names a domain with a string and looks its link up in a map. The Rust version makes the domain an enum and the lookup a match, so a domain with no link is a compile error.

One pod at a time

podAtATime plays the scheduler without gangs. It goes round the jobs, giving each one free GPU per turn, until a whole round passes in which nobody could be given anything.

Listing 8.3 Handing out GPUs one pod at a time, in Go. code/go/gangsched/main.go

// podAtATime gives each job one GPU in turn until none are left, the way a
// scheduler that sees only single pods would, and reports what each job
// ended up holding.
func podAtATime(fleet []*Node, jobs []Job) {
	held := map[string][]*Node{}

	for progress := true; progress; {
		progress = false

		for _, job := range jobs {
			open := free(fleet)

			if len(held[job.Name]) < job.Workers && len(open) > 0 {
				open[0].Job = job.Name
				held[job.Name] = append(held[job.Name], open[0])
				progress = true
			}
		}
	}

	running := 0

	for _, job := range jobs {
		fmt.Printf("  %-8s holds %d of %d GPUs: %s\n", job.Name, len(held[job.Name]), job.Workers, names(held[job.Name]))

		if len(held[job.Name]) == job.Workers {
			running++
		}
	}

	fmt.Printf("  %d of %d GPUs are held and %d jobs are running\n", len(fleet)-len(free(fleet)), len(fleet), running)
}

Listing 8.4 Handing out GPUs one pod at a time, in Rust. code/rust/gangsched/src/main.rs

/// Gives each job one GPU in turn until none are left, the way a scheduler
/// that sees only single pods would, and reports what each job ended up
/// holding.
fn pod_at_a_time(mut fleet: Vec<Node>, jobs: &[Job]) {
    let held = |fleet: &[Node], job: &Job| -> Vec<usize> {
        (0..fleet.len())
            .filter(|&i| fleet[i].job == Some(job.name))
            .collect()
    };
    let mut progress = true;

    while progress {
        progress = false;

        for job in jobs {
            if held(&fleet, job).len() < job.workers
                && let Some(&first) = free(&fleet).first()
            {
                fleet[first].job = Some(job.name);
                progress = true;
            }
        }
    }

    let mut running = 0;

    for job in jobs {
        let mine = held(&fleet, job);
        println!(
            "  {:<8} holds {} of {} GPUs: {}",
            job.name,
            mine.len(),
            job.workers,
            names(&fleet, &mine)
        );

        if mine.len() == job.workers {
            running += 1;
        }
    }

    let taken = fleet.len() - free(&fleet).len();
    println!(
        "  {taken} of {} GPUs are held and {running} jobs are running",
        fleet.len()
    );
}

Two ways to choose machines

A placer is a function that takes the free machines and a number of workers and returns the machines to use, or nothing. Making it a function lets admit work with either policy.

firstFree takes the first free machines in the list. It is what we get when nobody has thought about placement.

byTopology groups the free machines by domain, keeps the domains with enough room, and takes the smallest of those. When no single domain can hold the job, it falls back to firstFree, and the job runs across domains at the speed of the house network. A real scheduler would let the job’s owner choose between that and waiting.

Listing 8.5 The two placers, in Go. code/go/gangsched/main.go

// A placer chooses machines for a job from the free ones, or returns nil when
// it cannot place every worker.
type placer func(open []*Node, workers int) []*Node

// firstFree takes the first free machines in name order.
func firstFree(open []*Node, workers int) []*Node {
	if len(open) < workers {
		return nil
	}

	return open[:workers]
}

// byTopology looks for one domain that can hold the whole job and, among
// those, takes the one with the fewest free machines, so that large domains
// stay whole for large jobs. A job that fits in no single domain is spread
// over the first free machines.
func byTopology(open []*Node, workers int) []*Node {
	byDomain := map[string][]*Node{}
	var order []string

	for _, n := range open {
		if byDomain[n.Domain] == nil {
			order = append(order, n.Domain)
		}

		byDomain[n.Domain] = append(byDomain[n.Domain], n)
	}

	best := ""

	for _, domain := range order {
		size := len(byDomain[domain])

		if size >= workers && (best == "" || size < len(byDomain[best])) {
			best = domain
		}
	}

	if best == "" {
		return firstFree(open, workers)
	}

	return byDomain[best][:workers]
}

Listing 8.6 The two placers, in Rust. code/rust/gangsched/src/main.rs

/// Chooses machines for a job from the free ones, or returns `None` when it
/// cannot place every worker.
type Placer = fn(fleet: &[Node], open: &[usize], workers: usize) -> Option<Vec<usize>>;

/// Takes the first free machines in name order.
fn first_free(_fleet: &[Node], open: &[usize], workers: usize) -> Option<Vec<usize>> {
    open.get(..workers).map(<[usize]>::to_vec)
}

/// Looks for one domain that can hold the whole job and, among those, takes
/// the one with the fewest free machines, so that large domains stay whole
/// for large jobs. A job that fits in no single domain is spread over the
/// first free machines.
fn by_topology(fleet: &[Node], open: &[usize], workers: usize) -> Option<Vec<usize>> {
    let in_domain = |domain: Domain| -> Vec<usize> {
        open.iter()
            .copied()
            .filter(|&i| fleet[i].domain == domain)
            .collect()
    };
    [Domain::Bench, Domain::Desk, Domain::Shelf]
        .into_iter()
        .map(in_domain)
        .filter(|members| members.len() >= workers)
        .min_by_key(Vec::len)
        .map(|members| members[..workers].to_vec())
        .or_else(|| first_free(fleet, open, workers))
}

What a placement costs

To compare placements we need a number. slowest finds the link a set of machines is limited by: their domain’s link if they share one, the house network if they do not. exchangeSeconds applies chapter 7’s count to it.

Listing 8.7 The slowest link and the time of one all-reduce, in Go. code/go/gangsched/main.go

// gradientGB is the size of the gradient every worker contributes per step.
const gradientGB = 14.0

// slowest returns the link a job's all-reduce is limited by: the domain's
// link when every machine is in one domain, and the house network otherwise.
func slowest(nodes []*Node) Link {
	for _, n := range nodes {
		if n.Domain != nodes[0].Domain {
			return houseNetwork
		}
	}

	return links[nodes[0].Domain]
}

// exchangeSeconds is the time one ring all-reduce takes: each worker sends
// 2(n-1)/n gradients over the slowest link.
func exchangeSeconds(nodes []*Node) float64 {
	n := float64(len(nodes))

	return 2 * (n - 1) / n * gradientGB / slowest(nodes).GBps
}

Listing 8.8 The slowest link and the time of one all-reduce, in Rust. code/rust/gangsched/src/main.rs

/// The size of the gradient every worker contributes per step, in gigabytes.
const GRADIENT_GB: f64 = 14.0;

/// Returns the link a job's all-reduce is limited by: the domain's link when
/// every machine is in one domain, and the house network otherwise.
fn slowest(fleet: &[Node], nodes: &[usize]) -> Link {
    let first = fleet[nodes[0]].domain;
    if nodes.iter().all(|&i| fleet[i].domain == first) {
        first.link()
    } else {
        HOUSE_NETWORK
    }
}

/// Returns the time one ring all-reduce takes: each worker sends 2(n-1)/n
/// gradients over the slowest link.
fn exchange_seconds(fleet: &[Node], nodes: &[usize]) -> f64 {
    let n = nodes.len() as f64;

    2.0 * (n - 1.0) / n * GRADIENT_GB / slowest(fleet, nodes).gbps
}

Admitting a whole job

admit is gang scheduling in a dozen lines. It asks the placer for machines for every worker. If the placer returns nothing, the job waits and the fleet is untouched. Only when every worker has a machine does admit mark any of them as taken.

Listing 8.9 All of a job's workers, or none, in Go. code/go/gangsched/main.go

// admit starts a job only when every one of its workers has a machine. It
// reports the placement and what the job's all-reduce will cost, and returns
// false when the job has to wait.
func admit(fleet []*Node, job Job, place placer) bool {
	open := free(fleet)
	nodes := place(open, job.Workers)

	if nodes == nil {
		fmt.Printf("  %-8s waits: it needs %d GPUs and %d are free\n", job.Name, job.Workers, len(open))

		return false
	}

	for _, n := range nodes {
		n.Job = job.Name
	}

	fmt.Printf("  %-8s runs on %s\n", job.Name, names(nodes))

	if len(nodes) > 1 {
		fmt.Printf("           over %s: %.1f s for each all-reduce\n", slowest(nodes).Label, exchangeSeconds(nodes))
	}

	return true
}

// release frees every GPU a job holds.
func release(fleet []*Node, job string) {
	for _, n := range fleet {
		if n.Job == job {
			n.Job = ""
		}
	}

	fmt.Printf("  %-8s finishes\n", job)
}

Listing 8.10 All of a job's workers, or none, in Rust. code/rust/gangsched/src/main.rs

/// Starts a job only when every one of its workers has a machine. It reports
/// the placement and what the job's all-reduce will cost, and returns false
/// when the job has to wait.
fn admit(fleet: &mut [Node], job: Job, place: Placer) -> bool {
    let open = free(fleet);
    let Some(nodes) = place(fleet, &open, job.workers) else {
        println!(
            "  {:<8} waits: it needs {} GPUs and {} are free",
            job.name,
            job.workers,
            open.len()
        );

        return false;
    };

    for &i in &nodes {
        fleet[i].job = Some(job.name);
    }

    println!("  {:<8} runs on {}", job.name, names(fleet, &nodes));

    if nodes.len() > 1 {
        let link = slowest(fleet, &nodes);
        println!(
            "           over {}: {:.1} s for each all-reduce",
            link.label,
            exchange_seconds(fleet, &nodes)
        );
    }

    true
}

/// Frees every GPU a job holds.
fn release(fleet: &mut [Node], job: &str) {
    for node in fleet.iter_mut().filter(|n| n.job == Some(job)) {
        node.job = None;
    }

    println!("  {job:<8} finishes");
}

The run

Listing 8.11 Four experiments on the same fleet, in Go. code/go/gangsched/main.go

func main() {
	two := []Job{{"train-a", 5}, {"train-b", 5}}

	fmt.Println("one pod at a time, two jobs of five workers:")
	podAtATime(newFleet(), two)

	fmt.Println("whole jobs at a time:")
	fleet := newFleet()
	admit(fleet, two[0], byTopology)
	admit(fleet, two[1], byTopology)
	release(fleet, "train-a")
	admit(fleet, two[1], byTopology)

	three := []Job{{"pair", 2}, {"quad", 4}, {"single", 1}}
	fmt.Println("three jobs, on the first free machines:")
	fleet = newFleet()

	for _, job := range three {
		admit(fleet, job, firstFree)
	}

	fmt.Println("the same three jobs, placed by topology:")
	fleet = newFleet()

	for _, job := range three {
		admit(fleet, job, byTopology)
	}
}

Listing 8.12 Four experiments on the same fleet, in Rust. code/rust/gangsched/src/main.rs

fn main() {
    let two = [
        Job {
            name: "train-a",
            workers: 5,
        },
        Job {
            name: "train-b",
            workers: 5,
        },
    ];

    println!("one pod at a time, two jobs of five workers:");
    pod_at_a_time(new_fleet(), &two);

    println!("whole jobs at a time:");
    let mut fleet = new_fleet();
    admit(&mut fleet, two[0], by_topology);
    admit(&mut fleet, two[1], by_topology);
    release(&mut fleet, "train-a");
    admit(&mut fleet, two[1], by_topology);

    let three = [
        Job {
            name: "pair",
            workers: 2,
        },
        Job {
            name: "quad",
            workers: 4,
        },
        Job {
            name: "single",
            workers: 1,
        },
    ];
    println!("three jobs, on the first free machines:");
    let mut fleet = new_fleet();

    for job in three {
        admit(&mut fleet, job, first_free);
    }

    println!("the same three jobs, placed by topology:");
    let mut fleet = new_fleet();

    for job in three {
        admit(&mut fleet, job, by_topology);
    }
}
$ go run ./gangsched
one pod at a time, two jobs of five workers:
  train-a  holds 4 of 5 GPUs: orin-nx-1 spark-2 thor-2 thor-4
  train-b  holds 4 of 5 GPUs: spark-1 thor-1 thor-3 thor-5
  8 of 8 GPUs are held and 0 jobs are running
whole jobs at a time:
  train-a  runs on thor-1 thor-2 thor-3 thor-4 thor-5
           over the 25 GbE switch: 7.2 s for each all-reduce
  train-b  waits: it needs 5 GPUs and 3 are free
  train-a  finishes
  train-b  runs on thor-1 thor-2 thor-3 thor-4 thor-5
           over the 25 GbE switch: 7.2 s for each all-reduce
three jobs, on the first free machines:
  pair     runs on orin-nx-1 spark-1
           over the 1 GbE house network: 112.0 s for each all-reduce
  quad     runs on spark-2 thor-1 thor-2 thor-3
           over the 1 GbE house network: 168.0 s for each all-reduce
  single   runs on thor-4
the same three jobs, placed by topology:
  pair     runs on spark-1 spark-2
           over the 200 GbE cable: 0.6 s for each all-reduce
  quad     runs on thor-1 thor-2 thor-3 thor-4
           over the 25 GbE switch: 6.7 s for each all-reduce
  single   runs on orin-nx-1

The first experiment is the deadlock of Figure 8.1: train-a and train-b each hold four GPUs, all eight are taken, and zero jobs run.

In the second, train-a is admitted whole and placed on the five Thors, the only domain that can hold five workers. Its all-reduce takes 7.2 seconds over one 25 GbE lane, the figure from chapter 7’s table. train-b waits with three GPUs free, and starts on the Thors when train-a finishes.

The third experiment places three jobs on the first free machines. pair gets orin-nx-1 and spark-1, two machines in different domains, and each of its all-reduces takes 112 seconds over the house network. quad gets the other Spark and three Thors and takes 168 seconds per all-reduce.

The fourth places the same jobs by topology. pair fits on the desk and on the shelf, and the desk is the tighter fit, so it gets the two Sparks and 0.6 seconds per all-reduce. quad fits only on the shelf and gets four Thors at 6.7 seconds. single fits on the bench and on the one Thor that is left. Both have a single free machine, the bench is listed first, and it gets the Orin NX, which leaves the last Thor free. Every job runs inside one domain, and the communication of pair is about two hundred times faster than in the third experiment.

Animation 8.1 plays the four experiments on the fleet.

On a real cluster

The Kubernetes scheduler places single pods, so gang scheduling and topology are added around it. Three pieces do the work.

Kueue is a job queueing controller from the Kubernetes project. It decides when a job may start. A job managed by Kueue is created with spec.suspend: true, a field of the built-in Job object that tells the Job controller to create no pods yet. Kueue keeps such jobs in a queue and holds a quota of each resource for each team, in an object called a ClusterQueue. When the whole job fits within the quota, Kueue admits it by setting suspend to false, and only then do its pods exist and reach the scheduler. This is the admit of our model with quota in place of free machines. Kueue’s quotas are also where fairness between teams lives: each team has a share of the GPUs it is guaranteed, and a queue may borrow what another team is not using.

A pod group handles the case where quota is available and the machines are not. The scheduler can be extended with plugins, and a coscheduling plugin lets a set of pods declare that they form a group with a minimum size. The scheduler finds a node for each pod as usual and then, before binding, holds the pod until enough members of its group have found nodes too. If they do not within a timeout, all of them are released and retried. This removes the partial placements of our first experiment at the level of individual machines.

Topology labels tell the scheduler which machines share a link. A node carries labels, as we saw in chapter 5, and a cluster’s administrator adds one for each level of the network: the rack, the switch, the block of racks. For our fleet one label is enough:

$ kubectl label node thor-1 thor-2 thor-3 thor-4 thor-5 gpucloud.dev/domain=shelf
$ kubectl label node spark-1 spark-2 gpucloud.dev/domain=desk
$ kubectl label node orin-nx-1 gpucloud.dev/domain=bench

A job then asks for all of its pods to land on nodes with the same value of that label. Kueue’s topology-aware scheduling reads the levels from an object that lists the label keys from widest to narrowest, and picks the narrowest level whose free capacity holds the job, which is the rule byTopology applies to our three domains.

Health and scheduling meet here. A gang placed on five Thors depends on all five. If chapter 4’s controller cordons thor-4 while a five-worker job is queued, the shelf can hold only four workers and the job no longer fits in any domain. The scheduler needs the health controller’s verdicts as input, and the health controller’s disruption budget should count a draining machine against every gang that needs it.

The complete program

Listing 8.13 The complete program in Go. code/go/gangsched/main.go

// Command gangsched models two decisions a scheduler makes for a training job
// that needs several GPUs at once: whether to start the job, and which
// machines to give it.
//
// The fleet is the eight machines of chapter 4, each with one GPU. The
// program first hands out GPUs one pod at a time and shows two jobs blocking
// each other. It then admits whole jobs, and finally compares two ways of
// choosing a job's machines by the network link the job would have to use.
//
// Run it from code/go with:
//
//	go run ./gangsched
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"strings"
)

// Node is one machine with one GPU.
type Node struct {
	// Name is the machine's host name.
	Name string
	// Domain names the group of machines that share a fast link.
	Domain string
	// Job is the job that holds the machine's GPU, or "" when it is free.
	Job string
}

// Link is the network path a group of machines shares.
type Link struct {
	// Label is how the link is printed.
	Label string
	// GBps is what the link carries, in gigabytes per second.
	GBps float64
}

// links maps each domain to its link.
var links = map[string]Link{
	"shelf": {"the 25 GbE switch", 3.125},
	"desk":  {"the 200 GbE cable", 25},
	"bench": {"1 GbE", 0.125},
}

// houseNetwork is the link between machines in different domains.
var houseNetwork = Link{"the 1 GbE house network", 0.125}

// newFleet returns the machines in name order, all free.
func newFleet() []*Node {
	return []*Node{
		{Name: "orin-nx-1", Domain: "bench"},
		{Name: "spark-1", Domain: "desk"}, {Name: "spark-2", Domain: "desk"},
		{Name: "thor-1", Domain: "shelf"}, {Name: "thor-2", Domain: "shelf"}, {Name: "thor-3", Domain: "shelf"},
		{Name: "thor-4", Domain: "shelf"}, {Name: "thor-5", Domain: "shelf"},
	}
}

// Job is a group of workers that have to run at the same time, one GPU each.
type Job struct {
	Name    string
	Workers int
}


// free returns the machines whose GPU nobody holds, in name order.
func free(fleet []*Node) []*Node {
	var out []*Node

	for _, n := range fleet {
		if n.Job == "" {
			out = append(out, n)
		}
	}

	return out
}

// names joins the names of some machines with spaces.
func names(nodes []*Node) string {
	parts := make([]string, len(nodes))

	for i, n := range nodes {
		parts[i] = n.Name
	}

	return strings.Join(parts, " ")
}

// podAtATime gives each job one GPU in turn until none are left, the way a
// scheduler that sees only single pods would, and reports what each job
// ended up holding.
func podAtATime(fleet []*Node, jobs []Job) {
	held := map[string][]*Node{}

	for progress := true; progress; {
		progress = false

		for _, job := range jobs {
			open := free(fleet)

			if len(held[job.Name]) < job.Workers && len(open) > 0 {
				open[0].Job = job.Name
				held[job.Name] = append(held[job.Name], open[0])
				progress = true
			}
		}
	}

	running := 0

	for _, job := range jobs {
		fmt.Printf("  %-8s holds %d of %d GPUs: %s\n", job.Name, len(held[job.Name]), job.Workers, names(held[job.Name]))

		if len(held[job.Name]) == job.Workers {
			running++
		}
	}

	fmt.Printf("  %d of %d GPUs are held and %d jobs are running\n", len(fleet)-len(free(fleet)), len(fleet), running)
}


// A placer chooses machines for a job from the free ones, or returns nil when
// it cannot place every worker.
type placer func(open []*Node, workers int) []*Node

// firstFree takes the first free machines in name order.
func firstFree(open []*Node, workers int) []*Node {
	if len(open) < workers {
		return nil
	}

	return open[:workers]
}

// byTopology looks for one domain that can hold the whole job and, among
// those, takes the one with the fewest free machines, so that large domains
// stay whole for large jobs. A job that fits in no single domain is spread
// over the first free machines.
func byTopology(open []*Node, workers int) []*Node {
	byDomain := map[string][]*Node{}
	var order []string

	for _, n := range open {
		if byDomain[n.Domain] == nil {
			order = append(order, n.Domain)
		}

		byDomain[n.Domain] = append(byDomain[n.Domain], n)
	}

	best := ""

	for _, domain := range order {
		size := len(byDomain[domain])

		if size >= workers && (best == "" || size < len(byDomain[best])) {
			best = domain
		}
	}

	if best == "" {
		return firstFree(open, workers)
	}

	return byDomain[best][:workers]
}


// gradientGB is the size of the gradient every worker contributes per step.
const gradientGB = 14.0

// slowest returns the link a job's all-reduce is limited by: the domain's
// link when every machine is in one domain, and the house network otherwise.
func slowest(nodes []*Node) Link {
	for _, n := range nodes {
		if n.Domain != nodes[0].Domain {
			return houseNetwork
		}
	}

	return links[nodes[0].Domain]
}

// exchangeSeconds is the time one ring all-reduce takes: each worker sends
// 2(n-1)/n gradients over the slowest link.
func exchangeSeconds(nodes []*Node) float64 {
	n := float64(len(nodes))

	return 2 * (n - 1) / n * gradientGB / slowest(nodes).GBps
}


// admit starts a job only when every one of its workers has a machine. It
// reports the placement and what the job's all-reduce will cost, and returns
// false when the job has to wait.
func admit(fleet []*Node, job Job, place placer) bool {
	open := free(fleet)
	nodes := place(open, job.Workers)

	if nodes == nil {
		fmt.Printf("  %-8s waits: it needs %d GPUs and %d are free\n", job.Name, job.Workers, len(open))

		return false
	}

	for _, n := range nodes {
		n.Job = job.Name
	}

	fmt.Printf("  %-8s runs on %s\n", job.Name, names(nodes))

	if len(nodes) > 1 {
		fmt.Printf("           over %s: %.1f s for each all-reduce\n", slowest(nodes).Label, exchangeSeconds(nodes))
	}

	return true
}

// release frees every GPU a job holds.
func release(fleet []*Node, job string) {
	for _, n := range fleet {
		if n.Job == job {
			n.Job = ""
		}
	}

	fmt.Printf("  %-8s finishes\n", job)
}


func main() {
	two := []Job{{"train-a", 5}, {"train-b", 5}}

	fmt.Println("one pod at a time, two jobs of five workers:")
	podAtATime(newFleet(), two)

	fmt.Println("whole jobs at a time:")
	fleet := newFleet()
	admit(fleet, two[0], byTopology)
	admit(fleet, two[1], byTopology)
	release(fleet, "train-a")
	admit(fleet, two[1], byTopology)

	three := []Job{{"pair", 2}, {"quad", 4}, {"single", 1}}
	fmt.Println("three jobs, on the first free machines:")
	fleet = newFleet()

	for _, job := range three {
		admit(fleet, job, firstFree)
	}

	fmt.Println("the same three jobs, placed by topology:")
	fleet = newFleet()

	for _, job := range three {
		admit(fleet, job, byTopology)
	}
}

Listing 8.14 The complete program in Rust. code/rust/gangsched/src/main.rs

//! Models two decisions a scheduler makes for a training job that needs
//! several GPUs at once: whether to start the job, and which machines to give
//! it.
//!
//! The fleet is the eight machines of chapter 4, each with one GPU. The
//! program first hands out GPUs one pod at a time and shows two jobs blocking
//! each other. It then admits whole jobs, and finally compares two ways of
//! choosing a job's machines by the network link the job would have to use.
//!
//! Run it from code/rust with:
//!
//! ```text
//! cargo run -p gangsched
//! ```

/// One machine with one GPU.
struct Node {
    /// The machine's host name.
    name: &'static str,
    /// The group of machines that share a fast link.
    domain: Domain,
    /// The job that holds the machine's GPU, if any.
    job: Option<&'static str>,
}

/// A group of machines that share a fast link.
#[derive(Clone, Copy, PartialEq)]
enum Domain {
    /// The Orin NX, alone on 1 GbE.
    Bench,
    /// The two Sparks, on a 200 GbE cable.
    Desk,
