    /// The five Thors, on a 25 GbE switch.
    Shelf,
}

/// The network path a group of machines shares.
struct Link {
    /// How the link is printed.
    label: &'static str,
    /// What the link carries, in gigabytes per second.
    gbps: f64,
}

impl Domain {
    /// Returns the link the machines of this domain share.
    fn link(self) -> Link {
        match self {
            Domain::Shelf => Link {
                label: "the 25 GbE switch",
                gbps: 3.125,
            },
            Domain::Desk => Link {
                label: "the 200 GbE cable",
                gbps: 25.0,
            },
            Domain::Bench => Link {
                label: "1 GbE",
                gbps: 0.125,
            },
        }
    }
}

/// The link between machines in different domains.
const HOUSE_NETWORK: Link = Link {
    label: "the 1 GbE house network",
    gbps: 0.125,
};

/// Returns the machines in name order, all free.
fn new_fleet() -> Vec<Node> {
    [
        ("orin-nx-1", Domain::Bench),
        ("spark-1", Domain::Desk),
        ("spark-2", Domain::Desk),
        ("thor-1", Domain::Shelf),
        ("thor-2", Domain::Shelf),
        ("thor-3", Domain::Shelf),
        ("thor-4", Domain::Shelf),
        ("thor-5", Domain::Shelf),
    ]
    .into_iter()
    .map(|(name, domain)| Node {
        name,
        domain,
        job: None,
    })
    .collect()
}

/// A group of workers that have to run at the same time, one GPU each.
#[derive(Clone, Copy)]
struct Job {
    name: &'static str,
    workers: usize,
}

/// Returns the indexes of the machines whose GPU nobody holds, in name order.
fn free(fleet: &[Node]) -> Vec<usize> {
    (0..fleet.len())
        .filter(|&i| fleet[i].job.is_none())
        .collect()
}

/// Joins the names of some machines with spaces.
fn names(fleet: &[Node], nodes: &[usize]) -> String {
    nodes
        .iter()
        .map(|&i| fleet[i].name)
        .collect::<Vec<_>>()
        .join(" ")
}

/// Gives each job one GPU in turn until none are left, the way a scheduler
/// that sees only single pods would, and reports what each job ended up
/// holding.
fn pod_at_a_time(mut fleet: Vec<Node>, jobs: &[Job]) {
    let held = |fleet: &[Node], job: &Job| -> Vec<usize> {
        (0..fleet.len())
            .filter(|&i| fleet[i].job == Some(job.name))
            .collect()
    };
    let mut progress = true;

    while progress {
        progress = false;

        for job in jobs {
            if held(&fleet, job).len() < job.workers
                && let Some(&first) = free(&fleet).first()
            {
                fleet[first].job = Some(job.name);
                progress = true;
            }
        }
    }

    let mut running = 0;

    for job in jobs {
        let mine = held(&fleet, job);
        println!(
            "  {:<8} holds {} of {} GPUs: {}",
            job.name,
            mine.len(),
            job.workers,
            names(&fleet, &mine)
        );

        if mine.len() == job.workers {
            running += 1;
        }
    }

    let taken = fleet.len() - free(&fleet).len();
    println!(
        "  {taken} of {} GPUs are held and {running} jobs are running",
        fleet.len()
    );
}

/// Chooses machines for a job from the free ones, or returns `None` when it
/// cannot place every worker.
type Placer = fn(fleet: &[Node], open: &[usize], workers: usize) -> Option<Vec<usize>>;

/// Takes the first free machines in name order.
fn first_free(_fleet: &[Node], open: &[usize], workers: usize) -> Option<Vec<usize>> {
    open.get(..workers).map(<[usize]>::to_vec)
}

/// Looks for one domain that can hold the whole job and, among those, takes
/// the one with the fewest free machines, so that large domains stay whole
/// for large jobs. A job that fits in no single domain is spread over the
/// first free machines.
fn by_topology(fleet: &[Node], open: &[usize], workers: usize) -> Option<Vec<usize>> {
    let in_domain = |domain: Domain| -> Vec<usize> {
        open.iter()
            .copied()
            .filter(|&i| fleet[i].domain == domain)
            .collect()
    };
    [Domain::Bench, Domain::Desk, Domain::Shelf]
        .into_iter()
        .map(in_domain)
        .filter(|members| members.len() >= workers)
        .min_by_key(Vec::len)
        .map(|members| members[..workers].to_vec())
        .or_else(|| first_free(fleet, open, workers))
}

/// The size of the gradient every worker contributes per step, in gigabytes.
const GRADIENT_GB: f64 = 14.0;

/// Returns the link a job's all-reduce is limited by: the domain's link when
/// every machine is in one domain, and the house network otherwise.
fn slowest(fleet: &[Node], nodes: &[usize]) -> Link {
    let first = fleet[nodes[0]].domain;
    if nodes.iter().all(|&i| fleet[i].domain == first) {
        first.link()
    } else {
        HOUSE_NETWORK
    }
}

/// Returns the time one ring all-reduce takes: each worker sends 2(n-1)/n
/// gradients over the slowest link.
fn exchange_seconds(fleet: &[Node], nodes: &[usize]) -> f64 {
    let n = nodes.len() as f64;

    2.0 * (n - 1.0) / n * GRADIENT_GB / slowest(fleet, nodes).gbps
}

/// Starts a job only when every one of its workers has a machine. It reports
/// the placement and what the job's all-reduce will cost, and returns false
/// when the job has to wait.
fn admit(fleet: &mut [Node], job: Job, place: Placer) -> bool {
    let open = free(fleet);
    let Some(nodes) = place(fleet, &open, job.workers) else {
        println!(
            "  {:<8} waits: it needs {} GPUs and {} are free",
            job.name,
            job.workers,
            open.len()
        );

        return false;
    };

    for &i in &nodes {
        fleet[i].job = Some(job.name);
    }

    println!("  {:<8} runs on {}", job.name, names(fleet, &nodes));

    if nodes.len() > 1 {
        let link = slowest(fleet, &nodes);
        println!(
            "           over {}: {:.1} s for each all-reduce",
            link.label,
            exchange_seconds(fleet, &nodes)
        );
    }

    true
}

/// Frees every GPU a job holds.
fn release(fleet: &mut [Node], job: &str) {
    for node in fleet.iter_mut().filter(|n| n.job == Some(job)) {
        node.job = None;
    }

    println!("  {job:<8} finishes");
}

fn main() {
    let two = [
        Job {
            name: "train-a",
            workers: 5,
        },
        Job {
            name: "train-b",
            workers: 5,
        },
    ];

    println!("one pod at a time, two jobs of five workers:");
    pod_at_a_time(new_fleet(), &two);

    println!("whole jobs at a time:");
    let mut fleet = new_fleet();
    admit(&mut fleet, two[0], by_topology);
    admit(&mut fleet, two[1], by_topology);
    release(&mut fleet, "train-a");
    admit(&mut fleet, two[1], by_topology);

    let three = [
        Job {
            name: "pair",
            workers: 2,
        },
        Job {
            name: "quad",
            workers: 4,
        },
        Job {
            name: "single",
            workers: 1,
        },
    ];
    println!("three jobs, on the first free machines:");
    let mut fleet = new_fleet();

    for job in three {
        admit(&mut fleet, job, first_free);
    }

    println!("the same three jobs, placed by topology:");
    let mut fleet = new_fleet();

    for job in three {
        admit(&mut fleet, job, by_topology);
    }
}

Summary

  • The scheduler places one pod at a time by filtering nodes, scoring them, and binding the pod to the best one.
  • Jobs placed one pod at a time can each end up with part of what they need, holding every GPU while none of them runs.
  • Gang scheduling admits a job only when all of its workers can be placed, and a job that does not fit holds nothing while it waits.
  • A job’s workers should share one network domain, since its all-reduce runs at the speed of the slowest link among them.
  • Among the domains that fit, the tightest one keeps larger domains whole for larger jobs.
  • On a cluster, Kueue admits whole jobs against a quota, a pod group keeps the scheduler from binding part of a gang, and node labels describe the topology.

References

  • Kubernetes, Scheduling framework: https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/scheduling-eviction/scheduling-framework/
  • Kueue documentation: https://kueue.sigs.k8s.io/docs/overview/
  • Kueue, Topology aware scheduling: https://kueue.sigs.k8s.io/docs/concepts/topology_aware_scheduling/
  • Kubernetes scheduler plugins, Coscheduling: https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/scheduler-plugins/tree/master/pkg/coscheduling
  • Kubernetes, Jobs, suspending a Job: https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/workloads/controllers/job/#suspending-a-job

9. Counting across replicas

A GPU platform counts all day. It counts the GPU time each team’s jobs have used, so that the time can be billed or shared out fairly, and it counts the GPUs each team has in use right now, so that a quota can be enforced. Inside one cluster, counting is not hard: the API server is the single place every write passes through. A platform that spans several sites has several places where things happen, and they cannot always reach each other.

In this chapter, we will count across three replicas of a usage service, one beside each group of machines in our fleet. We will first see why the obvious ways of keeping the replicas in step lose counts or count twice. Then we will write counter, in Go and in Rust, which builds a count that survives a network partition, extends it to a count that goes down as well as up, and finally shows the one thing such a count cannot do, which is to enforce a limit, and what does that job in its place.

This chapter covers

  • one authority for a count, and what it costs when the network splits
  • why “keep the larger number” and “add the other’s number” both go wrong
  • a grow-only counter: one entry per replica, merged by taking the larger
  • the three properties of that merge, and what each one buys
  • a counter that goes up and down, built from two that only grow
  • a quota across replicas: why a merged count overshoots, and shares that do not

One authority, or several

Suppose each group of machines in our fleet has a small service beside it that records usage: one on the shelf with the Thors, one on the desk with the Sparks, one on the bench with the Orin NX. Jobs report to the nearest one. Each of these is a replica of the usage service, and all three are meant to hold the same totals.

The first design gives the count a single home. One replica, or a database that all three write to, holds the number, and every report becomes a write to it. etcd is a database of this kind: it stores each write on a majority of its members before it acknowledges the write, so that all members agree on the order of writes. The count is always exact.

The cost appears when the network between the sites fails. Our three groups are joined only by the house network. If it goes down, the bench is alone. A replica that cannot reach a majority has two choices, and both are bad for a usage counter:

  • It can refuse reports until the network returns. The jobs near it keep running, and their usage for that period is not recorded.
  • It can accept reports into a count of its own. Now there are two counts, and the design gave us no rule for combining them afterwards.

A split like this is called a network partition, and the choice is a general one. During a partition, a replica that is cut off can stay consistent, by refusing work it cannot coordinate, or stay available, by doing the work alone. It cannot do both. For a count of usage we want availability: a report should be accepted wherever it arrives. So we need the thing the second choice lacked, a rule for combining counts that were kept apart.

Two rules that do not work

Let each replica keep one number, its total. After some jobs have reported, the shelf holds 300 GPU-seconds, the desk 120, and the bench 40. The true total is 460. Now the replicas exchange numbers and each has to decide what its total should be.

Keep the larger. Each replica replaces its number with the larger of its own and the one it received. All three end at 300. The 160 GPU-seconds recorded at the desk and the bench are gone, because the rule treats two different counts as two versions of the same count.

Add the other’s number. Each replica adds what it receives to its own. After the shelf and the desk exchange once, both say 420, which is right for those two. But exchanges repeat, and messages are sometimes delivered twice. When the desk’s 120 arrives at the shelf a second time, the shelf says 540. A rule based on adding counts every report again each time the replicas talk.

Both rules fail for the same reason. A single number does not say whose reports it contains, so a replica that receives one cannot tell which part of it is news.

One entry per replica

The fix is to keep that information. Each replica holds a row with one entry for every replica, itself included. Two rules govern the row:

  • A replica records a report by adding to its own entry. It leaves every other entry as it received it.
  • When a replica receives another’s row, it goes through the entries one by one and keeps the larger of its own value and the received one.

The count is the sum of the row.

“Keep the larger” failed for totals and works here, and the difference is what the two numbers being compared mean. An entry is written by exactly one replica and only ever increases. So when two values of the same entry differ, they really are an older and a newer version of the same thing, and the larger is the newer.

Let us run the earlier example. After the first reports, written as rows of (shelf, desk, bench):

  • the shelf holds (300, 0, 0),
  • the desk holds (0, 120, 0),
  • the bench holds (0, 0, 40).

The shelf receives the desk’s row and takes the larger in each position: (300, 120, 0), which sums to 420. If the same row arrives again, nothing changes, because 120 is not larger than 120. When the bench’s row arrives, the shelf holds (300, 120, 40), and the sum is 460.

This structure is called a grow-only counter, or G-counter. Figure 9.1 follows three of them through a partition.

What the merge guarantees

The merge has three properties, and each one removes a requirement from the network between the replicas.

  • Merging the same row twice gives the same result as merging it once. So a message may be delivered twice, and replicas may exchange rows as often as they like.
  • Merging row A and then row B gives the same result as B and then A. So messages may arrive in any order.
  • Merging A with the result of merging B and C gives the same result as merging A and B first and then C. So a row may travel through other replicas on its way, picking up their entries, and nothing is lost or doubled.

Together they mean that any two replicas that have received the same reports, by any route, hold the same row. Nothing has to be coordinated, and a replica that was cut off for an hour catches up with one exchange. Data structures with a merge of this kind are called conflict-free replicated data types (CRDTs).

Counting down

A count of GPUs in use has to go down when a job ends, and “keep the larger” cannot represent a decrease: a replica that lowered its entry would have the old, larger value merged straight back in.

The way round this is to use two grow-only counters. One counts every increase and the other counts every decrease, and the value is the first minus the second. Both halves only grow, so both merge safely. This is a PN-counter, for positive and negative.

What a merged count cannot do

A quota is a limit: this team may use at most six GPUs at once. Enforcing it means refusing a job when the count is at the limit, and here the merged count lets us down.

Suppose the shelf and the desk both know that four of the six GPUs are in use, and then lose contact. A two-GPU job arrives at each. Each replica looks at its own count, sees four in use, and admits the job, correctly by everything it knows. When contact returns and the counts merge, eight GPUs are in use against a quota of six.

No cleverer merge fixes this. A limit is a statement about the sum of what all replicas have done, and a replica that is cut off cannot know what the others are doing at this moment. What we can do is divide the limit beforehand, while the replicas can still talk. Each replica is given a share of the quota and admits jobs only out of its own share. The shares add up to the quota, so whatever the replicas do while apart, the total stays within it. When they are in contact, a replica with unused share can hand some to one that has run out.

The price is that a job can be refused while capacity exists. If the shelf has one GPU of its share left and the desk has one, a two-GPU job is refused at both, even though two GPUs are free in total, until the replicas can talk and move a share across. The partition forces a choice here too, and for a limit we chose to refuse.

Low-level design: counter

TypeHoldsRule it keeps
GCounterone entry per replicaan entry is increased only by its own replica; Merge keeps the larger value of each entry
PNCountertwo GCounters, Up and Downthe value is Up minus Down; both halves merge independently
OperationDoes
Add(replica, n)records n at that replica’s entry
Merge(other)combines another replica’s state into this one
Value()the current count

Building counter

The grow-only counter

A GCounter is an array with one entry per replica. Add touches one entry, Merge takes the larger value of each, and Value sums them.

Listing 9.1 The grow-only counter, in Go. code/go/counter/main.go

// GCounter is a count that only grows. It keeps one entry per replica, and
// each replica adds only to its own entry.
type GCounter [replicas]int

// Add records n more at the given replica.
func (c *GCounter) Add(replica, n int) { c[replica] += n }

// Merge takes, entry by entry, the larger of this counter's value and the
// other's. Merging is safe to repeat and to do in any order.
func (c *GCounter) Merge(other GCounter) {
	for i := range c {
		c[i] = max(c[i], other[i])
	}
}

// Value is the count: the sum of every replica's entry.
func (c GCounter) Value() int {
	total := 0

	for _, n := range c {
		total += n
	}

	return total
}

Listing 9.2 The grow-only counter, in Rust. code/rust/counter/src/main.rs

/// A count that only grows. It keeps one entry per replica, and each replica
/// adds only to its own entry.
#[derive(Clone, Copy, Default)]
struct GCounter([i64; REPLICAS]);

impl GCounter {
    /// Records n more at the given replica.
    fn add(&mut self, replica: usize, n: i64) {
        self.0[replica] += n;
    }

    /// Takes, entry by entry, the larger of this counter's value and the
    /// other's. Merging is safe to repeat and to do in any order.
    fn merge(&mut self, other: GCounter) {
        for (mine, theirs) in self.0.iter_mut().zip(other.0) {
            *mine = (*mine).max(theirs);
        }
    }

    /// The count: the sum of every replica's entry.
    fn value(self) -> i64 {
        self.0.iter().sum()
    }
}

The counter is a fixed-size array in both languages, which makes it a plain value: assigning it copies it, and “send my row to another replica” in the program is passing the array to Merge.

The counter that goes down

PNCounter holds two grow-only counters. A positive change goes into Up, a negative one into Down with its sign removed.

Listing 9.3 The up-and-down counter, in Go. code/go/counter/main.go

// PNCounter is a count that goes up and down, built from two counters that
// only grow: one for the increments and one for the decrements.
type PNCounter struct {
	Up, Down GCounter
}

// Add records a change of n, which may be negative, at the given replica.
func (c *PNCounter) Add(replica, n int) {
	if n >= 0 {
		c.Up.Add(replica, n)
	} else {
		c.Down.Add(replica, -n)
	}
}

// Merge merges both halves.
func (c *PNCounter) Merge(other PNCounter) {
	c.Up.Merge(other.Up)
	c.Down.Merge(other.Down)
}

// Value is the increments minus the decrements.
func (c PNCounter) Value() int { return c.Up.Value() - c.Down.Value() }

Listing 9.4 The up-and-down counter, in Rust. code/rust/counter/src/main.rs

/// A count that goes up and down, built from two counters that only grow: one
/// for the increments and one for the decrements.
#[derive(Clone, Copy, Default)]
struct PnCounter {
    up: GCounter,
    down: GCounter,
}

impl PnCounter {
    /// Records a change of n, which may be negative, at the given replica.
    fn add(&mut self, replica: usize, n: i64) {
        if n >= 0 {
            self.up.add(replica, n);
        } else {
            self.down.add(replica, -n);
        }
    }

    /// Merges both halves.
    fn merge(&mut self, other: PnCounter) {
        self.up.merge(other.up);
        self.down.merge(other.down);
    }

    /// The increments minus the decrements.
    fn value(self) -> i64 {
        self.up.value() - self.down.value()
    }
}

One number per replica

The first experiment is the rule that fails: one total per replica, synchronised by keeping the larger.

Listing 9.5 Keeping the larger of two totals, in Go. code/go/counter/main.go

// oneNumber counts with a single number per replica and synchronises by
// keeping the larger of two numbers.
func oneNumber() {
	totals := [replicas]int{}
	adds := [replicas]int{300, 120, 40}
	sum := 0

	for r, n := range adds {
		totals[r] += n
		sum += n
		fmt.Printf("  %-5s records %d GPU-seconds\n", names[r], n)
	}

	largest := max(totals[shelf], totals[desk], totals[bench])
	fmt.Printf("  after synchronising, every replica says %d; the jobs used %d\n", largest, sum)
}

Listing 9.6 Keeping the larger of two totals, in Rust. code/rust/counter/src/main.rs

/// Counts with a single number per replica and synchronises by keeping the
/// larger of two numbers.
fn one_number() {
    let adds = [300, 120, 40];

    for (name, n) in NAMES.iter().zip(adds) {
        println!("  {name:<5} records {n} GPU-seconds");
    }

    let largest = adds.iter().max().expect("three replicas");
    let sum: i64 = adds.iter().sum();
    println!("  after synchronising, every replica says {largest}; the jobs used {sum}");
}

Through a partition

The second experiment gives each replica a GCounter. The bench is cut off, the shelf and the desk merge with each other, and reports keep arriving on both sides. After the partition the rows are merged in an order chosen to be untidy, with one merge repeated.

Listing 9.7 Grow-only counters, a partition, and an untidy recovery, in Go. code/go/counter/main.go

// growOnly counts the same usage with a GCounter at each replica, through a
// partition that cuts the bench off from the other two.
func growOnly() {
	var at [replicas]GCounter

	for r, n := range [replicas]int{300, 120, 40} {
		at[r].Add(r, n)
	}

	fmt.Println("  the bench is cut off; shelf and desk exchange counters")
	at[shelf].Merge(at[desk])
	at[desk].Merge(at[shelf])
	at[shelf].Add(shelf, 60)
	at[bench].Add(bench, 25)

	for r := range at {
		show(r, at[r])
	}

	fmt.Println("  the network heals; counters are exchanged in no particular order, some twice")
	at[desk].Merge(at[bench])
	at[shelf].Merge(at[desk])
	at[bench].Merge(at[shelf])
	at[desk].Merge(at[shelf])
	at[desk].Merge(at[shelf])

	for r := range at {
		show(r, at[r])
	}
}

Listing 9.8 Grow-only counters, a partition, and an untidy recovery, in Rust. code/rust/counter/src/main.rs

/// Counts the same usage with a `GCounter` at each replica, through a
/// partition that cuts the bench off from the other two.
fn grow_only() {
    let mut at = [GCounter::default(); REPLICAS];

    for (r, n) in [300, 120, 40].into_iter().enumerate() {
        at[r].add(r, n);
    }

    println!("  the bench is cut off; shelf and desk exchange counters");
    at[SHELF].merge(at[DESK]);
    at[DESK].merge(at[SHELF]);
    at[SHELF].add(SHELF, 60);
    at[BENCH].add(BENCH, 25);

    for (r, c) in at.into_iter().enumerate() {
        show(r, c);
    }

    println!("  the network heals; counters are exchanged in no particular order, some twice");
    at[DESK].merge(at[BENCH]);
    at[SHELF].merge(at[DESK]);
    at[BENCH].merge(at[SHELF]);
    at[DESK].merge(at[SHELF]);
    at[DESK].merge(at[SHELF]);

    for (r, c) in at.into_iter().enumerate() {
        show(r, c);
    }
}

GPUs in use

The third experiment counts jobs starting and ending. One job’s end is reported to a different replica from the one that recorded its start, which is ordinary: a job’s machine and the replica that hears of its end need not be in the same place.

Listing 9.9 Starts and ends recorded at different replicas, in Go. code/go/counter/main.go

// upAndDown counts GPUs in use. A job's start and its end may be recorded at
// different replicas.
func upAndDown() {
	var at [replicas]PNCounter
	at[shelf].Add(shelf, 3)  // three jobs start on the Thors
	at[desk].Add(desk, 2)    // two start on the Sparks
	at[shelf].Add(shelf, -1) // one Thor job ends
	at[bench].Add(bench, -1) // the bench replica hears that a Spark job ended

	for r := range at {
		for other := range at {
			at[r].Merge(at[other])
		}
	}

	c := at[bench]
	fmt.Printf("  started %v, ended %v: %d GPUs in use\n", [replicas]int(c.Up), [replicas]int(c.Down), c.Value())
}

Listing 9.10 Starts and ends recorded at different replicas, in Rust. code/rust/counter/src/main.rs

/// Counts GPUs in use. A job's start and its end may be recorded at different
/// replicas.
fn up_and_down() {
    let mut at = [PnCounter::default(); REPLICAS];
    at[SHELF].add(SHELF, 3); // three jobs start on the Thors
    at[DESK].add(DESK, 2); // two start on the Sparks
    at[SHELF].add(SHELF, -1); // one Thor job ends
    at[BENCH].add(BENCH, -1); // the bench replica hears that a Spark job ended

    for r in 0..REPLICAS {
        for other in 0..REPLICAS {
            let theirs = at[other];
            at[r].merge(theirs);
        }
    }

    let c = at[BENCH];
    println!(
        "  started {}, ended {}: {} GPUs in use",
        row(c.up.0),
        row(c.down.0),
        c.value()
    );
}

A quota, two ways

The last two experiments enforce a quota of six GPUs. sharedLimit lets each replica check its own merged count. allowances gives each replica a share.

Listing 9.11 A limit checked against a local count, and a limit divided into shares, in Go. code/go/counter/main.go

// quota is the number of GPUs one team may use at once.
const quota = 6

// sharedLimit lets each replica admit a job whenever its own view of the
// count leaves room under the quota.
func sharedLimit() {
	var at [replicas]PNCounter
	at[shelf].Add(shelf, 3)
	at[desk].Add(desk, 1)
	at[shelf].Merge(at[desk])
	at[desk].Merge(at[shelf])
	fmt.Printf("  both replicas see %d of %d in use; then they lose contact\n", at[shelf].Value(), quota)

	for _, r := range []int{shelf, desk} {
		if at[r].Value()+2 <= quota {
			at[r].Add(r, 2)
			fmt.Printf("  %-5s admits a 2-GPU job: by its count, %d of %d are in use\n", names[r], at[r].Value(), quota)
		}
	}

	at[shelf].Merge(at[desk])
	fmt.Printf("  after they merge: %d of %d in use\n", at[shelf].Value(), quota)
}

// allowances gives each replica a share of the quota. A replica admits a job
// only from its own share, and shares move between replicas when they can
// reach each other.
func allowances() {
	share := [replicas]int{4, 2, 0}
	used := [replicas]int{3, 1, 0}
	fmt.Printf("  shares %v, in use %v; then the replicas lose contact\n", share, used)
	admit := func(r, gpus int) {
		if left := share[r] - used[r]; left < gpus {
			fmt.Printf("  %-5s refuses a %d-GPU job: its share has %d left\n", names[r], gpus, left)

			return
		}

		used[r] += gpus
		fmt.Printf("  %-5s admits a %d-GPU job\n", names[r], gpus)
	}
	admit(shelf, 2)
	admit(desk, 2)
	fmt.Println("  contact returns; desk gives its unused GPU to shelf")
	share[desk]--
	share[shelf]++
	admit(shelf, 2)
	fmt.Printf("  shares %v, in use %v: %d of %d\n", share, used, used[shelf]+used[desk]+used[bench], quota)
}

Listing 9.12 A limit checked against a local count, and a limit divided into shares, in Rust. code/rust/counter/src/main.rs

/// The number of GPUs one team may use at once.
const QUOTA: i64 = 6;

/// Lets each replica admit a job whenever its own view of the count leaves
/// room under the quota.
fn shared_limit() {
    let mut at = [PnCounter::default(); REPLICAS];
    at[SHELF].add(SHELF, 3);
    at[DESK].add(DESK, 1);
    let (shelf, desk) = (at[SHELF], at[DESK]);
    at[SHELF].merge(desk);
    at[DESK].merge(shelf);
    println!(
        "  both replicas see {} of {QUOTA} in use; then they lose contact",
        at[SHELF].value()
    );

    for r in [SHELF, DESK] {
        if at[r].value() + 2 <= QUOTA {
            at[r].add(r, 2);
            println!(
                "  {:<5} admits a 2-GPU job: by its count, {} of {QUOTA} are in use",
                NAMES[r],
                at[r].value()
            );
        }
    }

    let desk = at[DESK];
    at[SHELF].merge(desk);
    println!(
        "  after they merge: {} of {QUOTA} in use",
        at[SHELF].value()
    );
}

/// Gives each replica a share of the quota. A replica admits a job only from
/// its own share, and shares move between replicas when they can reach each
/// other.
fn allowances() {
    let mut share = [4, 2, 0];
    let mut used = [3, 1, 0];
    println!(
        "  shares {}, in use {}; then the replicas lose contact",
        row(share),
        row(used)
    );
    let admit = |share: &[i64; REPLICAS], used: &mut [i64; REPLICAS], r: usize, gpus: i64| {
        let left = share[r] - used[r];

        if left < gpus {
            println!(
                "  {:<5} refuses a {gpus}-GPU job: its share has {left} left",
                NAMES[r]
            );

            return;
        }

        used[r] += gpus;
        println!("  {:<5} admits a {gpus}-GPU job", NAMES[r]);
    };
    admit(&share, &mut used, SHELF, 2);
    admit(&share, &mut used, DESK, 2);
    println!("  contact returns; desk gives its unused GPU to shelf");
    share[DESK] -= 1;
    share[SHELF] += 1;
    admit(&share, &mut used, SHELF, 2);
    println!(
        "  shares {}, in use {}: {} of {QUOTA}",
        row(share),
        row(used),
        used.iter().sum::<i64>()
    );
}

The run

$ go run ./counter
one number per replica:
  shelf records 300 GPU-seconds
  desk  records 120 GPU-seconds
  bench records 40 GPU-seconds
  after synchronising, every replica says 300; the jobs used 460
one entry per replica, merged by taking the larger:
  the bench is cut off; shelf and desk exchange counters
  shelf holds [360 120 0] = 480
  desk  holds [300 120 0] = 420
  bench holds [0 0 65] = 65
  the network heals; counters are exchanged in no particular order, some twice
  shelf holds [360 120 65] = 545
  desk  holds [360 120 65] = 545
  bench holds [360 120 65] = 545
a count that goes down as well:
  started [3 2 0], ended [1 0 1]: 3 GPUs in use
a quota checked against each replica's own count:
  both replicas see 4 of 6 in use; then they lose contact
  shelf admits a 2-GPU job: by its count, 6 of 6 are in use
  desk  admits a 2-GPU job: by its count, 6 of 6 are in use
  after they merge: 8 of 6 in use
a quota divided into shares:
  shares [4 2 0], in use [3 1 0]; then the replicas lose contact
  shelf refuses a 2-GPU job: its share has 1 left
  desk  refuses a 2-GPU job: its share has 1 left
  contact returns; desk gives its unused GPU to shelf
  shelf admits a 2-GPU job
  shares [5 1 0], in use [5 1 0]: 6 of 6

With one number per replica, every replica says 300 after synchronising, and the jobs used 460.

With one entry per replica, the two sides of the partition disagree while it lasts: the shelf counts 480, the desk 420, and the bench 65. That is expected, since each is missing reports the others hold. After the untidy recovery all three hold the row [360 120 65] and the count 545, which is every report added once: 300, 120, 40, 60, and 25.

Animation 9.1 plays the second experiment, one merge at a time.

The count of GPUs in use comes to 3: five jobs started, and two ended, one of them recorded at the bench.

In the fourth experiment both replicas admit a job at “6 of 6”, and the merged count is 8 of 6. In the fifth, with shares of 4 and 2 and usage of 3 and 1, both replicas refuse the two-GPU job while apart. When contact returns, the desk gives its unused GPU to the shelf, the shelf admits the job, and the total in use is exactly 6. Figure 9.2 shows the shares before and after.

Where Kubernetes stands

Inside one cluster, Kubernetes takes the first design. A ResourceQuota object limits what a namespace may use, and the API server checks it when a pod is created. The check is exact because the API server is the one authority and etcd puts every write in a single order: the resource version of chapter 1 is that order made visible. A cluster whose control plane is cut off from part of itself stops accepting changes from that part, which is the consistent choice.

The techniques of this chapter apply above that level: usage that is gathered from many clusters into one bill, a team’s quota that spans clusters in several regions, a rate limit enforced by many copies of an API gateway. In each of these, no single API server sees every event, and the count has to be assembled from replicas that are sometimes apart.

The complete program

Listing 9.13 The complete program in Go. code/go/counter/main.go

// Command counter shows how replicas of a service that cannot always reach
// each other can keep one count.
//
// Three replicas of a usage service run beside the three groups of machines
// in our fleet: the shelf, the desk, and the bench. Each records the GPU time
// that jobs near it report. The program counts in four ways: with one number
// per replica, with a grow-only counter, with a counter that also goes down,
// and with a quota divided into allowances.
//
// Run it from code/go with:
//
//	go run ./counter
package main

import "fmt"

// The replicas, by index.
const (
	shelf = iota
	desk
	bench
	replicas
)

// names lists the replicas in index order.
var names = [replicas]string{"shelf", "desk", "bench"}

// GCounter is a count that only grows. It keeps one entry per replica, and
// each replica adds only to its own entry.
type GCounter [replicas]int

// Add records n more at the given replica.
func (c *GCounter) Add(replica, n int) { c[replica] += n }

// Merge takes, entry by entry, the larger of this counter's value and the
// other's. Merging is safe to repeat and to do in any order.
func (c *GCounter) Merge(other GCounter) {
	for i := range c {
		c[i] = max(c[i], other[i])
	}
}

// Value is the count: the sum of every replica's entry.
func (c GCounter) Value() int {
	total := 0

	for _, n := range c {
		total += n
	}

	return total
}


// PNCounter is a count that goes up and down, built from two counters that
// only grow: one for the increments and one for the decrements.
type PNCounter struct {
	Up, Down GCounter
}

// Add records a change of n, which may be negative, at the given replica.
func (c *PNCounter) Add(replica, n int) {
	if n >= 0 {
		c.Up.Add(replica, n)
	} else {
		c.Down.Add(replica, -n)
	}
}

// Merge merges both halves.
func (c *PNCounter) Merge(other PNCounter) {
	c.Up.Merge(other.Up)
	c.Down.Merge(other.Down)
}

// Value is the increments minus the decrements.
func (c PNCounter) Value() int { return c.Up.Value() - c.Down.Value() }


// oneNumber counts with a single number per replica and synchronises by
// keeping the larger of two numbers.
func oneNumber() {
	totals := [replicas]int{}
	adds := [replicas]int{300, 120, 40}
	sum := 0

	for r, n := range adds {
		totals[r] += n
		sum += n
		fmt.Printf("  %-5s records %d GPU-seconds\n", names[r], n)
	}

	largest := max(totals[shelf], totals[desk], totals[bench])
	fmt.Printf("  after synchronising, every replica says %d; the jobs used %d\n", largest, sum)
}


// show prints one replica's counter and its value.
func show(replica int, c GCounter) {
	fmt.Printf("  %-5s holds %v = %d\n", names[replica], [replicas]int(c), c.Value())
}

// growOnly counts the same usage with a GCounter at each replica, through a
// partition that cuts the bench off from the other two.
func growOnly() {
	var at [replicas]GCounter

	for r, n := range [replicas]int{300, 120, 40} {
		at[r].Add(r, n)
	}

	fmt.Println("  the bench is cut off; shelf and desk exchange counters")
	at[shelf].Merge(at[desk])
	at[desk].Merge(at[shelf])
	at[shelf].Add(shelf, 60)
	at[bench].Add(bench, 25)

	for r := range at {
		show(r, at[r])
	}

	fmt.Println("  the network heals; counters are exchanged in no particular order, some twice")
	at[desk].Merge(at[bench])
	at[shelf].Merge(at[desk])
	at[bench].Merge(at[shelf])
	at[desk].Merge(at[shelf])
	at[desk].Merge(at[shelf])

	for r := range at {
		show(r, at[r])
	}
}


// upAndDown counts GPUs in use. A job's start and its end may be recorded at
// different replicas.
func upAndDown() {
	var at [replicas]PNCounter
	at[shelf].Add(shelf, 3)  // three jobs start on the Thors
	at[desk].Add(desk, 2)    // two start on the Sparks
	at[shelf].Add(shelf, -1) // one Thor job ends
	at[bench].Add(bench, -1) // the bench replica hears that a Spark job ended

	for r := range at {
		for other := range at {
			at[r].Merge(at[other])
		}
	}

	c := at[bench]
	fmt.Printf("  started %v, ended %v: %d GPUs in use\n", [replicas]int(c.Up), [replicas]int(c.Down), c.Value())
}


// quota is the number of GPUs one team may use at once.
const quota = 6

// sharedLimit lets each replica admit a job whenever its own view of the
// count leaves room under the quota.
func sharedLimit() {
	var at [replicas]PNCounter
	at[shelf].Add(shelf, 3)
	at[desk].Add(desk, 1)
	at[shelf].Merge(at[desk])
	at[desk].Merge(at[shelf])
	fmt.Printf("  both replicas see %d of %d in use; then they lose contact\n", at[shelf].Value(), quota)

	for _, r := range []int{shelf, desk} {
		if at[r].Value()+2 <= quota {
			at[r].Add(r, 2)
			fmt.Printf("  %-5s admits a 2-GPU job: by its count, %d of %d are in use\n", names[r], at[r].Value(), quota)
		}
	}

	at[shelf].Merge(at[desk])
	fmt.Printf("  after they merge: %d of %d in use\n", at[shelf].Value(), quota)
}

// allowances gives each replica a share of the quota. A replica admits a job
// only from its own share, and shares move between replicas when they can
// reach each other.
func allowances() {
	share := [replicas]int{4, 2, 0}
	used := [replicas]int{3, 1, 0}
	fmt.Printf("  shares %v, in use %v; then the replicas lose contact\n", share, used)
	admit := func(r, gpus int) {
		if left := share[r] - used[r]; left < gpus {
			fmt.Printf("  %-5s refuses a %d-GPU job: its share has %d left\n", names[r], gpus, left)

			return
		}

		used[r] += gpus
		fmt.Printf("  %-5s admits a %d-GPU job\n", names[r], gpus)
	}
	admit(shelf, 2)
	admit(desk, 2)
	fmt.Println("  contact returns; desk gives its unused GPU to shelf")
	share[desk]--
	share[shelf]++
	admit(shelf, 2)
	fmt.Printf("  shares %v, in use %v: %d of %d\n", share, used, used[shelf]+used[desk]+used[bench], quota)
}


func main() {
	fmt.Println("one number per replica:")
	oneNumber()
	fmt.Println("one entry per replica, merged by taking the larger:")
	growOnly()
	fmt.Println("a count that goes down as well:")
	upAndDown()
	fmt.Println("a quota checked against each replica's own count:")
	sharedLimit()
	fmt.Println("a quota divided into shares:")
	allowances()
}

Listing 9.14 The complete program in Rust. code/rust/counter/src/main.rs

//! Shows how replicas of a service that cannot always reach each other can
//! keep one count.
//!
//! Three replicas of a usage service run beside the three groups of machines
//! in our fleet: the shelf, the desk, and the bench. Each records the GPU time
//! that jobs near it report. The program counts in four ways: with one number
//! per replica, with a grow-only counter, with a counter that also goes down,
//! and with a quota divided into allowances.
//!
//! Run it from code/rust with:
//!
//! ```text
//! cargo run -p counter
//! ```

/// The number of replicas.
const REPLICAS: usize = 3;
/// The replicas, by index.
const SHELF: usize = 0;
const DESK: usize = 1;
const BENCH: usize = 2;
/// The replicas' names, in index order.
const NAMES: [&str; REPLICAS] = ["shelf", "desk", "bench"];

/// Formats a row of numbers as Go prints an array: `[1 2 3]`.
fn row(values: [i64; REPLICAS]) -> String {
    format!("[{}]", values.map(|v| v.to_string()).join(" "))
}

/// A count that only grows. It keeps one entry per replica, and each replica
/// adds only to its own entry.
#[derive(Clone, Copy, Default)]
struct GCounter([i64; REPLICAS]);

impl GCounter {
    /// Records n more at the given replica.
    fn add(&mut self, replica: usize, n: i64) {
        self.0[replica] += n;
    }

    /// Takes, entry by entry, the larger of this counter's value and the
    /// other's. Merging is safe to repeat and to do in any order.
    fn merge(&mut self, other: GCounter) {
        for (mine, theirs) in self.0.iter_mut().zip(other.0) {
            *mine = (*mine).max(theirs);
        }
    }

    /// The count: the sum of every replica's entry.
    fn value(self) -> i64 {
        self.0.iter().sum()
    }
}

/// A count that goes up and down, built from two counters that only grow: one
/// for the increments and one for the decrements.
#[derive(Clone, Copy, Default)]
struct PnCounter {
    up: GCounter,
    down: GCounter,
}

impl PnCounter {
    /// Records a change of n, which may be negative, at the given replica.
    fn add(&mut self, replica: usize, n: i64) {
        if n >= 0 {
            self.up.add(replica, n);
        } else {
            self.down.add(replica, -n);
        }
    }

    /// Merges both halves.
    fn merge(&mut self, other: PnCounter) {
        self.up.merge(other.up);
        self.down.merge(other.down);
    }

    /// The increments minus the decrements.
    fn value(self) -> i64 {
        self.up.value() - self.down.value()
    }
}

/// Counts with a single number per replica and synchronises by keeping the
/// larger of two numbers.
fn one_number() {
    let adds = [300, 120, 40];

    for (name, n) in NAMES.iter().zip(adds) {
        println!("  {name:<5} records {n} GPU-seconds");
    }

    let largest = adds.iter().max().expect("three replicas");
    let sum: i64 = adds.iter().sum();
    println!("  after synchronising, every replica says {largest}; the jobs used {sum}");
}

/// Prints one replica's counter and its value.
fn show(replica: usize, c: GCounter) {
    println!("  {:<5} holds {} = {}", NAMES[replica], row(c.0), c.value());
}

/// Counts the same usage with a `GCounter` at each replica, through a
/// partition that cuts the bench off from the other two.
fn grow_only() {
    let mut at = [GCounter::default(); REPLICAS];

    for (r, n) in [300, 120, 40].into_iter().enumerate() {
        at[r].add(r, n);
    }

    println!("  the bench is cut off; shelf and desk exchange counters");
    at[SHELF].merge(at[DESK]);
    at[DESK].merge(at[SHELF]);
    at[SHELF].add(SHELF, 60);
    at[BENCH].add(BENCH, 25);

    for (r, c) in at.into_iter().enumerate() {
        show(r, c);
    }

    println!("  the network heals; counters are exchanged in no particular order, some twice");
    at[DESK].merge(at[BENCH]);
    at[SHELF].merge(at[DESK]);
    at[BENCH].merge(at[SHELF]);
    at[DESK].merge(at[SHELF]);
    at[DESK].merge(at[SHELF]);

    for (r, c) in at.into_iter().enumerate() {
        show(r, c);
    }
}

/// Counts GPUs in use. A job's start and its end may be recorded at different
/// replicas.
fn up_and_down() {
    let mut at = [PnCounter::default(); REPLICAS];
    at[SHELF].add(SHELF, 3); // three jobs start on the Thors
    at[DESK].add(DESK, 2); // two start on the Sparks
    at[SHELF].add(SHELF, -1); // one Thor job ends
    at[BENCH].add(BENCH, -1); // the bench replica hears that a Spark job ended

    for r in 0..REPLICAS {
        for other in 0..REPLICAS {
            let theirs = at[other];
            at[r].merge(theirs);
        }
    }

    let c = at[BENCH];
    println!(
        "  started {}, ended {}: {} GPUs in use",
        row(c.up.0),
        row(c.down.0),
        c.value()
    );
}

/// The number of GPUs one team may use at once.
const QUOTA: i64 = 6;

/// Lets each replica admit a job whenever its own view of the count leaves
/// room under the quota.
fn shared_limit() {
    let mut at = [PnCounter::default(); REPLICAS];
    at[SHELF].add(SHELF, 3);
    at[DESK].add(DESK, 1);
    let (shelf, desk) = (at[SHELF], at[DESK]);
    at[SHELF].merge(desk);
    at[DESK].merge(shelf);
    println!(
        "  both replicas see {} of {QUOTA} in use; then they lose contact",
        at[SHELF].value()
    );

    for r in [SHELF, DESK] {
        if at[r].value() + 2 <= QUOTA {
            at[r].add(r, 2);
            println!(
                "  {:<5} admits a 2-GPU job: by its count, {} of {QUOTA} are in use",
                NAMES[r],
                at[r].value()
            );
        }
    }

    let desk = at[DESK];
    at[SHELF].merge(desk);
    println!(
        "  after they merge: {} of {QUOTA} in use",
        at[SHELF].value()
    );
}

/// Gives each replica a share of the quota. A replica admits a job only from
/// its own share, and shares move between replicas when they can reach each
/// other.
fn allowances() {
    let mut share = [4, 2, 0];
    let mut used = [3, 1, 0];
    println!(
        "  shares {}, in use {}; then the replicas lose contact",
        row(share),
        row(used)
    );
    let admit = |share: &[i64; REPLICAS], used: &mut [i64; REPLICAS], r: usize, gpus: i64| {
        let left = share[r] - used[r];

        if left < gpus {
            println!(
                "  {:<5} refuses a {gpus}-GPU job: its share has {left} left",
                NAMES[r]
            );

            return;
        }

        used[r] += gpus;
        println!("  {:<5} admits a {gpus}-GPU job", NAMES[r]);
    };
    admit(&share, &mut used, SHELF, 2);
    admit(&share, &mut used, DESK, 2);
    println!("  contact returns; desk gives its unused GPU to shelf");
    share[DESK] -= 1;
    share[SHELF] += 1;
    admit(&share, &mut used, SHELF, 2);
    println!(
        "  shares {}, in use {}: {} of {QUOTA}",
        row(share),
        row(used),
        used.iter().sum::<i64>()
    );
}

fn main() {
    println!("one number per replica:");
    one_number();
    println!("one entry per replica, merged by taking the larger:");
    grow_only();
    println!("a count that goes down as well:");
    up_and_down();
    println!("a quota checked against each replica's own count:");
    shared_limit();
    println!("a quota divided into shares:");
    allowances();
}

Summary

  • A single authority gives an exact count and stops counting on the cut-off side of a partition.
  • A single number per replica cannot be merged: keeping the larger loses counts, and adding counts them again on every exchange.
  • A grow-only counter keeps one entry per replica. Each replica adds to its own entry, and a merge keeps the larger value of each entry.
  • That merge can be repeated, reordered, and relayed through other replicas, so replicas that have received the same reports hold the same count.
  • Two grow-only counters, one for increases and one for decreases, give a count that goes down.
  • A merged count cannot enforce a limit. Dividing the limit into shares can, at the price of refusing some work while capacity exists.

References

  • M. Shapiro, N. Preguiça, C. Baquero, M. Zawirski, “A comprehensive study of Convergent and Commutative Replicated Data Types”, INRIA research report 7506, 2011.
  • V. Balegas and others, “Extending Eventually Consistent Cloud Databases for Enforcing Numeric Invariants”, 34th IEEE Symposium on Reliable Distributed Systems (SRDS), 2015, which describes the bounded counter with transferable shares.
  • S. Gilbert and N. Lynch, “Brewer’s conjecture and the feasibility of consistent, available, partition-tolerant web services”, ACM SIGACT News, 33(2), 2002.
  • Kubernetes, Resource quotas: https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/policy/resource-quotas/
  • etcd, KV API guarantees: https://etcd.io/docs/latest/learning/api_guarantees/

10. A content-addressed artifact store

Before a job can run on a machine, the machine needs two large things: the container image with the job’s programs, and usually a model’s weights. A Thor or a Spark has room for a model of a hundred gigabytes, and a training run produces a new version of such a model every few hours. Something has to hold all of these versions, deliver them to the machines, and do so without copying a hundred gigabytes every time one layer of a model changes.

In this chapter, we will build the storage scheme that container registries and model stores share. We will first see what is wrong with naming a file by a name someone chose, and what changes when a file is named by its own contents. Then we will write cas, in Go and in Rust, a store that keeps artifacts as chunks named by their hashes, and use it to push two versions of a model, pull one to a machine that already has the other, detect a corrupted chunk and repair it, and reclaim space.

This chapter covers

  • names that people choose, and what they fail to promise
  • a digest: a name computed from the contents
  • the same scheme in the containerd on our own node
  • chunks and manifests: an artifact as a list of digests
  • pushing a second version that shares most of its chunks with the first
  • pulling only what a machine lacks, and verifying every byte that arrives
  • garbage collection: removing what no tag leads to

What a chosen name does not promise

Suppose we store model weights in files named by hand: llm-v1.bin, llm-v2.bin, and llm-latest.bin. A machine that is told to load llm-latest.bin and has a file of that name faces three questions that the name cannot answer.

  • Is it the right version? latest pointed at one model yesterday and points at another today. Two machines in the same training job can hold different files under the same name, and the job’s results depend on which machine a worker landed on.
  • Is it intact? A file that was cut short by a full disk or changed by a failing memory chip still has its name.
  • Do I already have it? If llm-v2.bin is llm-v1.bin with one layer retrained, the two files are almost the same bytes, and nothing in their names says so. The machine downloads all of it.

All three come from one cause: the name and the contents are unrelated, so knowing one tells us nothing about the other.

A name computed from the contents

A cryptographic hash function takes any sequence of bytes and computes a short, fixed-size value from it. SHA-256, the one we will use, produces 256 bits, written as 64 hexadecimal digits. Two properties make it useful here. The same bytes always give the same value. And although different inputs that give the same value must exist, nobody knows how to find two, so in practice a value identifies one particular sequence of bytes. The value is called a digest.

A content-addressed store uses the digest as the name. To store a blob of bytes, we compute its digest and file the blob under it. To fetch a blob, we ask for a digest. This one change answers all three questions.

  • A digest names exactly one sequence of bytes, for ever. Two machines that load the same digest load the same model.
  • Anyone who receives a blob can compute its digest and compare it with the name they asked for. A blob that was cut short or altered does not match.
  • Two blobs with the same contents have the same name, so storing the second one is a lookup that finds the first. A machine that is asked for a digest it holds has nothing to download.

The same scheme on our node

The container images on our cluster are stored this way already. containerd keeps the parts of every image in a directory of files named by their SHA-256 digests. On our kind node:

$ docker exec gpu-lab-control-plane sh -c \
    'ls /var/lib/containerd/io.containerd.content.v1.content/blobs/sha256 | head -3'
0286df745178d6f77c036891bed8ddc93a624c1fa9c054d134674a096ee882e6
069d1e267530c2e681fbd4d481553b4d05f98082b18fafac86e7f12996dddd0b
0771a8a05619f4bb6f4c8484f942f509b21a04ebe56ee26d17a3006e250bfd46
$ docker exec gpu-lab-control-plane sh -c \
    'ls /var/lib/containerd/io.containerd.content.v1.content/blobs/sha256 | wc -l'
168

The node holds 168 blobs. The images we built in earlier chapters appear with the first digits of their digests when we list them:

$ docker exec gpu-lab-control-plane crictl images --digests | grep -E 'IMAGE|gpu-'
IMAGE                            TAG   DIGEST          IMAGE ID        SIZE
docker.io/library/gpu-check      1     3714eb7096522   07ee9035514b4   73.3MB
docker.io/library/gpu-sharing    1     413391f612a31   4212abdefc4b5   74.3MB

gpu-check and gpu-sharing were built from the same base image, so most of their 73 MB are the same blobs, stored once. A pod’s spec can name an image by its digest, gpu-check@sha256:..., and the kubelet then runs exactly those bytes on every node.

Chunks and manifests

Naming a whole 100 GB file by one digest answers the first two questions and does little for the third: change one byte and the digest changes, and the whole file is a new blob. To share the unchanged parts between versions, we store a large artifact in pieces.

  • The artifact is cut into chunks of a fixed size. Each chunk is a blob, stored under its own digest.
  • A manifest is a small text file that lists the chunks’ digests in order. It is a blob too, and its digest names the whole artifact: a different chunk anywhere gives a different line in the manifest and so a different manifest digest.
  • A tag is a name a person chose, such as llm:v2, that points at a manifest’s digest. Tags are the only part of the store that can change.

Figure 10.1 shows two versions of a model stored this way.

Version 2 is version 1 with its last quarter retrained. Its first six chunks have the same bytes as version 1’s, therefore the same digests, therefore they are the same blobs. Pushing version 2 stores two new chunks and one new manifest.

Container images have had this shape for years. An image is a manifest that lists layers, each layer is a blob named by its digest, and two images built from the same base share the base’s layers. The format is standardised as the OCI image specification, and registries that store images this way are now also used to store model weights as OCI artifacts.

Low-level design: cas

TypeHoldsRule it keeps
Digestsha256: and 64 hex digitscomputed from the bytes it names
Storeblobs by digest, and tags by namea blob’s bytes hash to its key; only tags are ever reassigned
manifest (a blob)one line per chunk: digest and sizelists the chunks in order
OperationDoes
Put(bytes)stores a blob under its digest, once
Get(digest)returns a blob after hashing it again and comparing
Push(tag, artifact)chunks the artifact, stores chunks and manifest, points the tag at the manifest
Pull(remote, tag)copies the manifest and whichever of its chunks are missing here
Read(tag)reassembles an artifact, verifying each chunk
Collect()removes every blob that no tag leads to

Building cas

Our store keeps its blobs in memory, in a map from digest to bytes, so that the program has no files to clean up. A store on disk is the same map with one file per blob, in a directory layout like the one containerd showed us.

Blobs by digest

Put computes the digest, and stores the bytes only if that digest is not already a key. Get does more than a lookup: it hashes the bytes again and compares the result with the name it was asked for. Every read is therefore a check of the data.

Listing 10.1 Digests, the store, and verified reads, in Go. code/go/cas/main.go

// Digest names a blob: "sha256:" followed by the hash of its bytes in hex.
type Digest string

// digestOf computes the name of some bytes.
func digestOf(data []byte) Digest {
	sum := sha256.Sum256(data)

	return Digest("sha256:" + hex.EncodeToString(sum[:]))
}

// short returns the first twelve hex digits, for printing.
func (d Digest) short() string { return string(d)[:19] }

// Store holds blobs by digest and tags by name.
type Store struct {
	// blobs maps each digest to the bytes it names.
	blobs map[Digest][]byte
	// tags maps a name people use, such as "llm:v1", to a manifest's digest.
	tags map[string]Digest
}

// NewStore returns an empty store.
func NewStore() *Store {
	return &Store{blobs: map[Digest][]byte{}, tags: map[string]Digest{}}
}

// Put stores a blob and returns its digest and whether it was new. Bytes the
// store already holds are not stored a second time.
func (s *Store) Put(data []byte) (Digest, bool) {
	d := digestOf(data)

	if _, held := s.blobs[d]; held {
		return d, false
	}

	s.blobs[d] = data

	return d, true
}

// ErrCorrupt is returned when a blob's bytes no longer match its name.
var ErrCorrupt = errors.New("contents do not match the digest")

// Get returns a blob after checking that its bytes still hash to its name.
func (s *Store) Get(d Digest) ([]byte, error) {
	data, held := s.blobs[d]

	if !held {
		return nil, fmt.Errorf("%s: not found", d.short())
	}

	if digestOf(data) != d {
		return nil, fmt.Errorf("%s: %w", d.short(), ErrCorrupt)
	}

	return data, nil
}

Listing 10.2 Digests, the store, and verified reads, in Rust. code/rust/cas/src/main.rs

/// Names a blob: "sha256:" followed by the hash of its bytes in hex.
#[derive(Clone, PartialEq, Eq, PartialOrd, Ord)]
struct Digest(String);

impl Digest {
    /// Computes the name of some bytes.
    fn of(data: &[u8]) -> Self {
        let mut name = String::from("sha256:");

        for byte in Sha256::digest(data) {
            write!(name, "{byte:02x}").expect("writing to a String");
        }

        Digest(name)
    }

    /// Returns the first twelve hex digits, for printing.
    fn short(&self) -> &str {
        &self.0[..19]
    }
}

/// The ways reading from a store can fail.
#[derive(Debug)]
enum StoreError {
    /// The store holds no blob of this name.
    NotFound(String),
    /// A blob's bytes no longer match its name.
    Corrupt(String),
    /// A chunk of an artifact could not be read.
    Chunk(usize, Box<StoreError>),
}

impl std::fmt::Display for StoreError {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut std::fmt::Formatter<'_>) -> std::fmt::Result {
        match self {
            StoreError::NotFound(d) => write!(f, "{d}: not found"),
            StoreError::Corrupt(d) => write!(f, "{d}: contents do not match the digest"),
            StoreError::Chunk(i, e) => write!(f, "chunk {i}: {e}"),
        }
    }
}

/// Holds blobs by digest and tags by name.
#[derive(Default)]
struct Store {
    /// Maps each digest to the bytes it names.
    blobs: BTreeMap<Digest, Vec<u8>>,
    /// Maps a name people use, such as "llm:v1", to a manifest's digest.
    tags: BTreeMap<&'static str, Digest>,
}

impl Store {
    /// Stores a blob and returns its digest and whether it was new. Bytes the
    /// store already holds are not stored a second time.
    fn put(&mut self, data: &[u8]) -> (Digest, bool) {
        let d = Digest::of(data);

        if self.blobs.contains_key(&d) {
            return (d, false);
        }

        self.blobs.insert(d.clone(), data.to_vec());

        (d, true)
    }

    /// Returns a blob after checking that its bytes still hash to its name.
    fn get(&self, d: &Digest) -> Result<&[u8], StoreError> {
        let data = self
            .blobs
            .get(d)
            .ok_or_else(|| StoreError::NotFound(d.short().to_owned()))?;
        if Digest::of(data) != *d {
            return Err(StoreError::Corrupt(d.short().to_owned()));
        }

        Ok(data)
    }
}

The Go standard library includes SHA-256 in crypto/sha256. Rust’s standard library has no hash functions of this kind, and the Rust version uses the sha2 crate. The two programs print the same digests, which is itself a check: both computed the same function over the same bytes.

Pushing an artifact

Push walks the artifact in steps of the chunk size, stores each chunk with Put, and writes one line of the manifest for it. It counts how many chunks Put reported as new, which is the amount of data a push to a remote registry would have to send. The manifest is then stored like any other blob, and the tag is pointed at it.

Listing 10.3 Chunking an artifact and writing its manifest, in Go. code/go/cas/main.go

// chunkSize is the size of the pieces an artifact is cut into.
const chunkSize = 8 * 1024

// Push cuts an artifact into chunks, stores each chunk, stores a manifest
// that lists the chunks' digests in order, and points the tag at the
// manifest. It returns how many chunks were new to the store.
func (s *Store) Push(tag string, artifact []byte) (manifest Digest, chunks, added int) {
	var lines strings.Builder

	for start := 0; start < len(artifact); start += chunkSize {
		chunk := artifact[start:min(start+chunkSize, len(artifact))]
		d, fresh := s.Put(chunk)

		if fresh {
			added++
		}

		chunks++
		fmt.Fprintf(&lines, "%s %d\n", d, len(chunk))
	}

	manifest, _ = s.Put([]byte(lines.String()))
	s.tags[tag] = manifest

	return manifest, chunks, added
}

// chunksOf reads a manifest and returns the digests it lists.
func (s *Store) chunksOf(manifest Digest) ([]Digest, error) {
	data, err := s.Get(manifest)

	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	var out []Digest

	for line := range strings.Lines(string(data)) {
		digest, _, _ := strings.Cut(line, " ")
		out = append(out, Digest(digest))
	}

	return out, nil
}

Listing 10.4 Chunking an artifact and writing its manifest, in Rust. code/rust/cas/src/main.rs

impl Store {
    /// Cuts an artifact into chunks, stores each chunk, stores a manifest that
    /// lists the chunks' digests in order, and points the tag at the manifest.
    /// Returns the manifest's digest, the number of chunks, and how many were
    /// new to the store.
    fn push(&mut self, tag: &'static str, artifact: &[u8]) -> (Digest, usize, usize) {
        let mut lines = String::new();
        let (mut chunks, mut added) = (0, 0);

        for chunk in artifact.chunks(CHUNK_SIZE) {
            let (d, fresh) = self.put(chunk);
            added += usize::from(fresh);
            chunks += 1;
            writeln!(lines, "{} {}", d.0, chunk.len()).expect("writing to a String");
        }

        let (manifest, _) = self.put(lines.as_bytes());
        self.tags.insert(tag, manifest.clone());

        (manifest, chunks, added)
    }

    /// Reads a manifest and returns the digests it lists.
    fn chunks_of(&self, manifest: &Digest) -> Result<Vec<Digest>, StoreError> {
        let text = String::from_utf8_lossy(self.get(manifest)?);
        Ok(text
            .lines()
            .filter_map(|line| line.split(' ').next())
            .map(|d| Digest(d.to_owned()))
            .collect())
    }
}

Our chunks are 8 KiB so that the example stays small. Real stores use chunks of a few megabytes: large enough that the list of digests stays short, small enough that a change touches few of them.

Pulling and reading

Pull is how a machine gets an artifact. It reads the manifest from the remote store, and for each chunk it lists, fetches the chunk only if the local store has no blob of that name. Read reassembles the artifact from local chunks, and since it goes through Get, a damaged chunk stops the read with an error that says which chunk it was.

Listing 10.5 Fetching what is missing, and reading with verification, in Go. code/go/cas/main.go

// Pull copies a tagged artifact from a remote store into this one, fetching
// only the chunks this store does not hold. It returns the number of chunks
// and bytes fetched.
func (s *Store) Pull(remote *Store, tag string) (fetched, bytes int, err error) {
	manifest := remote.tags[tag]
	chunks, err := remote.chunksOf(manifest)

	if err != nil {
		return 0, 0, err
	}

	for _, d := range chunks {
		if _, held := s.blobs[d]; held {
			continue
		}

		data, err := remote.Get(d)

		if err != nil {
			return fetched, bytes, err
		}

		s.blobs[d] = data
		fetched++
		bytes += len(data)
	}

	data, _ := remote.Get(manifest)
	s.blobs[manifest] = data
	s.tags[tag] = manifest

	return fetched, bytes, nil
}

// Read reassembles a tagged artifact, verifying every chunk on the way.
func (s *Store) Read(tag string) ([]byte, error) {
	chunks, err := s.chunksOf(s.tags[tag])

	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	var artifact []byte

	for i, d := range chunks {
		data, err := s.Get(d)

		if err != nil {
			return nil, fmt.Errorf("chunk %d: %w", i, err)
		}

		artifact = append(artifact, data...)
	}

	return artifact, nil
}

Listing 10.6 Fetching what is missing, and reading with verification, in Rust. code/rust/cas/src/main.rs

impl Store {
    /// Copies a tagged artifact from a remote store into this one, fetching
    /// only the chunks this store does not hold. Returns the number of chunks
    /// and bytes fetched.
    fn pull(&mut self, remote: &Store, tag: &'static str) -> Result<(usize, usize), StoreError> {
        let manifest = &remote.tags[tag];
        let (mut fetched, mut bytes) = (0, 0);

        for d in remote.chunks_of(manifest)? {
            if self.blobs.contains_key(&d) {
                continue;
            }

            let data = remote.get(&d)?;
            fetched += 1;
            bytes += data.len();
            self.blobs.insert(d, data.to_vec());
        }

        self.blobs
            .insert(manifest.clone(), remote.get(manifest)?.to_vec());
        self.tags.insert(tag, manifest.clone());

        Ok((fetched, bytes))
    }

    /// Reassembles a tagged artifact, verifying every chunk on the way.
    fn read(&self, tag: &str) -> Result<Vec<u8>, StoreError> {
        let mut artifact = Vec::new();

        for (i, d) in self.chunks_of(&self.tags[tag])?.iter().enumerate() {
            let data = self.get(d).map_err(|e| StoreError::Chunk(i, Box::new(e)))?;
            artifact.extend_from_slice(data);
        }

        Ok(artifact)
    }
}

Reclaiming space

A content-addressed store grows with every push, and blobs cannot be deleted one artifact at a time, because a chunk may be listed by several manifests. The store removes blobs by garbage collection in two phases. The mark phase starts from the tags, which are the only names people use, and notes every manifest they point at and every chunk those manifests list. The sweep phase removes every blob that was not noted.

Listing 10.7 Mark from the tags, then sweep, in Go. code/go/cas/main.go

// Collect removes every blob that no tag leads to. It first marks the
// manifests the tags name and the chunks those manifests list, then sweeps
// the rest. It returns the number of blobs and bytes removed.
func (s *Store) Collect() (removed, bytes int) {
	live := map[Digest]bool{}

	for _, manifest := range s.tags {
		live[manifest] = true
		chunks, _ := s.chunksOf(manifest)

		for _, d := range chunks {
			live[d] = true
		}
	}

	for d, data := range s.blobs {
		if !live[d] {
			removed++
			bytes += len(data)
			delete(s.blobs, d)
		}
	}

	return removed, bytes
}

Listing 10.8 Mark from the tags, then sweep, in Rust. code/rust/cas/src/main.rs

impl Store {
    /// Removes every blob that no tag leads to. It first marks the manifests
    /// the tags name and the chunks those manifests list, then sweeps the
    /// rest. Returns the number of blobs and bytes removed.
    fn collect(&mut self) -> (usize, usize) {
        let mut live = BTreeSet::new();

        for manifest in self.tags.values() {
            live.extend(self.chunks_of(manifest).unwrap_or_default());
            live.insert(manifest.clone());
        }

        let (before, bytes_before) = self.size();
        self.blobs.retain(|d, _| live.contains(d));
        let (after, bytes_after) = self.size();

        (before - after, bytes_before - bytes_after)
    }
}

The run

The model’s weights are 64 KiB from a fixed pseudo-random sequence, which stand in for gigabytes. Version 2 keeps the first 48 KiB and replaces the last 16.

Listing 10.9 Two versions, a pull, a damaged chunk, and a collection, in Go. code/go/cas/main.go

func main() {
	// Version 1 is 64 KiB. Version 2 is a fine-tuned copy: the first three
	// quarters are unchanged and the last quarter is new.
	v1 := weights(64*1024, 1)
	v2 := append(append([]byte{}, v1[:48*1024]...), weights(16*1024, 2)...)

	registry := NewStore()
	m1, chunks, added := registry.Push("llm:v1", v1)
	fmt.Printf("push llm:v1: %d chunks, %d new; manifest %s\n", chunks, added, m1.short())
	m2, chunks, added := registry.Push("llm:v2", v2)
	fmt.Printf("push llm:v2: %d chunks, %d new; manifest %s\n", chunks, added, m2.short())
	blobs, bytes := registry.size()
	fmt.Printf("the registry holds %d blobs and %d bytes for %d bytes of artifacts\n", blobs, bytes, len(v1)+len(v2))

	node := NewStore()
	fetched, n, _ := node.Pull(registry, "llm:v1")
	fmt.Printf("thor-1 pulls llm:v1: %d chunks fetched, %d bytes\n", fetched, n)
	fetched, n, _ = node.Pull(registry, "llm:v2")
	fmt.Printf("thor-1 pulls llm:v2: %d chunks fetched, %d bytes\n", fetched, n)

	fmt.Println("one bit flips in a chunk stored on thor-1:")
	chunkList, _ := node.chunksOf(node.tags["llm:v1"])
	damaged := append([]byte{}, node.blobs[chunkList[3]]...)
	damaged[100] ^= 1
	node.blobs[chunkList[3]] = damaged

	if _, err := node.Read("llm:v1"); err != nil {
		fmt.Printf("  read llm:v1: %v\n", err)
	}

	// The bad chunk is dropped and pulled again, by name, from the registry.
	delete(node.blobs, chunkList[3])
	fetched, n, _ = node.Pull(registry, "llm:v1")
	fmt.Printf("  thor-1 drops the chunk and pulls llm:v1 again: %d chunk fetched, %d bytes\n", fetched, n)

	if got, err := node.Read("llm:v1"); err == nil {
		fmt.Printf("  read llm:v1: %d bytes, digest %s\n", len(got), digestOf(got).short())
	}

	fmt.Println("the tag llm:v1 is deleted from the registry:")
	delete(registry.tags, "llm:v1")
	removed, n := registry.Collect()
	blobs, bytes = registry.size()
	fmt.Printf("  collected %d blobs, %d bytes; %d blobs and %d bytes remain\n", removed, n, blobs, bytes)
}

Listing 10.10 Two versions, a pull, a damaged chunk, and a collection, in Rust. code/rust/cas/src/main.rs

fn main() -> Result<(), StoreError> {
    // Version 1 is 64 KiB. Version 2 is a fine-tuned copy: the first three
    // quarters are unchanged and the last quarter is new.
    let v1 = weights(64 * 1024, 1);
    let v2 = [&v1[..48 * 1024], &weights(16 * 1024, 2)].concat();

    let mut registry = Store::default();
    let (m1, chunks, added) = registry.push("llm:v1", &v1);
    println!(
        "push llm:v1: {chunks} chunks, {added} new; manifest {}",
        m1.short()
    );
    let (m2, chunks, added) = registry.push("llm:v2", &v2);
    println!(
        "push llm:v2: {chunks} chunks, {added} new; manifest {}",
        m2.short()
    );
    let (blobs, bytes) = registry.size();
    println!(
        "the registry holds {blobs} blobs and {bytes} bytes for {} bytes of artifacts",
        v1.len() + v2.len()
    );

    let mut node = Store::default();
    let (fetched, n) = node.pull(&registry, "llm:v1")?;
    println!("thor-1 pulls llm:v1: {fetched} chunks fetched, {n} bytes");
    let (fetched, n) = node.pull(&registry, "llm:v2")?;
    println!("thor-1 pulls llm:v2: {fetched} chunks fetched, {n} bytes");

    println!("one bit flips in a chunk stored on thor-1:");
    let fourth = node.chunks_of(&node.tags["llm:v1"])?[3].clone();
    node.blobs.get_mut(&fourth).expect("the chunk was pulled")[100] ^= 1;

    if let Err(e) = node.read("llm:v1") {
        println!("  read llm:v1: {e}");
    }

    // The bad chunk is dropped and pulled again, by name, from the registry.
    node.blobs.remove(&fourth);
    let (fetched, n) = node.pull(&registry, "llm:v1")?;
    println!("  thor-1 drops the chunk and pulls llm:v1 again: {fetched} chunk fetched, {n} bytes");
    let got = node.read("llm:v1")?;
    println!(
        "  read llm:v1: {} bytes, digest {}",
        got.len(),
        Digest::of(&got).short()
    );

    println!("the tag llm:v1 is deleted from the registry:");
    registry.tags.remove("llm:v1");
    let (removed, n) = registry.collect();
    let (blobs, bytes) = registry.size();
    println!("  collected {removed} blobs, {n} bytes; {blobs} blobs and {bytes} bytes remain");

    Ok(())
}
$ go run ./cas
push llm:v1: 8 chunks, 8 new; manifest sha256:eea2b9042521
push llm:v2: 8 chunks, 2 new; manifest sha256:7df8dd2bef7e
the registry holds 12 blobs and 83152 bytes for 131072 bytes of artifacts
thor-1 pulls llm:v1: 8 chunks fetched, 65536 bytes
thor-1 pulls llm:v2: 2 chunks fetched, 16384 bytes
one bit flips in a chunk stored on thor-1:
  read llm:v1: chunk 3: sha256:156fd5154cde: contents do not match the digest
  thor-1 drops the chunk and pulls llm:v1 again: 1 chunk fetched, 8192 bytes
  read llm:v1: 65536 bytes, digest sha256:a6a98d7f309f
the tag llm:v1 is deleted from the registry:
  collected 3 blobs, 17000 bytes; 9 blobs and 66152 bytes remain

Pushing llm:v1 stores eight chunks. Pushing llm:v2 cuts the new artifact into eight chunks as well and finds six of them already in the store, so it adds two. The registry holds 12 blobs, ten chunks and two manifests, in 83,152 bytes, for two artifacts that total 131,072 bytes.

thor-1 starts empty and fetches all eight chunks of version 1. For version 2 it fetches two chunks, 16,384 bytes, a quarter of the artifact. On a 100 GB model with the same proportion changed, that is 25 GB where a store of whole files would send 100, and at the 3.125 GB per second of one 25 GbE lane it is 8 seconds where the whole file takes 32.

Then one bit of one chunk on thor-1 flips. The next read of llm:v1 stops at chunk 3 and names the digest whose contents no longer match. The repair is as local as the damage: the machine drops that one blob and pulls again, the pull finds seven chunks present and one missing, and 8,192 bytes come from the registry. The digest printed for the reassembled artifact, sha256:a6a98d7f309f, is the digest of the whole 64 KiB of version 1.

Finally the tag llm:v1 is deleted. Collection marks the manifest of version 2 and its eight chunks, and sweeps three blobs: the manifest of version 1 and the two chunks that only version 1 listed. The six shared chunks stay, because version 2 still leads to them.

Animation 10.1 plays the run, with the registry’s chunks on the left and those on thor-1 on the right.

Collection and pushes at the same time. A push stores chunks first and the manifest and tag last. A collection that runs between those steps sees chunks that no tag leads to yet and removes them, and the push then completes with a manifest that lists missing chunks. Real registries prevent this either by stopping pushes during a collection or by sweeping only blobs older than some age, long enough for any push in progress to finish.

What the control plane gains

Content addressing is useful to every part of the system built in the earlier chapters.

A GpuJob that names its image and its model by digest runs the same bytes on every worker of a gang, which chapter 7’s synchronous step depends on. The checkpoint store of chapter 4 can be one of these: a checkpoint is an artifact, its chunks that did not change since the last checkpoint are not written again, and the epoch check sits on the tag, which is the only thing that is overwritten. And a machine that returns from a reimage, the last rung of chapter 4’s ladder, refills its local store by pulling, and the verification on every chunk doubles as a test of its new disk.

The complete program

Listing 10.11 The complete program in Go. code/go/cas/main.go

// Command cas builds a small content-addressed store for model artifacts and
// runs it through the situations such a store exists for.
//
// A blob is stored under the SHA-256 digest of its bytes. An artifact is cut
// into chunks, each chunk is a blob, and a manifest lists the chunks. The
// program pushes two versions of a model that differ in their last quarter,
// pulls the second to a machine that has the first, corrupts one stored
// chunk, and removes what nothing refers to any more.
//
// Run it from code/go with:
//
//	go run ./cas
package main

import (
	"crypto/sha256"
	"encoding/hex"
	"errors"
	"fmt"
	"strings"
)

// Digest names a blob: "sha256:" followed by the hash of its bytes in hex.
type Digest string

// digestOf computes the name of some bytes.
func digestOf(data []byte) Digest {
	sum := sha256.Sum256(data)

	return Digest("sha256:" + hex.EncodeToString(sum[:]))
}

// short returns the first twelve hex digits, for printing.
func (d Digest) short() string { return string(d)[:19] }

// Store holds blobs by digest and tags by name.
type Store struct {
	// blobs maps each digest to the bytes it names.
	blobs map[Digest][]byte
	// tags maps a name people use, such as "llm:v1", to a manifest's digest.
	tags map[string]Digest
}

// NewStore returns an empty store.
func NewStore() *Store {
	return &Store{blobs: map[Digest][]byte{}, tags: map[string]Digest{}}
}

// Put stores a blob and returns its digest and whether it was new. Bytes the
// store already holds are not stored a second time.
func (s *Store) Put(data []byte) (Digest, bool) {
	d := digestOf(data)

	if _, held := s.blobs[d]; held {
		return d, false
	}

	s.blobs[d] = data

	return d, true
}

// ErrCorrupt is returned when a blob's bytes no longer match its name.
var ErrCorrupt = errors.New("contents do not match the digest")

// Get returns a blob after checking that its bytes still hash to its name.
func (s *Store) Get(d Digest) ([]byte, error) {
	data, held := s.blobs[d]

	if !held {
		return nil, fmt.Errorf("%s: not found", d.short())
	}

	if digestOf(data) != d {
		return nil, fmt.Errorf("%s: %w", d.short(), ErrCorrupt)
	}

	return data, nil
}


// chunkSize is the size of the pieces an artifact is cut into.
const chunkSize = 8 * 1024

// Push cuts an artifact into chunks, stores each chunk, stores a manifest
// that lists the chunks' digests in order, and points the tag at the
// manifest. It returns how many chunks were new to the store.
func (s *Store) Push(tag string, artifact []byte) (manifest Digest, chunks, added int) {
	var lines strings.Builder

	for start := 0; start < len(artifact); start += chunkSize {
		chunk := artifact[start:min(start+chunkSize, len(artifact))]
		d, fresh := s.Put(chunk)

		if fresh {
			added++
		}

		chunks++
		fmt.Fprintf(&lines, "%s %d\n", d, len(chunk))
	}

	manifest, _ = s.Put([]byte(lines.String()))
	s.tags[tag] = manifest

	return manifest, chunks, added
}

// chunksOf reads a manifest and returns the digests it lists.
func (s *Store) chunksOf(manifest Digest) ([]Digest, error) {
	data, err := s.Get(manifest)

	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	var out []Digest

	for line := range strings.Lines(string(data)) {
		digest, _, _ := strings.Cut(line, " ")
		out = append(out, Digest(digest))
	}

	return out, nil
}


// Pull copies a tagged artifact from a remote store into this one, fetching
// only the chunks this store does not hold. It returns the number of chunks
// and bytes fetched.
func (s *Store) Pull(remote *Store, tag string) (fetched, bytes int, err error) {
	manifest := remote.tags[tag]
	chunks, err := remote.chunksOf(manifest)

	if err != nil {
		return 0, 0, err
	}

	for _, d := range chunks {
		if _, held := s.blobs[d]; held {
			continue
		}

		data, err := remote.Get(d)

		if err != nil {
			return fetched, bytes, err
		}

		s.blobs[d] = data
		fetched++
		bytes += len(data)
	}

	data, _ := remote.Get(manifest)
	s.blobs[manifest] = data
	s.tags[tag] = manifest

	return fetched, bytes, nil
}
