// Read reassembles a tagged artifact, verifying every chunk on the way.
func (s *Store) Read(tag string) ([]byte, error) {
	chunks, err := s.chunksOf(s.tags[tag])

	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	var artifact []byte

	for i, d := range chunks {
		data, err := s.Get(d)

		if err != nil {
			return nil, fmt.Errorf("chunk %d: %w", i, err)
		}

		artifact = append(artifact, data...)
	}

	return artifact, nil
}


// Collect removes every blob that no tag leads to. It first marks the
// manifests the tags name and the chunks those manifests list, then sweeps
// the rest. It returns the number of blobs and bytes removed.
func (s *Store) Collect() (removed, bytes int) {
	live := map[Digest]bool{}

	for _, manifest := range s.tags {
		live[manifest] = true
		chunks, _ := s.chunksOf(manifest)

		for _, d := range chunks {
			live[d] = true
		}
	}

	for d, data := range s.blobs {
		if !live[d] {
			removed++
			bytes += len(data)
			delete(s.blobs, d)
		}
	}

	return removed, bytes
}


// size returns the number of blobs in a store and their total size.
func (s *Store) size() (blobs, bytes int) {
	for _, data := range s.blobs {
		bytes += len(data)
	}

	return len(s.blobs), bytes
}

// weights returns n bytes that stand for a model's weights. The bytes come
// from a fixed sequence, so every run and both languages produce the same.
func weights(n int, seed uint32) []byte {
	out := make([]byte, n)
	x := seed

	for i := range out {
		x = x*1664525 + 1013904223
		out[i] = byte(x >> 24)
	}

	return out
}

func main() {
	// Version 1 is 64 KiB. Version 2 is a fine-tuned copy: the first three
	// quarters are unchanged and the last quarter is new.
	v1 := weights(64*1024, 1)
	v2 := append(append([]byte{}, v1[:48*1024]...), weights(16*1024, 2)...)

	registry := NewStore()
	m1, chunks, added := registry.Push("llm:v1", v1)
	fmt.Printf("push llm:v1: %d chunks, %d new; manifest %s\n", chunks, added, m1.short())
	m2, chunks, added := registry.Push("llm:v2", v2)
	fmt.Printf("push llm:v2: %d chunks, %d new; manifest %s\n", chunks, added, m2.short())
	blobs, bytes := registry.size()
	fmt.Printf("the registry holds %d blobs and %d bytes for %d bytes of artifacts\n", blobs, bytes, len(v1)+len(v2))

	node := NewStore()
	fetched, n, _ := node.Pull(registry, "llm:v1")
	fmt.Printf("thor-1 pulls llm:v1: %d chunks fetched, %d bytes\n", fetched, n)
	fetched, n, _ = node.Pull(registry, "llm:v2")
	fmt.Printf("thor-1 pulls llm:v2: %d chunks fetched, %d bytes\n", fetched, n)

	fmt.Println("one bit flips in a chunk stored on thor-1:")
	chunkList, _ := node.chunksOf(node.tags["llm:v1"])
	damaged := append([]byte{}, node.blobs[chunkList[3]]...)
	damaged[100] ^= 1
	node.blobs[chunkList[3]] = damaged

	if _, err := node.Read("llm:v1"); err != nil {
		fmt.Printf("  read llm:v1: %v\n", err)
	}

	// The bad chunk is dropped and pulled again, by name, from the registry.
	delete(node.blobs, chunkList[3])
	fetched, n, _ = node.Pull(registry, "llm:v1")
	fmt.Printf("  thor-1 drops the chunk and pulls llm:v1 again: %d chunk fetched, %d bytes\n", fetched, n)

	if got, err := node.Read("llm:v1"); err == nil {
		fmt.Printf("  read llm:v1: %d bytes, digest %s\n", len(got), digestOf(got).short())
	}

	fmt.Println("the tag llm:v1 is deleted from the registry:")
	delete(registry.tags, "llm:v1")
	removed, n := registry.Collect()
	blobs, bytes = registry.size()
	fmt.Printf("  collected %d blobs, %d bytes; %d blobs and %d bytes remain\n", removed, n, blobs, bytes)
}

Listing 10.12 The complete program in Rust. code/rust/cas/src/main.rs

//! Builds a small content-addressed store for model artifacts and runs it
//! through the situations such a store exists for.
//!
//! A blob is stored under the SHA-256 digest of its bytes. An artifact is cut
//! into chunks, each chunk is a blob, and a manifest lists the chunks. The
//! program pushes two versions of a model that differ in their last quarter,
//! pulls the second to a machine that has the first, corrupts one stored
//! chunk, and removes what nothing refers to any more.
//!
//! Run it from code/rust with:
//!
//! ```text
//! cargo run -p cas
//! ```

use std::collections::{BTreeMap, BTreeSet};
use std::fmt::Write as _;

use sha2::{Digest as _, Sha256};

/// Names a blob: "sha256:" followed by the hash of its bytes in hex.
#[derive(Clone, PartialEq, Eq, PartialOrd, Ord)]
struct Digest(String);

impl Digest {
    /// Computes the name of some bytes.
    fn of(data: &[u8]) -> Self {
        let mut name = String::from("sha256:");

        for byte in Sha256::digest(data) {
            write!(name, "{byte:02x}").expect("writing to a String");
        }

        Digest(name)
    }

    /// Returns the first twelve hex digits, for printing.
    fn short(&self) -> &str {
        &self.0[..19]
    }
}

/// The ways reading from a store can fail.
#[derive(Debug)]
enum StoreError {
    /// The store holds no blob of this name.
    NotFound(String),
    /// A blob's bytes no longer match its name.
    Corrupt(String),
    /// A chunk of an artifact could not be read.
    Chunk(usize, Box<StoreError>),
}

impl std::fmt::Display for StoreError {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut std::fmt::Formatter<'_>) -> std::fmt::Result {
        match self {
            StoreError::NotFound(d) => write!(f, "{d}: not found"),
            StoreError::Corrupt(d) => write!(f, "{d}: contents do not match the digest"),
            StoreError::Chunk(i, e) => write!(f, "chunk {i}: {e}"),
        }
    }
}

/// Holds blobs by digest and tags by name.
#[derive(Default)]
struct Store {
    /// Maps each digest to the bytes it names.
    blobs: BTreeMap<Digest, Vec<u8>>,
    /// Maps a name people use, such as "llm:v1", to a manifest's digest.
    tags: BTreeMap<&'static str, Digest>,
}

impl Store {
    /// Stores a blob and returns its digest and whether it was new. Bytes the
    /// store already holds are not stored a second time.
    fn put(&mut self, data: &[u8]) -> (Digest, bool) {
        let d = Digest::of(data);

        if self.blobs.contains_key(&d) {
            return (d, false);
        }

        self.blobs.insert(d.clone(), data.to_vec());

        (d, true)
    }

    /// Returns a blob after checking that its bytes still hash to its name.
    fn get(&self, d: &Digest) -> Result<&[u8], StoreError> {
        let data = self
            .blobs
            .get(d)
            .ok_or_else(|| StoreError::NotFound(d.short().to_owned()))?;
        if Digest::of(data) != *d {
            return Err(StoreError::Corrupt(d.short().to_owned()));
        }

        Ok(data)
    }
}

impl Store {
    /// Cuts an artifact into chunks, stores each chunk, stores a manifest that
    /// lists the chunks' digests in order, and points the tag at the manifest.
    /// Returns the manifest's digest, the number of chunks, and how many were
    /// new to the store.
    fn push(&mut self, tag: &'static str, artifact: &[u8]) -> (Digest, usize, usize) {
        let mut lines = String::new();
        let (mut chunks, mut added) = (0, 0);

        for chunk in artifact.chunks(CHUNK_SIZE) {
            let (d, fresh) = self.put(chunk);
            added += usize::from(fresh);
            chunks += 1;
            writeln!(lines, "{} {}", d.0, chunk.len()).expect("writing to a String");
        }

        let (manifest, _) = self.put(lines.as_bytes());
        self.tags.insert(tag, manifest.clone());

        (manifest, chunks, added)
    }

    /// Reads a manifest and returns the digests it lists.
    fn chunks_of(&self, manifest: &Digest) -> Result<Vec<Digest>, StoreError> {
        let text = String::from_utf8_lossy(self.get(manifest)?);
        Ok(text
            .lines()
            .filter_map(|line| line.split(' ').next())
            .map(|d| Digest(d.to_owned()))
            .collect())
    }
}

impl Store {
    /// Copies a tagged artifact from a remote store into this one, fetching
    /// only the chunks this store does not hold. Returns the number of chunks
    /// and bytes fetched.
    fn pull(&mut self, remote: &Store, tag: &'static str) -> Result<(usize, usize), StoreError> {
        let manifest = &remote.tags[tag];
        let (mut fetched, mut bytes) = (0, 0);

        for d in remote.chunks_of(manifest)? {
            if self.blobs.contains_key(&d) {
                continue;
            }

            let data = remote.get(&d)?;
            fetched += 1;
            bytes += data.len();
            self.blobs.insert(d, data.to_vec());
        }

        self.blobs
            .insert(manifest.clone(), remote.get(manifest)?.to_vec());
        self.tags.insert(tag, manifest.clone());

        Ok((fetched, bytes))
    }

    /// Reassembles a tagged artifact, verifying every chunk on the way.
    fn read(&self, tag: &str) -> Result<Vec<u8>, StoreError> {
        let mut artifact = Vec::new();

        for (i, d) in self.chunks_of(&self.tags[tag])?.iter().enumerate() {
            let data = self.get(d).map_err(|e| StoreError::Chunk(i, Box::new(e)))?;
            artifact.extend_from_slice(data);
        }

        Ok(artifact)
    }
}

impl Store {
    /// Removes every blob that no tag leads to. It first marks the manifests
    /// the tags name and the chunks those manifests list, then sweeps the
    /// rest. Returns the number of blobs and bytes removed.
    fn collect(&mut self) -> (usize, usize) {
        let mut live = BTreeSet::new();

        for manifest in self.tags.values() {
            live.extend(self.chunks_of(manifest).unwrap_or_default());
            live.insert(manifest.clone());
        }

        let (before, bytes_before) = self.size();
        self.blobs.retain(|d, _| live.contains(d));
        let (after, bytes_after) = self.size();

        (before - after, bytes_before - bytes_after)
    }
}

impl Store {
    /// Returns the number of blobs in the store and their total size.
    fn size(&self) -> (usize, usize) {
        (self.blobs.len(), self.blobs.values().map(Vec::len).sum())
    }
}

/// The size of the pieces an artifact is cut into.
const CHUNK_SIZE: usize = 8 * 1024;

/// Returns n bytes that stand for a model's weights. The bytes come from a
/// fixed sequence, so every run and both languages produce the same.
fn weights(n: usize, seed: u32) -> Vec<u8> {
    let mut x = seed;
    (0..n)
        .map(|_| {
            x = x.wrapping_mul(1_664_525).wrapping_add(1_013_904_223);
            (x >> 24) as u8
        })
        .collect()
}

fn main() -> Result<(), StoreError> {
    // Version 1 is 64 KiB. Version 2 is a fine-tuned copy: the first three
    // quarters are unchanged and the last quarter is new.
    let v1 = weights(64 * 1024, 1);
    let v2 = [&v1[..48 * 1024], &weights(16 * 1024, 2)].concat();

    let mut registry = Store::default();
    let (m1, chunks, added) = registry.push("llm:v1", &v1);
    println!(
        "push llm:v1: {chunks} chunks, {added} new; manifest {}",
        m1.short()
    );
    let (m2, chunks, added) = registry.push("llm:v2", &v2);
    println!(
        "push llm:v2: {chunks} chunks, {added} new; manifest {}",
        m2.short()
    );
    let (blobs, bytes) = registry.size();
    println!(
        "the registry holds {blobs} blobs and {bytes} bytes for {} bytes of artifacts",
        v1.len() + v2.len()
    );

    let mut node = Store::default();
    let (fetched, n) = node.pull(&registry, "llm:v1")?;
    println!("thor-1 pulls llm:v1: {fetched} chunks fetched, {n} bytes");
    let (fetched, n) = node.pull(&registry, "llm:v2")?;
    println!("thor-1 pulls llm:v2: {fetched} chunks fetched, {n} bytes");

    println!("one bit flips in a chunk stored on thor-1:");
    let fourth = node.chunks_of(&node.tags["llm:v1"])?[3].clone();
    node.blobs.get_mut(&fourth).expect("the chunk was pulled")[100] ^= 1;

    if let Err(e) = node.read("llm:v1") {
        println!("  read llm:v1: {e}");
    }

    // The bad chunk is dropped and pulled again, by name, from the registry.
    node.blobs.remove(&fourth);
    let (fetched, n) = node.pull(&registry, "llm:v1")?;
    println!("  thor-1 drops the chunk and pulls llm:v1 again: {fetched} chunk fetched, {n} bytes");
    let got = node.read("llm:v1")?;
    println!(
        "  read llm:v1: {} bytes, digest {}",
        got.len(),
        Digest::of(&got).short()
    );

    println!("the tag llm:v1 is deleted from the registry:");
    registry.tags.remove("llm:v1");
    let (removed, n) = registry.collect();
    let (blobs, bytes) = registry.size();
    println!("  collected {removed} blobs, {n} bytes; {blobs} blobs and {bytes} bytes remain");

    Ok(())
}

Summary

  • A name that a person chooses says nothing about the bytes behind it. A digest is computed from the bytes, so it identifies them, lets any reader verify them, and makes identical data one blob.
  • containerd stores container images as blobs named by SHA-256 digest, and an image can be run by digest.
  • A large artifact is stored as chunks and a manifest that lists their digests. Versions that share data share chunks.
  • A pull fetches only the chunks a machine lacks, and every read verifies what it returns.
  • Space is reclaimed by marking everything the tags lead to and sweeping the rest.

References

  • Open Container Initiative, Image Format Specification: https://github.com/opencontainers/image-spec/blob/main/spec.md
  • Open Container Initiative, Distribution Specification: https://github.com/opencontainers/distribution-spec/blob/main/spec.md
  • containerd, Content flow: https://github.com/containerd/containerd/blob/main/docs/content-flow.md
  • Kubernetes, Images: https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/containers/images/
  • NIST, FIPS 180-4, Secure Hash Standard: https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/180-4/upd1/final

11. A command-line tool for Windows, macOS, and Linux

Everything we have built so far runs inside the cluster or next to it, on Linux. The people who use the cluster sit somewhere else. A researcher submits jobs from a MacBook, an operator checks nodes from a Windows laptop, and a build pipeline does both from a Linux container. What they all run is a command-line tool, and it is the one piece of a GPU platform that has to work on three operating systems.

In this chapter, we will write that tool. gpuctl is small: it lists the nodes of a cluster with their GPUs, reports where it keeps its files, and prints its version. We will first decide how a command-line tool should behave so that both people and scripts can use it, then write it in Go and in Rust, and then build it for the three systems from our Jetson and see where each language makes that easy and where it does not.

This chapter covers

  • what a command line promises: output, messages, and an exit status
  • where a tool’s files belong on Linux, macOS, and Windows
  • reading the cluster’s nodes with client-go and with kube-rs
  • a table for people and JSON for programs, from the same data
  • building for three operating systems from one machine with Go
  • what stops the same build in Rust, and how projects get around it

What a command line promises

A command-line tool has two kinds of user. A person reads what it prints. A script runs it, captures what it prints, and decides what to do next. A tool that serves both keeps three channels apart.

  • Standard output carries the result and nothing else. A script that runs gpuctl nodes -o json and feeds the output to another program must receive only JSON, with no progress messages or warnings mixed in.
  • Standard error carries everything meant for a person: errors, warnings, and help text shown after a mistake.
  • The exit status is a small number the tool returns to whatever started it. By convention 0 means success. A script checks the status before it trusts the output.

gpuctl uses three statuses. 0 is success. 1 means the command was understood and failed, for example because the cluster could not be reached. 2 means the command line itself was wrong. The distinction lets a script retry a failure of the first kind and stop at once on the second, since a mistyped command stays mistyped on the next attempt.

Figure 11.1 shows the tool’s shape.

Low-level design: gpuctl

CommandPrints to standard outputFails with
gpuctl nodes [-o table|json]one row per node: name, GPUs, architecture, operating system, kubelet version1 if the cluster cannot be reached
gpuctl configthe paths of its configuration file and of the kubeconfig1 if the home directory is unknown
gpuctl versionthe version and the platform
anything elsenothing; the usage text goes to standard error2
FunctionDoes
configFile(), kubeconfig()work out the two paths for the operating system the tool runs on
listNodes()one list request to the API server, reduced to the fields printed
printNodes(rows, format)writes a table or JSON
run(args)chooses the command and returns the exit status

Where a tool’s files live

A tool that remembers settings needs a place to keep them, and each operating system has its own rule for where that is.

SystemDirectory for a user’s settingsExample for gpuctl
Linux$XDG_CONFIG_HOME, or ~/.config if it is unset/home/ana/.config/gpuctl/config.toml
macOS~/Library/Application Support/Users/ana/Library/Application Support/gpuctl/config.toml
Windowsthe folder named by %AppData%C:\Users\ana\AppData\Roaming\gpuctl\config.toml

Two details in this table catch programs that were written on one system and run on another. The directory differs, so a path built by joining the home directory and .config is wrong on two of the three. And the separator between the parts of a path differs: Windows uses a backslash. Both languages provide a path type that joins parts with the right separator, filepath.Join in Go and PathBuf::join in Rust, and a portable program builds every path with it and writes no separator of its own.

Go’s standard library knows the table: os.UserConfigDir returns the right directory on each system. Rust’s standard library has no such function, so the Rust version writes the three cases out. cfg!(windows) and cfg!(target_os = "macos") are decided when the program is compiled, so each build contains only its own case.

Listing 11.1 The configuration file and the kubeconfig, in Go. code/go/gpuctl/main.go

// configFile returns the path of gpuctl's own configuration file. The
// directory is the one each operating system sets aside for a user's
// settings.
func configFile() (string, error) {
	dir, err := os.UserConfigDir()

	if err != nil {
		return "", err
	}

	return filepath.Join(dir, "gpuctl", "config.toml"), nil
}

// kubeconfig returns the file that names the cluster and the credentials:
// the KUBECONFIG environment variable if it is set, and .kube/config in the
// user's home directory otherwise.
func kubeconfig() string {
	return clientcmd.NewDefaultClientConfigLoadingRules().GetDefaultFilename()
}

Listing 11.2 The configuration file and the kubeconfig, in Rust. code/rust/gpuctl/src/main.rs

/// Returns the path of gpuctl's own configuration file. The directory is the
/// one each operating system sets aside for a user's settings.
fn config_file() -> Option<PathBuf> {
    let dir = if cfg!(windows) {
        PathBuf::from(env::var_os("AppData")?)
    } else if cfg!(target_os = "macos") {
        env::home_dir()?.join("Library").join("Application Support")
    } else {
        match env::var_os("XDG_CONFIG_HOME") {
            Some(dir) if !dir.is_empty() => PathBuf::from(dir),
            _ => env::home_dir()?.join(".config"),
        }
    };

    Some(dir.join("gpuctl").join("config.toml"))
}

/// Returns the file that names the cluster and the credentials: the
/// KUBECONFIG environment variable if it is set, and .kube/config in the
/// user's home directory otherwise.
fn kubeconfig() -> Option<PathBuf> {
    match env::var_os("KUBECONFIG") {
        Some(path) if !path.is_empty() => Some(PathBuf::from(path)),
        _ => Some(env::home_dir()?.join(".kube").join("config")),
    }
}

The second path is the kubeconfig, the file from which kubectl and every other client reads the address of the API server and the user’s credentials. Its location follows one rule on all three systems: the KUBECONFIG environment variable if it is set, and .kube/config under the home directory otherwise. gpuctl reads the same file, so it talks to whichever cluster kubectl talks to. The Go version asks client-go’s loading rules for the path. The Rust version applies the rule itself.

Reading the nodes

gpuctl nodes makes one request, a list of Node objects, which we met in chapter 1, and keeps five fields of each: the name, the allocatable nvidia.com/gpu count from chapter 5, and the architecture, operating system, and kubelet version that the kubelet reports about its machine.

Listing 11.3 Listing the nodes and keeping five fields, in Go. code/go/gpuctl/main.go

// NodeInfo is one row of "gpuctl nodes".
type NodeInfo struct {
	Name    string `json:"name"`
	GPUs    int64  `json:"gpus"`
	Arch    string `json:"arch"`
	OS      string `json:"os"`
	Kubelet string `json:"kubelet"`
}

// listNodes asks the API server for every node and keeps the fields the
// command prints.
func listNodes(ctx context.Context) ([]NodeInfo, error) {
	rules := clientcmd.NewDefaultClientConfigLoadingRules()
	config, err := clientcmd.NewNonInteractiveDeferredLoadingClientConfig(rules, nil).ClientConfig()

	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	clientset, err := kubernetes.NewForConfig(config)

	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	nodes, err := clientset.CoreV1().Nodes().List(ctx, metav1.ListOptions{})

	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	rows := make([]NodeInfo, 0, len(nodes.Items))

	for _, n := range nodes.Items {
		gpus := n.Status.Allocatable["nvidia.com/gpu"]
		rows = append(rows, NodeInfo{
			Name:    n.Name,
			GPUs:    gpus.Value(),
			Arch:    n.Status.NodeInfo.Architecture,
			OS:      n.Status.NodeInfo.OperatingSystem,
			Kubelet: n.Status.NodeInfo.KubeletVersion,
		})
	}

	return rows, nil
}

Listing 11.4 Listing the nodes and keeping five fields, in Rust. code/rust/gpuctl/src/main.rs

/// One row of "gpuctl nodes".
#[derive(Serialize)]
struct NodeInfo {
    name: String,
    gpus: i64,
    arch: String,
    os: String,
    kubelet: String,
}

/// Asks the API server for every node and keeps the fields the command
/// prints.
async fn list_nodes() -> Result<Vec<NodeInfo>, kube::Error> {
    let client = Client::try_default().await?;
    let nodes = Api::<Node>::all(client)
        .list(&ListParams::default())
        .await?;
    Ok(nodes
        .into_iter()
        .map(|node| {
            let status = node.status.unwrap_or_default();
            let info = status.node_info.unwrap_or_default();
            let gpus = status
                .allocatable
                .and_then(|resources| resources.get("nvidia.com/gpu")?.0.parse().ok())
                .unwrap_or(0);
            NodeInfo {
                name: node.metadata.name.unwrap_or_default(),
                gpus,
                arch: info.architecture,
                os: info.operating_system,
                kubelet: info.kubelet_version,
            }
        })
        .collect())
}

The Go version builds its client from the kubeconfig with clientcmd, the package of client-go that implements the loading rule. Earlier programs used controller-runtime’s GetConfigOrDie for this. A command-line tool wants the error back as a value, so that it can print it and choose the exit status. In the Rust version, Client::try_default does the same loading and returns a Result.

Kubernetes stores a resource amount as a quantity, a number with an optional unit suffix such as 16031232Ki. A GPU count is a plain whole number. The Go version reads it with the quantity’s Value method. In k8s-openapi a quantity is a wrapper around the string the API server sent, so the Rust version parses that string as an integer, and a node with no GPUs, which has no such entry at all, counts as zero.

One result, two forms

The rows are printed in one of two forms, chosen by the -o flag.

Listing 11.5 A table or JSON, in Go. code/go/gpuctl/main.go

// printNodes writes the rows as a table for a person or as JSON for a program.
func printNodes(out io.Writer, rows []NodeInfo, format string) error {
	if format == "json" {
		enc := json.NewEncoder(out)
		enc.SetIndent("", "  ")

		return enc.Encode(rows)
	}

	table := tabwriter.NewWriter(out, 0, 0, 2, ' ', 0)
	fmt.Fprintln(table, "NAME\tGPUS\tARCH\tOS\tKUBELET")

	for _, r := range rows {
		fmt.Fprintf(table, "%s\t%d\t%s\t%s\t%s\n", r.Name, r.GPUs, r.Arch, r.OS, r.Kubelet)
	}

	return table.Flush()
}

Listing 11.6 A table or JSON, in Rust. code/rust/gpuctl/src/main.rs

/// Writes the rows as a table for a person or as JSON for a program.
fn print_nodes(rows: &[NodeInfo], json: bool) -> serde_json::Result<()> {
    if json {
        println!("{}", serde_json::to_string_pretty(rows)?);

        return Ok(());
    }

    let header = ["NAME", "GPUS", "ARCH", "OS", "KUBELET"].map(String::from);
    let table: Vec<[String; 5]> = std::iter::once(header)
        .chain(rows.iter().map(|r| {
            [
                r.name.clone(),
                r.gpus.to_string(),
                r.arch.clone(),
                r.os.clone(),
                r.kubelet.clone(),
            ]
        }))
        .collect();
    // Each column is as wide as its longest cell, plus two spaces.
    let widths: [usize; 5] =
        std::array::from_fn(|col| table.iter().map(|row| row[col].len()).max().unwrap_or(0));

    for row in &table {
        let line: String = (0..5)
            .map(|col| format!("{:<width$}", row[col], width = widths[col] + 2))
            .collect();
        println!("{}", line.trim_end());
    }

    Ok(())
}

The table is for reading. Its columns are padded so that they line up, which Go’s text/tabwriter does from tab-separated cells and the Rust version does by measuring each column’s widest cell.

The JSON is for programs, and it is part of the tool’s interface in a way the table is not. A script that reads gpus from each object keeps working when a later version adds a column to the table or changes its spacing. For that reason the field names are written out in both versions, as struct tags in Go and as the field names of a Serialize struct in Rust, and they would not be renamed lightly.

Commands and exit statuses

run ties the pieces together. It looks at the first argument, carries out the command, and returns the status. Every path through it ends in one of the three statuses, and every message for a person goes to standard error.

Listing 11.7 Choosing the command and returning the status, in Go. code/go/gpuctl/main.go

// Exit statuses. A script that calls gpuctl tells a failed command from a
// mistyped one by the status alone.
const (
	exitOK      = 0
	exitFailure = 1
	exitUsage   = 2
)

const usage = `usage:
  gpuctl nodes [-o table|json]
  gpuctl config
  gpuctl version
`

Listing 11.8 Choosing the command and returning the status, in Rust. code/rust/gpuctl/src/main.rs

/// The exit status for a command line that is wrong. A script that calls
/// gpuctl tells a failed command from a mistyped one by the status alone:
/// success is 0 and failure is 1, which `ExitCode` already names.
const EXIT_USAGE: u8 = 2;

const USAGE: &str = "usage:
  gpuctl nodes [-o table|json]
  gpuctl config
  gpuctl version
";

Listing 11.9 The run function and main, in Go. code/go/gpuctl/main.go

// run carries out one command line and returns the exit status. It takes its
// arguments and its two output streams as parameters, so a test can call it
// without starting a process.
func run(args []string, out, errOut io.Writer) int {
	if len(args) == 0 {
		fmt.Fprint(errOut, usage)

		return exitUsage
	}

	switch args[0] {
	case "version":
		fmt.Fprintf(out, "gpuctl %s %s/%s\n", version, runtime.GOOS, runtime.GOARCH)
	case "config":
		file, err := configFile()

		if err != nil {
			fmt.Fprintln(errOut, "gpuctl:", err)

			return exitFailure
		}

		fmt.Fprintf(out, "config file  %s\nkubeconfig   %s\n", file, kubeconfig())
	case "nodes":
		flags := flag.NewFlagSet("nodes", flag.ContinueOnError)
		flags.SetOutput(io.Discard)
		format := flags.String("o", "table", "output format: table or json")

		if err := flags.Parse(args[1:]); err != nil || (*format != "table" && *format != "json") {
			fmt.Fprint(errOut, usage)

			return exitUsage
		}

		rows, err := listNodes(context.Background())

		if err == nil {
			err = printNodes(out, rows, *format)
		}

		if err != nil {
			fmt.Fprintln(errOut, "gpuctl:", err)

			return exitFailure
		}
	default:
		fmt.Fprintf(errOut, "gpuctl: unknown command %q\n%s", args[0], usage)

		return exitUsage
	}

	return exitOK
}

func main() {
	os.Exit(run(os.Args[1:], os.Stdout, os.Stderr))
}

Listing 11.10 The run function and main, in Rust. code/rust/gpuctl/src/main.rs

/// Carries out one command line and returns the exit status.
async fn run(args: &[String]) -> ExitCode {
    let args: Vec<&str> = args.iter().map(String::as_str).collect();

    match args.as_slice() {
        ["version"] => println!("gpuctl {} {}", env!("CARGO_PKG_VERSION"), platform()),
        ["config"] => {
            let (Some(file), Some(kube)) = (config_file(), kubeconfig()) else {
                eprintln!("gpuctl: cannot find the home directory");

                return ExitCode::FAILURE;
            };
            println!(
                "config file  {}\nkubeconfig   {}",
                file.display(),
                kube.display()
            );
        }
        ["nodes"] | ["nodes", "-o", "table" | "json"] => {
            let json = args.last() == Some(&"json");
            let rows = match list_nodes().await {
                Ok(rows) => rows,
                Err(e) => {
                    eprintln!("gpuctl: {e}");

                    return ExitCode::FAILURE;
                }
            };

            if let Err(e) = print_nodes(&rows, json) {
                eprintln!("gpuctl: {e}");

                return ExitCode::FAILURE;
            }
        }
        [] | ["nodes", ..] => {
            eprint!("{USAGE}");

            return ExitCode::from(EXIT_USAGE);
        }
        [other, ..] => {
            eprint!("gpuctl: unknown command \"{other}\"\n{USAGE}");

            return ExitCode::from(EXIT_USAGE);
        }
    }

    ExitCode::SUCCESS
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> ExitCode {
    let args: Vec<String> = env::args().skip(1).collect();

    run(&args).await
}

The Go version parses the -o flag with the standard flag package and tells it to stay silent, so that run prints one consistent usage text for every kind of mistake. The Rust version matches the whole argument list against patterns. The pattern ["nodes", "-o", "table" | "json"] accepts exactly the two valid forms, and any other list that starts with nodes falls through to the usage arm.

Here is the tool on our cluster. Both versions print exactly this:

$ gpuctl nodes
NAME                   GPUS  ARCH   OS     KUBELET
gpu-lab-control-plane  1     arm64  linux  v1.37.0
$ gpuctl nodes -o json
[
  {
    "name": "gpu-lab-control-plane",
    "gpus": 1,
    "arch": "arm64",
    "os": "linux",
    "kubelet": "v1.37.0"
  }
]
$ gpuctl config
config file  /home/jetson/.config/gpuctl/config.toml
kubeconfig   /home/jetson/.kube/config
$ gpuctl version
gpuctl 0.1.0 linux/arm64
$ gpuctl pods
gpuctl: unknown command "pods"
usage:
  gpuctl nodes [-o table|json]
  gpuctl config
  gpuctl version
$ echo $?
2

The table and the JSON hold the same row: our one node, with its one GPU, on arm64. The unknown command prints its message and the usage text on standard error and exits with 2. When the cluster cannot be reached, each version prints the error its client library produced and exits with 1. The Go version prints:

$ KUBECONFIG=/nonexistent gpuctl nodes
gpuctl: invalid configuration: no configuration has been provided, try setting KUBERNETES_MASTER environment variable
$ echo $?
1

Animation 11.1 shows three of these runs, each with its two streams and its status.

Building for three systems

A program that runs on our Jetson is a file of ARM machine code in the format Linux loads, called ELF. A Windows laptop with an Intel processor needs different machine code in a different format, PE, and a MacBook needs ARM code in a third, Mach-O. Producing a program for a system other than the one the compiler runs on is called cross-compilation. Figure 11.2 shows what we are after.

In Go

The Go compiler and linker are written in Go and contain the code generators for every platform Go supports. Two environment variables select the target: GOOS names the operating system and GOARCH the processor. On our Jetson:

$ GOOS=windows GOARCH=amd64 go build -o gpuctl-windows-amd64 ./gpuctl
$ GOOS=darwin  GOARCH=arm64 go build -o gpuctl-darwin-arm64  ./gpuctl
$ GOOS=linux   GOARCH=amd64 go build -o gpuctl-linux-amd64   ./gpuctl
$ file gpuctl-*
gpuctl-darwin-arm64:  Mach-O 64-bit arm64 executable, flags:<|DYLDLINK|PIE>
gpuctl-linux-amd64:   ELF 64-bit LSB executable, x86-64, version 1 (SYSV), statically linked
gpuctl-windows-amd64: PE32+ executable (console) x86-64, for MS Windows

Three commands on an ARM Linux board produced programs for Windows, macOS, and Intel Linux. Each file is between 37 and 39 MB and needs nothing else installed: the Go runtime and all of client-go are inside it. This works because gpuctl and everything it imports are written in Go. A Go program that calls C code through cgo loses this property and needs a C compiler for each target.

In Rust

Rust selects a target with a target triple, such as x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu or aarch64-apple-darwin, and rustup target add installs the standard library for one. Our Jetson has these installed:

$ rustup target list --installed
aarch64-apple-darwin
aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu
x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu

The Rust compiler can generate code for all three. The build stops for another reason:

$ cargo check -p gpuctl --target x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu
warning: ring@0.17.14: Compiler family detection failed due to error: ToolNotFound:
failed to find tool "x86_64-linux-gnu-gcc": No such file or directory (os error 2)
error: failed to run custom build command for `ring v0.17.14`

ring is the cryptography library that kube-rs uses, through its TLS library, for its encrypted connection to the API server. Parts of it are written in C and assembly, and its build compiles them with a C compiler for the target. Our board has a C compiler for ARM Linux and none for Intel Linux or for macOS. The same would happen at the last step of any Rust build for another system: Rust calls the target system’s linker to produce the final file, and that linker has to be installed.

So cross-compiling Rust means supplying a C toolchain for each target. Projects handle this in one of two ways. Most build each target on a machine that runs it: a pipeline with one Linux, one macOS, and one Windows runner, each running a plain cargo build --release and uploading its binary. Others install cross toolchains, either one by one or through a tool such as cross, which runs the build inside a container image that has the right toolchain for the target.

A Rust program with no C in its dependencies gets further. fleethealth from chapter 4 uses only the standard library, and cargo check -p fleethealth --target aarch64-apple-darwin passes on our Jetson, which shows that the compiler itself has no difficulty with the target. The difficulty belongs to one dependency with C in it, the same condition under which Go’s cross-compilation stops working.

Two names for one processor. gpuctl version prints linux/arm64 from both versions, and the Rust version has to translate to do so. Go and Kubernetes call the 64-bit ARM architecture arm64 and the 64-bit Intel one amd64. Rust and the Linux kernel call them aarch64 and x86_64. Tools that download a binary for the current machine have to map between the two spellings, and a release that names its files by one convention is found by scripts that expect the other only if someone wrote that mapping down.

The complete program

Listing 11.11 The complete program in Go. code/go/gpuctl/main.go

// Command gpuctl is a small command-line client for a GPU cluster, written to
// build and behave the same on Linux, macOS, and Windows.
//
//	gpuctl nodes [-o table|json]   list the cluster's nodes and their GPUs
//	gpuctl config                  print where gpuctl looks for its files
//	gpuctl version                 print the version and the platform
//
// Data goes to standard output and messages to standard error. The exit
// status is 0 on success, 1 when a command fails, and 2 when the command line
// is wrong.
//
// Run it from code/go with:
//
//	go run ./gpuctl nodes
package main

import (
	"context"
	"encoding/json"
	"flag"
	"fmt"
	"io"
	"os"
	"path/filepath"
	"runtime"
	"text/tabwriter"

	metav1 "k8s.io/apimachinery/pkg/apis/meta/v1"
	"k8s.io/client-go/kubernetes"
	"k8s.io/client-go/tools/clientcmd"
)

// version is the tool's version, printed by "gpuctl version".
const version = "0.1.0"

// Exit statuses. A script that calls gpuctl tells a failed command from a
// mistyped one by the status alone.
const (
	exitOK      = 0
	exitFailure = 1
	exitUsage   = 2
)

const usage = `usage:
  gpuctl nodes [-o table|json]
  gpuctl config
  gpuctl version
`


// configFile returns the path of gpuctl's own configuration file. The
// directory is the one each operating system sets aside for a user's
// settings.
func configFile() (string, error) {
	dir, err := os.UserConfigDir()

	if err != nil {
		return "", err
	}

	return filepath.Join(dir, "gpuctl", "config.toml"), nil
}

// kubeconfig returns the file that names the cluster and the credentials:
// the KUBECONFIG environment variable if it is set, and .kube/config in the
// user's home directory otherwise.
func kubeconfig() string {
	return clientcmd.NewDefaultClientConfigLoadingRules().GetDefaultFilename()
}


// NodeInfo is one row of "gpuctl nodes".
type NodeInfo struct {
	Name    string `json:"name"`
	GPUs    int64  `json:"gpus"`
	Arch    string `json:"arch"`
	OS      string `json:"os"`
	Kubelet string `json:"kubelet"`
}

// listNodes asks the API server for every node and keeps the fields the
// command prints.
func listNodes(ctx context.Context) ([]NodeInfo, error) {
	rules := clientcmd.NewDefaultClientConfigLoadingRules()
	config, err := clientcmd.NewNonInteractiveDeferredLoadingClientConfig(rules, nil).ClientConfig()

	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	clientset, err := kubernetes.NewForConfig(config)

	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	nodes, err := clientset.CoreV1().Nodes().List(ctx, metav1.ListOptions{})

	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}

	rows := make([]NodeInfo, 0, len(nodes.Items))

	for _, n := range nodes.Items {
		gpus := n.Status.Allocatable["nvidia.com/gpu"]
		rows = append(rows, NodeInfo{
			Name:    n.Name,
			GPUs:    gpus.Value(),
			Arch:    n.Status.NodeInfo.Architecture,
			OS:      n.Status.NodeInfo.OperatingSystem,
			Kubelet: n.Status.NodeInfo.KubeletVersion,
		})
	}

	return rows, nil
}


// printNodes writes the rows as a table for a person or as JSON for a program.
func printNodes(out io.Writer, rows []NodeInfo, format string) error {
	if format == "json" {
		enc := json.NewEncoder(out)
		enc.SetIndent("", "  ")

		return enc.Encode(rows)
	}

	table := tabwriter.NewWriter(out, 0, 0, 2, ' ', 0)
	fmt.Fprintln(table, "NAME\tGPUS\tARCH\tOS\tKUBELET")

	for _, r := range rows {
		fmt.Fprintf(table, "%s\t%d\t%s\t%s\t%s\n", r.Name, r.GPUs, r.Arch, r.OS, r.Kubelet)
	}

	return table.Flush()
}


// run carries out one command line and returns the exit status. It takes its
// arguments and its two output streams as parameters, so a test can call it
// without starting a process.
func run(args []string, out, errOut io.Writer) int {
	if len(args) == 0 {
		fmt.Fprint(errOut, usage)

		return exitUsage
	}

	switch args[0] {
	case "version":
		fmt.Fprintf(out, "gpuctl %s %s/%s\n", version, runtime.GOOS, runtime.GOARCH)
	case "config":
		file, err := configFile()

		if err != nil {
			fmt.Fprintln(errOut, "gpuctl:", err)

			return exitFailure
		}

		fmt.Fprintf(out, "config file  %s\nkubeconfig   %s\n", file, kubeconfig())
	case "nodes":
		flags := flag.NewFlagSet("nodes", flag.ContinueOnError)
		flags.SetOutput(io.Discard)
		format := flags.String("o", "table", "output format: table or json")

		if err := flags.Parse(args[1:]); err != nil || (*format != "table" && *format != "json") {
			fmt.Fprint(errOut, usage)

			return exitUsage
		}

		rows, err := listNodes(context.Background())

		if err == nil {
			err = printNodes(out, rows, *format)
		}

		if err != nil {
			fmt.Fprintln(errOut, "gpuctl:", err)

			return exitFailure
		}
	default:
		fmt.Fprintf(errOut, "gpuctl: unknown command %q\n%s", args[0], usage)

		return exitUsage
	}

	return exitOK
}

func main() {
	os.Exit(run(os.Args[1:], os.Stdout, os.Stderr))
}

Listing 11.12 The complete program in Rust. code/rust/gpuctl/src/main.rs

//! A small command-line client for a GPU cluster, written to build and behave
//! the same on Linux, macOS, and Windows.
//!
//! ```text
//! gpuctl nodes [-o table|json]   list the cluster's nodes and their GPUs
//! gpuctl config                  print where gpuctl looks for its files
//! gpuctl version                 print the version and the platform
//! ```
//!
//! Data goes to standard output and messages to standard error. The exit
//! status is 0 on success, 1 when a command fails, and 2 when the command line
//! is wrong.
//!
//! Run it from code/rust with:
//!
//! ```text
//! cargo run -p gpuctl -- nodes
//! ```

use std::env;
use std::path::PathBuf;
use std::process::ExitCode;

use k8s_openapi::api::core::v1::Node;
use kube::{Api, Client, api::ListParams};
use serde::Serialize;

/// The exit status for a command line that is wrong. A script that calls
/// gpuctl tells a failed command from a mistyped one by the status alone:
/// success is 0 and failure is 1, which `ExitCode` already names.
const EXIT_USAGE: u8 = 2;

const USAGE: &str = "usage:
  gpuctl nodes [-o table|json]
  gpuctl config
  gpuctl version
";

/// Returns the path of gpuctl's own configuration file. The directory is the
/// one each operating system sets aside for a user's settings.
fn config_file() -> Option<PathBuf> {
    let dir = if cfg!(windows) {
        PathBuf::from(env::var_os("AppData")?)
    } else if cfg!(target_os = "macos") {
        env::home_dir()?.join("Library").join("Application Support")
    } else {
        match env::var_os("XDG_CONFIG_HOME") {
            Some(dir) if !dir.is_empty() => PathBuf::from(dir),
            _ => env::home_dir()?.join(".config"),
        }
    };

    Some(dir.join("gpuctl").join("config.toml"))
}

/// Returns the file that names the cluster and the credentials: the
/// KUBECONFIG environment variable if it is set, and .kube/config in the
/// user's home directory otherwise.
fn kubeconfig() -> Option<PathBuf> {
    match env::var_os("KUBECONFIG") {
        Some(path) if !path.is_empty() => Some(PathBuf::from(path)),
        _ => Some(env::home_dir()?.join(".kube").join("config")),
    }
}

/// One row of "gpuctl nodes".
#[derive(Serialize)]
struct NodeInfo {
    name: String,
    gpus: i64,
    arch: String,
    os: String,
    kubelet: String,
}

/// Asks the API server for every node and keeps the fields the command
/// prints.
async fn list_nodes() -> Result<Vec<NodeInfo>, kube::Error> {
    let client = Client::try_default().await?;
    let nodes = Api::<Node>::all(client)
        .list(&ListParams::default())
        .await?;
    Ok(nodes
        .into_iter()
        .map(|node| {
            let status = node.status.unwrap_or_default();
            let info = status.node_info.unwrap_or_default();
            let gpus = status
                .allocatable
                .and_then(|resources| resources.get("nvidia.com/gpu")?.0.parse().ok())
                .unwrap_or(0);
            NodeInfo {
                name: node.metadata.name.unwrap_or_default(),
                gpus,
                arch: info.architecture,
                os: info.operating_system,
                kubelet: info.kubelet_version,
            }
        })
        .collect())
}

/// Writes the rows as a table for a person or as JSON for a program.
fn print_nodes(rows: &[NodeInfo], json: bool) -> serde_json::Result<()> {
    if json {
        println!("{}", serde_json::to_string_pretty(rows)?);

        return Ok(());
    }

    let header = ["NAME", "GPUS", "ARCH", "OS", "KUBELET"].map(String::from);
    let table: Vec<[String; 5]> = std::iter::once(header)
        .chain(rows.iter().map(|r| {
            [
                r.name.clone(),
                r.gpus.to_string(),
                r.arch.clone(),
                r.os.clone(),
                r.kubelet.clone(),
            ]
        }))
        .collect();
    // Each column is as wide as its longest cell, plus two spaces.
    let widths: [usize; 5] =
        std::array::from_fn(|col| table.iter().map(|row| row[col].len()).max().unwrap_or(0));

    for row in &table {
        let line: String = (0..5)
            .map(|col| format!("{:<width$}", row[col], width = widths[col] + 2))
            .collect();
        println!("{}", line.trim_end());
    }

    Ok(())
}

/// Returns the platform in the form Go prints it, such as `linux/arm64`.
/// Rust calls the architectures `aarch64` and `x86_64`.
fn platform() -> String {
    let arch = match env::consts::ARCH {
        "aarch64" => "arm64",
        "x86_64" => "amd64",
        other => other,
    };

    format!("{}/{arch}", env::consts::OS)
}

/// Carries out one command line and returns the exit status.
async fn run(args: &[String]) -> ExitCode {
    let args: Vec<&str> = args.iter().map(String::as_str).collect();

    match args.as_slice() {
        ["version"] => println!("gpuctl {} {}", env!("CARGO_PKG_VERSION"), platform()),
        ["config"] => {
            let (Some(file), Some(kube)) = (config_file(), kubeconfig()) else {
                eprintln!("gpuctl: cannot find the home directory");

                return ExitCode::FAILURE;
            };
            println!(
                "config file  {}\nkubeconfig   {}",
                file.display(),
                kube.display()
            );
        }
        ["nodes"] | ["nodes", "-o", "table" | "json"] => {
            let json = args.last() == Some(&"json");
            let rows = match list_nodes().await {
                Ok(rows) => rows,
                Err(e) => {
                    eprintln!("gpuctl: {e}");

                    return ExitCode::FAILURE;
                }
            };

            if let Err(e) = print_nodes(&rows, json) {
                eprintln!("gpuctl: {e}");

                return ExitCode::FAILURE;
            }
        }
        [] | ["nodes", ..] => {
            eprint!("{USAGE}");

            return ExitCode::from(EXIT_USAGE);
        }
        [other, ..] => {
            eprint!("gpuctl: unknown command \"{other}\"\n{USAGE}");

            return ExitCode::from(EXIT_USAGE);
        }
    }

    ExitCode::SUCCESS
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> ExitCode {
    let args: Vec<String> = env::args().skip(1).collect();

    run(&args).await
}

Summary

  • A command-line tool writes its result to standard output, its messages to standard error, and reports success or the kind of failure in its exit status.
  • A user’s settings live in a different directory on Linux, macOS, and Windows, and paths are joined with the path type of the language, which supplies the separator.
  • The kubeconfig is found by one rule on every system, and a tool that follows it talks to the same cluster as kubectl.
  • A table serves a reader, and JSON with stable field names serves scripts.
  • Go cross-compiles a pure Go program with two environment variables. A Rust program cross-compiles when a C toolchain and linker for the target are present, and projects usually build each target on its own operating system.

References

  • Go, os.UserConfigDir: https://pkg.go.dev/os#UserConfigDir
  • Go, Installing Go from source, the list of GOOS and GOARCH values: https://go.dev/doc/install/source#environment
  • The rustc book, Platform support: https://doc.rust-lang.org/rustc/platform-support.html
  • The Cargo book, cargo build --target: https://doc.rust-lang.org/cargo/commands/cargo-build.html
  • Kubernetes, Organizing cluster access using kubeconfig files: https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/configuration/organize-cluster-access-kubeconfig/
  • XDG Base Directory Specification: https://specifications.freedesktop.org/basedir-spec/latest/
  • cross, zero-setup cross compilation for Rust: https://github.com/cross-rs/cross

Appendix A. A local GPU cluster with kind

Our controller in section 1.1 runs pods that ask for GPUs. In this appendix, we will set up a kind cluster on a Jetson so that those pods really run on the board’s GPU. We will first follow a GPU request all the way from a pod to the hardware, so that we know every piece involved and why it is there. Then we will look at a fresh kind node, see which of those pieces it is missing, and add them one at a time, checking our work after each step. Finally we will run a CUDA program in a pod and watch it use the GPU. All the files are in code/jetson-gpu.

We tested every step on this system:

$ tr -d '\0' < /proc/device-tree/model; echo
NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX Engineering Reference Developer Kit Super
$ head -1 /etc/nv_tegra_release
# R36 (release), REVISION: 4.3, GCID: 38968081, BOARD: generic, EABI: aarch64, DATE: Wed Jan  8 01:49:37 UTC 2025
$ nvidia-ctk --version | head -1
NVIDIA Container Toolkit CLI version 1.17.3
$ kind version
kind v0.33.0 go1.26.7 linux/arm64

Release R36.4.3 of Jetson Linux is part of JetPack 6.2, which ships CUDA 12.6 and the NVIDIA Container Toolkit.

How a program reaches the GPU

The GPU in a Jetson sits on the same chip as the CPU and shares its memory. A program on the board reaches it in the same two ways it would reach any device on Linux.

The first is through device files under /dev. The kernel’s GPU driver creates files such as /dev/nvhost-gpu and /dev/nvmap, and a program talks to the driver by opening these files and making system calls on them.

The second is through the user-space driver libraries: code that runs inside the program and knows how to talk to those device files. The most important one is libcuda.so, the CUDA driver library. When a CUDA program calls a function such as cudaMalloc, the call ends up in libcuda.so, which turns it into operations on the device files. JetPack installs these libraries under /usr/lib/aarch64-linux-gnu/nvidia.

A program running directly on the board has both of these. A program in a container has neither.

Why a container cannot see the GPU

A container’s files come from its image, and its /dev holds only a handful of harmless devices such as /dev/null. The GPU’s device files are not there, and the host’s driver libraries are not in the image. The image cannot simply bring its own copy of the driver libraries, either, because they have to match the kernel driver running on this particular board exactly.

So something has to add the device files and the host’s libraries to each GPU container, and it has to do so at the moment the container is created. To see where that can happen, we need to know how containers are created.

When Kubernetes starts a container, the kubelet (the Kubernetes agent on each machine) asks the machine’s container engine, containerd in our case, to create it. containerd prepares the container’s filesystem and writes a JSON document called the OCI runtime specification, which lists everything about the container: its mounts, its device files, its environment variables, and the command to run. containerd then hands that document to a low-level runtime, usually a program called runc, which creates the container exactly as the document says.

The NVIDIA Container Runtime slots into that hand-off. NVIDIA’s documentation describes it as “a shim for OCI-compliant low-level runtimes such as runc”. In practice, containerd is configured to call nvidia-container-runtime instead of runc. The NVIDIA runtime reads the specification, adds the GPU’s device files and mounts for the driver libraries, and then calls runc with the edited document. The container starts with the GPU already in place.

How the runtime knows what to add

On a data center machine with PCIe GPUs, the runtime asks the driver which GPUs exist and which files each one needs. On a Jetson it reads that information from plain text files instead. NVIDIA’s runtime documentation calls this CSV mode: “CSV files at /etc/nvidia-container-runtime/host-files-for-container.d define the devices and mounts that are to be injected”, and the mode “is primarily targeted at Tegra-based systems without NVML available”. NVML is the management library that data center drivers provide; Tegra is the family name of NVIDIA’s system-on-chip products, including the Orin in our Jetson.

Each line of a CSV file gives a type and a path. Here are the lines for libcuda:

$ grep -h "libcuda" /etc/nvidia-container-runtime/host-files-for-container.d/drivers.csv
lib, /usr/lib/aarch64-linux-gnu/nvidia/libcuda.so.1.1
sym, /usr/lib/aarch64-linux-gnu/libcuda.so
sym, /usr/lib/aarch64-linux-gnu/nvidia/libcuda.so
sym, /usr/lib/aarch64-linux-gnu/nvidia/libcuda.so.1
$ wc -l /etc/nvidia-container-runtime/host-files-for-container.d/*.csv
   40 /etc/nvidia-container-runtime/host-files-for-container.d/devices.csv
  285 /etc/nvidia-container-runtime/host-files-for-container.d/drivers.csv
  325 total

The type tells the runtime what to do with the path:

  • A dev line names a device file to add to the container.
  • A lib line names a file to bind-mount from the host: the same file appears inside the container at the same path, without being copied.
  • A sym line names a symbolic link. The runtime recreates the link inside the container, pointing at the same target as on the host.

The libcuda lines show why the last type exists. The real library is libcuda.so.1.1, and programs look for it under other names, such as libcuda.so.1, which are links to the real file. The runtime mounts the real file once and recreates the links around it.

The lists contain the driver and nothing else. The CUDA toolkit, libcudart and the rest of /usr/local/cuda, is not in them, so a container has to bring its own CUDA runtime library, either in its image or linked into its program. We will do the latter for our test program.

One question remains: how does the runtime know which containers should get a GPU? It looks for an environment variable, NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES. If a container’s specification sets it, the runtime adds the GPU. If not, the runtime passes the specification to runc untouched, and the container starts like any other. The next piece of the puzzle is who sets that variable.

How a pod asks for a GPU

Kubernetes does not know what a GPU is. It knows about CPU and memory, and beyond those it supports extended resources: named quantities, such as nvidia.com/gpu, that a machine advertises and pods request. The scheduler, the component that chooses a machine for each new pod, counts extended resources the same way it counts CPU and memory, without knowing what they are. Extended resources come in whole units only, and a machine never hands out more units than it advertises.

A machine advertises an extended resource through a device plugin. A device plugin is a small program, usually running as a pod on the machine, that speaks a protocol defined by Kubernetes to the kubelet. The conversation goes like this:

  1. The plugin starts a gRPC server on a Unix socket in /var/lib/kubelet/device-plugins. It then connects to the kubelet’s own socket in the same directory, kubelet.sock, and registers: “I serve the resource nvidia.com/gpu, and you can reach me at this socket.”
  2. The kubelet calls the plugin’s ListAndWatch method. The plugin answers with a list of device IDs and their health, and keeps the call open to report changes, such as a GPU becoming unhealthy. The kubelet adds the number of healthy devices to the machine’s advertised capacity, and from that moment the scheduler sees, for example, nvidia.com/gpu: 1 on our node.
  3. When the scheduler places a pod that asks for nvidia.com/gpu: 1 on the machine, the kubelet chooses a free device ID and calls the plugin’s Allocate method with it. The plugin answers with what the container needs in order to use that device: environment variables, device files, or mounts. The kubelet adds them to the container’s configuration before asking containerd to create it.
  4. The kubelet writes every assignment of a device to a container into a checkpoint file, kubelet_internal_checkpoint in the same directory, so that it still knows which devices are in use after it restarts.

NVIDIA’s device plugin can answer Allocate in several ways. Its README lists the options for the setting DEVICE_LIST_STRATEGY and describes the default: “envvar (default): the NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES environment variable … is used to select the devices that are to be injected by the NVIDIA Container Runtime.” That is the missing link: the plugin sets the variable, and the runtime acts on it.

Putting the whole path together:

pod: limits: nvidia.com/gpu: 1
 |
 |  scheduler: places the pod on a machine that advertises a free nvidia.com/gpu
 v
kubelet ---- Allocate(device) ----> NVIDIA device plugin
 |       <--- NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=<device> ---
 v
containerd: writes the OCI specification, with that variable in the environment
 |
 v
nvidia-container-runtime: sees the variable, reads the CSV lists,
 |                        adds the device files and library mounts to the
 |                        specification, and registers hooks that recreate
 |                        the symlinks and refresh the library cache
 v
runc: creates the container

Figure A.1 draws the same path, with the CSV lists that the runtime reads beside it.

For this path to work, containerd must call the NVIDIA runtime for every container, since it cannot know in advance which ones will carry the variable. The plugin’s README says so directly: it requires “nvidia-container-runtime configured as the default low-level runtime” on each GPU machine. It also mentions an alternative, a RuntimeClass that names the nvidia runtime, but then every GPU pod would have to name that RuntimeClass too.

Step 1: create the cluster

Now we can build our cluster. A kind cluster with a single node is enough, and the node image kindest/node:v1.37.0 gives us Kubernetes 1.37:

$ kind create cluster --name gpu-lab --image kindest/node:v1.37.0
Creating cluster "gpu-lab" ...
...
Set kubectl context to "kind-gpu-lab"
$ kubectl get nodes
NAME                    STATUS   ROLES           AGE   VERSION
gpu-lab-control-plane   Ready    control-plane   22s   v1.37.0

kind names the node’s container after the cluster, gpu-lab-control-plane. In the steps below, we use that name with docker exec to run commands inside the node.

What our kind node is missing

A kind node is a Docker container that runs systemd, containerd, and the kubelet, much like a small virtual machine. kind starts it with Docker’s --privileged option, and a privileged container sees all of the host’s device files. So the GPU’s device files are already there:

$ docker exec gpu-lab-control-plane ls -l /dev/nvhost-gpu /dev/nvmap
crw-rw---- 1 root video 487,   1 Sep 26 13:59 /dev/nvhost-gpu
crw-rw---- 1 root video  10, 124 Sep 26 13:59 /dev/nvmap

Everything else on our path is missing. The node’s files come from the kindest/node image, a Debian system that has none of the Jetson’s driver libraries, none of the CSV lists, and no NVIDIA Container Toolkit. Its containerd calls plain runc. And no device plugin runs on it, so it advertises no nvidia.com/gpu, and every pod that asks for one waits forever.

On a data center cluster, NVIDIA’s GPU Operator would install all of these pieces for us. It cannot help here: its platform support page states that “NVIDIA Jetson, or other embedded products with integrated GPUs, are not supported.” So we will install the same pieces by hand.

Step 2: give the node the driver and the runtime

Our script, enable-gpu-in-kind.sh, copies the files the node lacks and then configures containerd:

Listing A.1 Giving the kind node the driver and the runtime. code/jetson-gpu/enable-gpu-in-kind.sh

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Makes the Jetson's integrated GPU usable from pods in a kind cluster.
#
# A kind node is a privileged container, so it already sees the GPU's device
# files. It lacks three things that the host has: the driver libraries listed
# in the Tegra CSV files, the NVIDIA Container Toolkit binaries, and a
# containerd configuration that uses nvidia-container-runtime. This script
# copies the first two into every node and then uses nvidia-ctk, inside the
# node, to configure the runtime as NVIDIA's documentation describes.
#
# Usage: ./enable-gpu-in-kind.sh [cluster-name]
set -euo pipefail

cluster="${1:-gpu-lab}"
csv_dir=/etc/nvidia-container-runtime/host-files-for-container.d
toolkit=(/usr/bin/nvidia-container-runtime /usr/bin/nvidia-container-runtime-hook
         /usr/bin/nvidia-ctk /usr/bin/nvidia-cdi-hook
         /etc/nvidia-container-runtime/config.toml)

# Every file named in the CSV lists that exists on this board. Device nodes
# are left out, because the privileged node already has them.
mapfile -t host_files < <(cut -d, -f2 "$csv_dir"/*.csv | tr -d ' ' | sort -u |
    grep -v '^/dev/' | while read -r f; do [[ -e $f ]] && echo "$f"; done)

for node in $(kind get nodes --name "$cluster"); do
    echo "$node: copying ${#host_files[@]} driver files and the container toolkit"
    # The runtime bind-mounts the CSV "lib" entries and recreates the "sym"
    # entries as links, so symlinks must arrive as symlinks. Paths that are
    # links on the host are removed first, in case an earlier copy left a
    # regular file or directory there.
    for f in "${host_files[@]}"; do [[ -L $f ]] && echo "$f"; done |
        docker exec -i "$node" xargs rm -rf
    tar -cf - --hard-dereference "${host_files[@]}" "$csv_dir" "${toolkit[@]}" 2>/dev/null |
        docker exec -i "$node" tar -xf - -C /

    # The host runs Ubuntu, where the real ldconfig is /sbin/ldconfig.real.
    # The kind node runs Debian, where it is /sbin/ldconfig.
    docker exec "$node" nvidia-ctk config --in-place \
        --set nvidia-container-cli.ldconfig=@/sbin/ldconfig

    if docker exec "$node" grep -q 'default_runtime_name = "nvidia"' /etc/containerd/config.toml; then
        echo "$node: containerd already uses the nvidia runtime by default"
        continue
    fi
    echo "$node: configuring containerd and restarting it"
    docker exec "$node" nvidia-ctk runtime configure --runtime=containerd --set-as-default
    docker exec "$node" systemctl restart containerd
done

The script builds its list of files from the CSV lists themselves, so it copies exactly what the runtime will later try to mount. It skips the dev lines, because the device files are already in the node, and it skips paths that do not exist on this particular board.

Two details of the copy matter, and we ran into both while building this setup. First, sym entries must arrive as symbolic links. Suppose a link were copied as a regular file. The runtime would bind-mount that file into the container, and then its hook would try to replace the mounted file with a link and fail with “device or resource busy”. So the script copies links as links, and before copying it deletes any path that is a link on the host, in case an earlier copy left a regular file or a directory there. Second, some firmware files are hard links, two names for the same file, and --hard-dereference makes tar store each name as a separate file so that both arrive intact.

The host’s configuration file for the runtime, config.toml, travels along with the binaries. One setting in it names the ldconfig program, which the runtime runs inside each GPU container to refresh the list of libraries the container can load. On the host, which runs Ubuntu, that program is /sbin/ldconfig.real. On the kind node, which runs Debian, it is /sbin/ldconfig. The script corrects the path with nvidia-ctk config, the toolkit’s own tool for editing that file.

Finally, the script configures containerd with the command from NVIDIA’s installation guide, adding --set-as-default because the device plugin requires the NVIDIA runtime to be the default. Then it restarts containerd. You might expect that to stop every running pod, but it does not: each container is supervised by its own small shim process, which keeps running while containerd restarts and reconnects to it afterwards. The check at the top of that block skips the whole step when containerd is already configured, so the script is safe to run again.

We run it with the name of our cluster:

$ cd code/jetson-gpu
$ ./enable-gpu-in-kind.sh gpu-lab
gpu-lab-control-plane: copying 283 driver files and the container toolkit
gpu-lab-control-plane: configuring containerd and restarting it
time="2026-09-26T14:00:01Z" level=info msg="Wrote updated config to /etc/containerd/config.toml"
time="2026-09-26T14:00:01Z" level=info msg="It is recommended that containerd daemon be restarted."

Version 1.17.3 of the toolkit edits config.toml directly. Newer versions write a separate file, /etc/containerd/conf.d/99-nvidia.toml, and add it to the imports list in config.toml, as NVIDIA’s installation guide describes. The result is the same.

Let us check our work. A sym entry should be a link inside the node, the NVIDIA runtime should be registered and set as the default, and the node should still be Ready:

$ docker exec gpu-lab-control-plane ls -l /usr/lib/aarch64-linux-gnu/nvidia/libcuda.so
lrwxrwxrwx 1 root root 14 Jan  8  2025 /usr/lib/aarch64-linux-gnu/nvidia/libcuda.so -> libcuda.so.1.1
$ docker exec gpu-lab-control-plane grep -A5 'runtimes.nvidia\]' /etc/containerd/config.toml
        [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri".containerd.runtimes.nvidia]
          base_runtime_spec = "/etc/containerd/cri-base.json"
          runtime_type = "io.containerd.runc.v2"

          [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri".containerd.runtimes.nvidia.options]
            BinaryName = "/usr/bin/nvidia-container-runtime"
$ docker exec gpu-lab-control-plane grep default_runtime_name /etc/containerd/config.toml
      default_runtime_name = "nvidia"
$ kubectl get nodes
NAME                    STATUS   ROLES           AGE   VERSION
gpu-lab-control-plane   Ready    control-plane   30s   v1.37.0

Step 3: run the device plugin

The device plugin runs as a DaemonSet, a Kubernetes workload that runs one pod on every node, which is exactly what a per-machine agent needs:

Listing A.2 The device plugin. code/jetson-gpu/device-plugin.yaml

# NVIDIA device plugin configured for a Jetson. The integrated GPU is found
# from the Tegra CSV file lists under the driver root. For each pod that
# requests nvidia.com/gpu, the plugin sets NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES, and the
# node's nvidia-container-runtime mounts the devices and libraries.
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: DaemonSet
metadata:
  name: nvidia-device-plugin
  namespace: kube-system
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: nvidia-device-plugin
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: nvidia-device-plugin
    spec:
      priorityClassName: system-node-critical
      tolerations:
        - key: nvidia.com/gpu
          operator: Exists
          effect: NoSchedule
      containers:
        - name: plugin
          image: nvcr.io/nvidia/k8s-device-plugin:v0.20.1
          env:
            - name: DEVICE_DISCOVERY_STRATEGY
              value: tegra
            - name: NVIDIA_DRIVER_ROOT      # driver root as the node sees it
              value: /
            - name: DRIVER_ROOT_CTR_PATH    # the same root inside this container
              value: /driver-root
            - name: FAIL_ON_INIT_ERROR
              value: "true"
          volumeMounts:
            - name: device-plugins
              mountPath: /var/lib/kubelet/device-plugins
            - name: driver-root
              mountPath: /driver-root
              readOnly: true
      volumes:
        - name: device-plugins
          hostPath:
            path: /var/lib/kubelet/device-plugins
        - name: driver-root
          hostPath:
            path: /

The plugin needs to read the same CSV lists and driver files as the runtime, so it mounts the node’s whole root filesystem, read-only, at /driver-root inside its own container. The two driver-root settings tell it where to find the driver from both points of view: NVIDIA_DRIVER_ROOT is where the driver is on the node, and DRIVER_ROOT_CTR_PATH is where that same root appears inside the plugin’s container.

DEVICE_DISCOVERY_STRATEGY=tegra tells the plugin to discover the GPU from the CSV lists. This setting is not in the plugin’s README; it is defined among the plugin’s command-line flags in its source code (cmd/nvidia-device-plugin/main.go), which accepts auto, nvml, or tegra. The default, auto, inspects the system and would most likely choose the same, but we prefer to be explicit. We leave DEVICE_LIST_STRATEGY at its default, envvar, the mechanism we traced above.

The plugin also offers newer strategies based on CDI, the Container Device Interface, a standard file format that describes devices to container engines. They do not work on this board: with DEVICE_LIST_STRATEGY=cdi-cri, version 0.20.1 stops at startup with CDI --device-list-strategy options are only supported on NVML-based systems.

We apply the DaemonSet and read the plugin’s log:

$ kubectl apply -f device-plugin.yaml
$ kubectl -n kube-system rollout status ds/nvidia-device-plugin
daemon set "nvidia-device-plugin" successfully rolled out
$ kubectl -n kube-system logs ds/nvidia-device-plugin | tail -6
I0926 14:00:19.469889  1 main.go:391] Retrieving plugins.
I0926 14:00:19.469935  1 plugin-manager.go:96] Using requested device discovery strategy: tegra
I0926 14:00:19.469986  1 plugin-manager.go:50] Using device discovery strategy: tegra
I0926 14:00:19.470717  1 server.go:198] Starting GRPC server for 'nvidia.com/gpu'
I0926 14:00:19.472172  1 server.go:142] Starting to serve 'nvidia.com/gpu' on /var/lib/kubelet/device-plugins/nvidia-gpu.sock
I0926 14:00:19.477135  1 server.go:149] Registered device plugin for 'nvidia.com/gpu' with Kubelet
$ kubectl get node -o jsonpath='{.items[0].status.allocatable.nvidia\.com/gpu}'; echo
1

The last three log lines are the first step of the conversation we described earlier: the plugin starts its gRPC server, listens on nvidia-gpu.sock, and registers with the kubelet. The node now advertises one GPU. If you check within a few seconds of the plugin starting, you may still see 0, because the kubelet updates the node’s status shortly after the plugin registers.

Step 4: run a CUDA program in a pod

To prove the whole path works, we need a program that uses the GPU and tells us so. Ours adds two vectors of a million elements on the GPU and prints the device it ran on. If no GPU is visible, or the result is wrong, it exits with a non-zero status, so Kubernetes marks the pod as failed.

Listing A.3 The kernel: one addition per thread. code/jetson-gpu/gpu_check.cu

// add writes a[i] + b[i] to out[i]. Each thread handles one element, and
// threads past the end of the vectors do nothing.
__global__ void add(const float *a, const float *b, float *out, int n) {
    int i = blockIdx.x * blockDim.x + threadIdx.x;
    if (i < n) {
        out[i] = a[i] + b[i];
    }
}

Listing A.4 Allocating, launching, and checking the result. code/jetson-gpu/gpu_check.cu

// main prints the device, runs the addition, and prints the kernel's status
// with the first and last results. It exits with status 1 if no GPU is
// visible, the kernel failed, or the result is wrong.
int main() {
    cudaDeviceProp prop;
    if (cudaGetDeviceProperties(&prop, 0) != cudaSuccess) {
        std::printf("no CUDA device visible\n");
        return 1;
    }
    std::printf("device: %s, compute %d.%d, %d SMs\n", prop.name, prop.major, prop.minor,
                prop.multiProcessorCount);

    float *a, *b, *out;
    cudaMallocManaged(&a, kElements * sizeof(float));
    cudaMallocManaged(&b, kElements * sizeof(float));
    cudaMallocManaged(&out, kElements * sizeof(float));
    for (int i = 0; i < kElements; i++) {
        a[i] = 1.0f;
        b[i] = 2.0f;
    }

    add<<<(kElements + kThreads - 1) / kThreads, kThreads>>>(a, b, out, kElements);
    cudaError_t err = cudaDeviceSynchronize();
    std::printf("kernel: %s, out[0] = %.1f, out[n-1] = %.1f\n", cudaGetErrorString(err),
                out[0], out[kElements - 1]);

    bool ok = err == cudaSuccess && out[kElements - 1] == 3.0f;
    cudaFree(a);
    cudaFree(b);
    cudaFree(out);
    return ok ? 0 : 1;
}

We build it on the board with the CUDA compiler from JetPack. -arch=sm_87 targets the Orin’s GPU, whose compute capability, NVIDIA’s version number for a GPU’s feature set, is 8.7. -cudart static links the CUDA runtime library into the program itself, so our image does not need to contain it. The only library the program needs at run time is the driver library, libcuda, and that is one of the files the NVIDIA runtime mounts.

$ /usr/local/cuda/bin/nvcc -O2 -arch=sm_87 -cudart static -o gpu_check gpu_check.cu
$ ./gpu_check
device: Orin, compute 8.7, 8 SMs
kernel: no error, out[0] = 3.0, out[n-1] = 3.0

The image only has to hold the program and the C++ standard library that Ubuntu provides:

Listing A.5 The image for the test program. code/jetson-gpu/Dockerfile

FROM ubuntu:22.04
COPY gpu_check /usr/local/bin/gpu_check
CMD ["gpu_check"]

A kind node has its own image store and cannot see the images in the host’s Docker, so we build the image and then copy it into the node:

$ docker build -t gpu-check:1 .
$ kind load docker-image gpu-check:1 --name gpu-lab

We run the image in two pods. The first asks for a GPU and the second does not, so the second one tells us whether containers that did not ask for a GPU really stay without one.

Listing A.6 A pod that runs `gpu_check` with one GPU. code/jetson-gpu/gpu-check-pod.yaml

apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: gpu-check
spec:
  restartPolicy: Never
  containers:
    - name: check
      image: gpu-check:1
      imagePullPolicy: Never
      resources:
        limits:
          nvidia.com/gpu: 1

Listing A.7 The same program in a pod that asks for no GPU. code/jetson-gpu/no-gpu-pod.yaml

apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: no-gpu
spec:
  restartPolicy: Never
  containers:
    - name: check
      image: gpu-check:1
      imagePullPolicy: Never

imagePullPolicy: Never tells the kubelet to use the image that kind load placed on the node and not to look for it in a registry.

$ kubectl apply -f gpu-check-pod.yaml -f no-gpu-pod.yaml
$ kubectl get pods gpu-check no-gpu
NAME        READY   STATUS      RESTARTS   AGE
gpu-check   0/1     Completed   0          15s
no-gpu      0/1     Error       0          15s
$ kubectl logs gpu-check
device: Orin, compute 8.7, 8 SMs
kernel: no error, out[0] = 3.0, out[n-1] = 3.0
$ kubectl logs no-gpu
no CUDA device visible
$ kubectl delete -f gpu-check-pod.yaml -f no-gpu-pod.yaml

The first pod ran our kernel on the GPU. The second ran the same image, through the same default runtime, and saw no GPU, because the device plugin was never asked to allocate one for it and so never set NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES.

We can also look inside a GPU container while it runs, to see every piece of the path at work. This pod asks for a GPU and then just sleeps for five minutes, which gives us time to look around inside it:

Listing A.8 A pod that sets the variable itself. code/jetson-gpu/gpu-env-pod.yaml

apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: gpu-env
spec:
  restartPolicy: Never
  containers:
    - name: check
      image: gpu-check:1
      imagePullPolicy: Never
      command: ["sleep", "300"]
      resources:
        limits:
          nvidia.com/gpu: 1

We start it, wait until it is running, and look at its environment, its device files, and its driver libraries:

$ kubectl apply -f gpu-env-pod.yaml
$ kubectl wait --for=condition=Ready pod/gpu-env
$ kubectl exec gpu-env -- sh -c 'env | grep NVIDIA; ls /dev | grep -E "nvhost-gpu|nvmap"'
NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=tegra
nvhost-gpu
nvmap
$ kubectl exec gpu-env -- sh -c 'ls /usr/lib/aarch64-linux-gnu/nvidia/libcuda.so*'
/usr/lib/aarch64-linux-gnu/nvidia/libcuda.so
/usr/lib/aarch64-linux-gnu/nvidia/libcuda.so.1
/usr/lib/aarch64-linux-gnu/nvidia/libcuda.so.1.1

The device plugin advertises our single GPU under the device ID tegra, and its answer to Allocate set NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=tegra in the container. The NVIDIA runtime saw the variable and added the device files, the real library libcuda.so.1.1, and the two links to it. The kubelet’s checkpoint file on the node shows the same allocation from the kubelet’s side:

$ docker exec gpu-lab-control-plane cat /var/lib/kubelet/device-plugins/kubelet_internal_checkpoint
{"Data":{"PodDeviceEntries":[{"PodUID":"bf52b2cd-b611-4e79-86b1-280b283185c3","ContainerName":"check","ResourceName":"nvidia.com/gpu","DeviceIDs":{"-1":["tegra"]},"AllocResp":"Ch8KFk5WSURJQV9WSVNJQkxFX0RFVklDRVMSBXRlZ3Jh"}],"RegisteredDevices":{"nvidia.com/gpu":["tegra"]}},"Checksum":3660592824}

RegisteredDevices is the device list the plugin reported through ListAndWatch, and the entry under PodDeviceEntries records that the container check of our pod holds the device tegra. AllocResp is the plugin’s answer to Allocate, stored in its binary Protocol Buffers encoding; decoded, it contains the environment variable NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=tegra. When we are done looking, we delete the pod so that it releases the GPU:

$ kubectl delete -f gpu-env-pod.yaml

Because the plugin advertises the board’s one GPU as one unit, only one pod that asks for a GPU can run at a time. Any other GPU pod waits in Pending until the first one finishes, and section 1.1 shows a job in exactly that state.

When a step fails

We ran into each of these problems while building this setup.

The plugin exits with CDI --device-list-strategy options are only supported on NVML-based systems. DEVICE_LIST_STRATEGY is set to one of the CDI strategies. Remove the setting, so that the plugin uses envvar.

A GPU pod fails with failed to create symlink ... device or resource busy. A sym entry reached the node as a regular file, so the runtime mounted it and its hook could not replace it with a link. Run enable-gpu-in-kind.sh again; it removes such paths before copying.

A pod that asked for a GPU prints no CUDA device visible. The container was not created through the NVIDIA runtime. Check default_runtime_name in the node’s containerd configuration, and make sure containerd was restarted after the change.

A GPU pod stays Pending with Insufficient nvidia.com/gpu. Either the plugin has not registered, which its log and the node’s allocatable resources will show, or another pod is holding the GPU.

GPU pods fail after the cluster was deleted and created again. A new node starts from the kindest/node image, without any of our changes. Run the script and apply the DaemonSet again. Restarting the node’s container, or Docker itself, keeps the changes, since they live in the container’s filesystem.

The complete test program

Listing A.9 `gpu_check`, complete. code/jetson-gpu/gpu_check.cu

// gpu_check proves that a container can use the GPU: it prints the device it
// sees, adds two vectors of a million elements on the GPU, and checks the
// result.
//
// How it runs, end to end:
//   1. nvcc compiles this file on the host for the Orin's GPU (-arch=sm_87) and
//      links the CUDA runtime library statically (-cudart static), so the
//      binary needs only the CUDA driver library, libcuda, at run time.
//   2. The binary is copied into the gpu-check:1 image and run in a pod.
//   3. If the pod requested nvidia.com/gpu, the device plugin sets
//      NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES and the NVIDIA container runtime mounts the GPU's
//      device files and libcuda into the container. If it did not, the
//      container has neither, and the first CUDA call fails.
//   4. The vectors live in managed memory, which the CPU and the GPU can both
//      address, so the program fills them on the CPU, adds them on the GPU,
//      and reads the result on the CPU without explicit copies.
//
// Build: nvcc -O2 -arch=sm_87 -cudart static -o gpu_check gpu_check.cu
#include <cstdio>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>

// kElements is the length of each vector.
constexpr int kElements = 1 << 20;

// kThreads is the number of threads in each block of the add kernel.
constexpr int kThreads = 256;

// add writes a[i] + b[i] to out[i]. Each thread handles one element, and
// threads past the end of the vectors do nothing.
__global__ void add(const float *a, const float *b, float *out, int n) {
    int i = blockIdx.x * blockDim.x + threadIdx.x;
    if (i < n) {
        out[i] = a[i] + b[i];
    }
}

// main prints the device, runs the addition, and prints the kernel's status
// with the first and last results. It exits with status 1 if no GPU is
// visible, the kernel failed, or the result is wrong.
int main() {
    cudaDeviceProp prop;
    if (cudaGetDeviceProperties(&prop, 0) != cudaSuccess) {
        std::printf("no CUDA device visible\n");
        return 1;
    }
    std::printf("device: %s, compute %d.%d, %d SMs\n", prop.name, prop.major, prop.minor,
                prop.multiProcessorCount);

    float *a, *b, *out;
    cudaMallocManaged(&a, kElements * sizeof(float));
    cudaMallocManaged(&b, kElements * sizeof(float));
    cudaMallocManaged(&out, kElements * sizeof(float));
    for (int i = 0; i < kElements; i++) {
        a[i] = 1.0f;
        b[i] = 2.0f;
    }

    add<<<(kElements + kThreads - 1) / kThreads, kThreads>>>(a, b, out, kElements);
    cudaError_t err = cudaDeviceSynchronize();
    std::printf("kernel: %s, out[0] = %.1f, out[n-1] = %.1f\n", cudaGetErrorString(err),
                out[0], out[kElements - 1]);

    bool ok = err == cudaSuccess && out[kElements - 1] == 3.0f;
    cudaFree(a);
    cudaFree(b);
    cudaFree(out);
    return ok ? 0 : 1;
}
